A few years, however, will settle her opinions on the reasonable foundation of common sense and observation; thereafter, they may be easier to define and justify than they currently are, by anyone but herself."

"This will likely occur," he responded, "and yet there is something so amiable in the prejudices of a young mind that one regrets seeing them yield to the reception of more generalized opinions."

"I cannot concur with you there," said Elinor. "There are inconveniences attendant upon such feelings as Marianne's that all the charms of enthusiasm and ignorance of the world cannot atone for. Her systems possess the unfortunate tendency of setting propriety at naught; and a deeper acquaintance with the world is what I anticipate as her greatest possible advantage."

After a brief pause, he resumed the discussion: "Does your sister make no distinction in her objections against a second attachment? Or is it equally criminal in everyone? Are those who have been disappointed in their first choice, whether due to the inconstancy of its object or the perverseness of circumstances, to remain equally indifferent for the remainder of their lives?"

"Upon my word, I am unfamiliar with the minutiae of her principles. I only know that I have never yet heard her admit any instance of a second attachment being pardonable."

"This," he stated, "cannot hold; but a change—a total transformation of sentiments—No, no, do not desire it; for when the romantic refinements of a young mind are compelled to yield, how frequently are they succeeded by opinions that are but too common and too dangerous! I speak from experience. I once knew a lady whose temperament and mind greatly resembled your sister, who thought and judged alike, but who, from an enforced change—from a series of unfortunate circumstances—"

He stopped abruptly, appearing to realize he had spoken too freely, and by his countenance aroused conjectures that might not otherwise have entered Elinor's thoughts. Had the lady passed without suspicion, had he not convinced Miss Dashwood that matters concerning her ought not to escape his lips. As it was, it required but a slight effort of imagination to link his emotion with the tender recollection of past regard. Elinor attempted no further inquiry. But Marianne, in her position, would not have restrained herself so little. The entire narrative would have been swiftly constructed under her active imagination, with every detail established in the most melancholy order of disastrous love.

The morning sun cast a deceptive, golden glow over the Downs, masking the chill that lingered in the air. As Elinor and Marianne walked together, the younger sister delivered a piece of news that struck Elinor with the force of a physical blow. Despite her prior knowledge of Marianne's impulsivity and lack of foresight, the extravagance of the revelation shocked her nonetheless. With a radiant expression, Marianne announced that Willoughby had gifted her a horse—a stallion he had personally bred on his Somersetshire estate, perfectly suited to carry a woman.

Marianne spoke of the acquisition with unbridled delight, failing to register the logistical catastrophe she had inadvertently signed away. She ignored the fact that her mother had no intention of keeping a horse, nor did she consider the necessity of purchasing a second mount for the servant, hiring a rider, and constructing a stable to house them. Instead, she had accepted the gift without hesitation, recounting the event to her sister in rapturous tones. "He intends to dispatch his groom to Somersetshire immediately," she added, her eyes shining with anticipation. "Upon its arrival, we shall ride daily. You must share its use with me. Imagine, my dear Elinor, the sheer ecstasy of a gallop across these downs."

Elinor found herself violently resisting the urge to snap out of this fever dream of felicity and confront the harsh realities underpinning the transaction. The financial burden was negligible; her mother would surely approve of the addition to the household. Any horse would suffice for *him*; he could procure one at the park whenever needed. As for a stable, a mere shed would serve adequately. Yet, Elinor dared to question the propriety of accepting such a substantial gift from a man she barely knew.

"You are mistaken, Elinor," Marianne retorted, her voice warming with defensive passion. "In supposing I know Willoughby little. While I have not known him long, my acquaintance with him surpasses that of any other living soul, save yourself and Mother. Intimacy is not measured by time or opportunity, but solely by disposition. Seven years may fail to acquaint two individuals, whereas seven days can forge a bond deeper than a lifetime. I would deem it far greater impropriety to accept a horse from my brother than from Willoughby. Of John, I know little, despite our years of cohabitation; yet my judgment regarding Willoughby has been formed long ago."

Recognizing the futility of opposition on such a tender subject, Elinor wisely chose to withdraw. She knew her sister's temperament; resistance would only bind Marianne tighter to her erroneous convictions. However, Elinor attempted to subdue Marianne by appealing to her maternal affection, outlining the inevitable inconveniences and burdens this expansion of the establishment would impose upon their indulgent mother. Slowly, Marianne yielded. She promised not to tempt her mother into imprudent generosity by mentioning the offer and vowed to inform Willoughby upon their next meeting that the gift must be declined.

True to her word, when Willoughby visited the cottage later that day, Elinor heard Marianne express her regret in a low, disappointed voice, lamenting her inability to accept his present. She relayed the reasons for her change of heart, rendering further persuasion on his part futile. Nevertheless, his distress was palpable. After voicing his concern earnestly, he added, also in a hushed tone, "Yet, Marianne, the horse remains yours, though you cannot utilize it at this juncture. I shall retain it only until you are able to claim it. When you depart Barton to establish your own permanent home, Queen Mab shall await your arrival."

Miss Dashwood, overhearing the entire exchange, noted the specific cadence of his speech and the intimate use of her sister's Christian name alone. Instantly, she perceived a level of intimacy so profound, a meaning so explicit, that it signaled a perfect accord between them. From that moment, Elinor harbored no doubt that they were engaged; the only surprise was that such frank natures would allow their union to remain undiscovered by accident.

Later, Margaret provided corroborating evidence that illuminated the situation further. Willoughby had spent the previous evening with the family, and Margaret, having been left alone in the parlor with only the two of them, had observed details she deemed crucial. With a grave demeanor, she confided in her eldest sister. "Oh, Elinor!" she exclaimed. "I possess a secret concerning Marianne. I am certain she will marry Mr. Willoughby very soon."

"You have asserted this," Elinor replied dryly, "almost daily since their first encounter on High-church Down. We had not known each other a week before you were convinced Marianne wore his portrait around her neck; yet it transpired to be merely the miniature of our great-uncle."

"But this is entirely distinct," Margaret insisted. "I am positive they will wed soon, for he possesses a lock of her hair."

"Exercise caution, Margaret. It may belong to some great-uncle of *his*."

"It is undoubtedly Marianne's," Margaret countered. "I am nearly certain, for I witnessed him sever it. Last night, after tea, when you and Mother exited the room, they whispered furiously together. He appeared to beg something of her, then seized her scissors. Her hair cascaded down her back, and he snipped a long lock. He kissed it, folded it within a sheet of white paper, and deposited it into his pocket-book."

Given the specificity of these particulars and the authority of the source, Elinor could not withhold her credence, nor was she inclined to do so, as the circumstances aligned perfectly with her own observations. Margaret's sagacity, however, often manifested in ways unsatisfactory to her sister. One evening at the Park, when Mrs. Jennings aggressively demanded the name of the young man who was Elinor's particular favorite—a query that had long plagued her curiosity—Margaret responded by glancing at her sister and asking, "Must I not tell, may I, Elinor?"

This response naturally elicited laughter from everyone present, including Elinor, though the effort to smile was painfully strained. She suspected Margaret had selected a suitor whose name she could not utter with composure, thereby ensuring it became a perpetual jest among Mrs. Jennings' circle. Marianne sympathized with her sister's plight but ultimately exacerbated the situation by flushing crimson and angrily admonishing Margaret, "Recall that regardless of your conjectures, you possess no right to disseminate them."

"I never entertained any conjectures," Margaret retorted. "It was you who revealed it to me yourself."

This only intensified the merriment of the assembly, and Margaret was eagerly prodded to elaborate. "Oh! Pray, Miss Margaret, enlighten us completely," urged Mrs. Jennings. "What is the gentleman's name?"

"I must not reveal it, ma'am. Yet I know precisely who he is, and I know his location as well."

"Yes, yes, we can deduce his whereabouts; surely at his own residence at Norland. He is the parish curate, I presume."

"No, *that* he is not. He holds no profession whatsoever."

"Margaret," Marianne interjected with considerable heat, "you know this is a fabrication of your own imagination, and that no such individual exists."

"Well, then, he is recently deceased, Marianne, for I am convinced such a man once existed, and his name commences with an 'F.'"

At this critical juncture, Elinor felt immense gratitude toward Lady Middleton for interjecting, "It is raining exceedingly heavily." Although she suspected the interruption stemmed less from genuine concern for her than from her ladyship's aversion to the inelegant topics of ridicule that delighted her husband and mother, the diversion proved timely. Colonel Brandon, ever mindful of the sensitivities of others, immediately seized upon the subject of precipitation, and much discourse ensued on the matter.

Willoughby rose, opening the piano-forte and inviting Marianne to sit. Thus, amidst the concerted efforts of various guests to bury the topic, it was successfully laid to rest. Yet, Elinor found herself unable to recover so readily from the alarm into which the conversation had plunged her.

That evening, plans were formulated for a visit the following day to a magnificent estate located approximately twelve miles from Barton, owned by a brother-in-law of Colonel Brandon. Access to the property required his intervention, as the proprietor, currently abroad, had issued strict prohibitions against unauthorized entry. The grounds were lauded as exceptionally beautiful, with Sir John serving as a particularly ardent advocate; having organized excursions to the site at least twice annually for the past decade, he qualified as a discerning judge. The itinerary included a noble expanse of water, where sailing would constitute the primary amusement. Cold provisions were to be carried, and travel arranged via open carriages, adhering strictly to the conventional style of a complete party of pleasure.

For several members of the company, the undertaking appeared audacious, given the season and the fact that rain had persisted daily for the preceding fortnight. Consequently, Mrs. Dashwood, who was already suffering from a cold, was persuaded by Elinor to remain at home.

The excursion to Whitwell, meticulously planned by Elinor, dissolved into absolute nullity before it ever commenced. Her preparations had accounted for the worst: drenching rains, crushing fatigue, and the gnawing terror of exposure. Yet, the outcome proved far more calamitous than any storm could have been—they did not move at all.

By ten o'clock, the entire assembly gathered at the park gates, ready for breakfast. The morning held a deceptive promise; though the night had poured its weight upon the earth, the clouds were fracturing, allowing intermittent shafts of sunlight to pierce the grey canopy. Spirits ran high, fueled by a collective determination to endure whatever inconvenience fate might throw their way, provided they remained united.

Breakfast proceeded until the arrival of correspondence shattered the mood. A letter addressed to Colonel Brandon arrived among the pile. He seized it, scanned the handwriting, and his complexion drained of color. With sudden, jerky movements, he rose and exited the room.

"What is the matter with Brandon?" Sir John demanded, his voice cutting through the chatter.

"Nothing," Elinor whispered, though her heart hammered against her ribs. "I trust he has received no bad tidings."

"It must be extraordinary," Lady Middleton interjected, her brow furrowing. "To abandon my breakfast table so abruptly suggests a crisis of the highest order."

Five minutes elapsed before Brandon reappeared, his demeanor rigid.

"No bad news, I hope," Mrs. Jennings called out as he entered.

"None at all, ma'am," he replied, his voice tight.

"Was it from Avignon?" Mrs. Jennings pressed, her curiosity overriding her decorum. "Does it concern your sister's health?"

"No, ma'am. It originated in town. Merely a letter of business."

"But how could a routine commercial missive disturb you so profoundly?" Mrs. Jennings persisted, ignoring the sharp rebuke from Lady Middleton. "Speak plainly, Colonel. Let us have the truth."

"My dear madam," Lady Middleton snapped, "remember your station."

"Perhaps it announces that your cousin Fanny is wedded?" Mrs. Jennings guessed, oblivious to the correction.

"Not at all."

"Well, then, I deduce the sender. And I pray she fares well."

"Who do you imply, ma'am?" Brandon asked, flushing slightly.

"Oh! You know precisely who I mean."

"I am deeply sorry, ma'am," Brandon addressed Lady Middleton, "to receive this dispatch today. It concerns business requiring my immediate presence in town."

"In town!" Mrs. Jennings exclaimed. "At this season? What necessitates such haste?"

"My personal loss is significant," Brandon continued, his tone grave, "in forfeiting the company of such an agreeable gathering. However, my absence is imperative to secure your entry to Whitwell."

A collective gasp rippled through the room. This was a blow of devastating proportions.

"If you draft a note to the housekeeper, Mr. Brandon," Marianne suggested eagerly, "would that not suffice?"

Brandon shook his head firmly.

"We must proceed," Sir John declared. "Delay is not an option when we stand on the threshold. You cannot depart for town until tomorrow, Brandon—that is final."

"I wish it were so simple," Brandon replied, his voice devoid of inflection. "Yet, I possess no authority to postpone my journey by a single hour."

"If you would merely disclose the nature of your errand," Mrs. Jennings urged, "we might determine if it could be deferred."

"You would not be delayed six hours," Willoughby interjected smoothly, "were you to await our return."

"I cannot afford to lose *one* hour," Brandon stated flatly.

Elinor caught Willoughby leaning toward Marianne, his voice dropping to a conspiratorial whisper. "Some souls simply cannot tolerate a party atmosphere. Brandon fears catching a chill, I suspect. He has concocted this elaborate ruse to escape. I would wager fifty guineas the letter bears his own signature."

"I harbor no doubts," Marianne retorted instantly.

"There is no convincing you to alter your course, Brandon, as I have known you since childhood," Sir John observed, his tone laced with resignation. "Nevertheless, I trust you will reconsider. Contemplate this: the two Miss Careys have traveled from Newton; the three Miss Dashwoods have trekked from the cottage; and Mr. Willoughby rose two hours prior to his custom to join us at Whitwell."

Colonel Brandon reiterated his profound sorrow at disappointing the group, yet maintained that his departure was unavoidable.

"Very well," Sir John conceded. "When shall you return?"

"I hope we shall meet you at Barton," Lady Jennings added warmly, "the moment you can conveniently leave town. Consequently, we shall postpone the Whitwell excursion until your return."

"You are exceedingly obliging. Yet, given the uncertainty of my return date, I dare not commit to any specific timeline."

"Oh! He must and shall return," Sir John insisted loudly. "Should he not appear by the end of the week, I shall ride after him myself."

"Aye, do so, Sir John," Mrs. Jennings chimed in. "Perhaps you will finally uncover the mystery of his business."

"I have no desire to pry into another man's affairs," Brandon replied coolly. "I presume it involves some subject of shame."

His horses were announced.

"Do you intend to travel to town on horseback?" Sir John queried.

"No. Only to Honiton. From there, I shall take the post."

"As you are resolved," Sir John said, rising, "I wish you a safe journey. Though I advise you to change your mind."

"I assure you, it lies entirely outside my power."

With that, he took his leave of the assembly.

"Is there no possibility of seeing you and your sisters in town this winter, Miss Dashwood?" he asked politely.

"I fear, none whatsoever."

"Then I must bid you farewell for a duration longer than I would prefer."

To Marianne, he offered only a curt bow and silence.

"Come, Colonel," Mrs. Jennings pleaded as he moved toward the door. "Before you depart, do reveal the nature of your undertaking."

He exchanged a polite morning greeting with her and, accompanied by Sir John, withdrew.

Only then did the dam break. The polite restraint previously holding back their grievances evaporated. Complaints and lamentations erupted universally, a chorus of frustration echoing through the hall. They unanimously agreed that the disappointment was excruciating.

"I can surmise his business," Mrs. Jennings declared triumphantly.

"And who is it, ma'am?" nearly everyone asked in unison.

"Yes! It concerns Miss Williams, surely."

"And who is Miss Williams?" Marianne asked, genuinely perplexed.

"What! Do you not know Miss Williams? I am certain you have heard of her. She is a relative of the Colonel's, my dear; a very close kin. We shall not specify the degree, lest we shock the young ladies." Lowering her voice to a theatrical whisper, she turned to Elinor. "She is his natural daughter."

"Indeed!"

"Oh, yes! And she resembles him in every feature, save for her ability to stare. I daresay the Colonel will leave her his entire fortune."

When Sir John returned, he joined the chorus of regret regarding the thwarted plans, though he concluded by insisting that, since they were all assembled, they must manufacture some form of joy. After brief deliberation, it was decided that while true happiness awaited them at Whitwell, they might achieve a tolerable composure of mind by touring the countryside. Carriages were dispatched; Willoughby's led the procession, and Marianne appeared more radiant than she had ever been as she settled within.

Willoughby drove swiftly through the park, and they vanished from sight quickly. No further communication occurred until their return, which happened only after the rest of the party had resumed their activities. Both seemed utterly delighted with their excursion, offering only vague remarks that they had ventured through the lanes while the others traversed the downs.

Evening promised a dance, and the day was dedicated to merriment. Additional members of the Carey family joined for dinner, swelling the table count to nearly twenty, a spectacle Sir John observed with evident satisfaction. Willoughby claimed his customary seat between the two elder Miss Dashwoods. Mrs. Jennings took her place to Elinor's right. Hardly had they taken their seats before she leaned forward, addressing Marianne with a volume audible to both women.

"I have uncovered your secret, despite all your stratagems. I know exactly where you spent the morning."

Marianne flushed crimson and replied hurriedly, "Where, pray?"

"Did you not know," Willoughby added smoothly, "that we had driven out in my curricle?"

"Yes, yes, Mr. Impudence, I am fully aware of that fact," Marianne shot back. "I was determined to ascertain *where* you had gone. I trust you enjoy your residence, Miss Marianne. It is a spacious property, I understand; and when I next visit, I hope you have furnished it anew, for it required it desperately during my stay six years ago."

Marianne turned away, overwhelmed by confusion. Mrs. Jennings laughed heartily. Meanwhile, Elinor realized that in her fervent quest to discover the location, she had inadvertently prompted her own maid to inquire with Mr. Willoughby's groom. Through this indirect channel, she learned they had visited Allenham, spending considerable time wandering the gardens and inspecting the interior of the house.

Elinor

The abrupt cessation of Colonel Brandon's visits to the Park, coupled with his steely resolve to conceal the motive behind his departure, occupied Mrs. Jennings' thoughts for three consecutive days. She was a creature of perpetual speculation, finding the shifting comings and goings of her acquaintances a fertile ground for endless conjecture. With barely a moment's respite, she interrogated the void left by Brandon's absence. Was it bad news? Had some calamity befallen him? She catalogued every conceivable distress, determined that he would not escape her exhaustive list of misfortunes.

"Something profoundly melancholy must be the matter," she declared, her voice heavy with certainty. "I could read it in his face. Poor man! I fear his circumstances have deteriorated. The Delaford estate was never valued above two thousand pounds a year, and his brother left affairs hopelessly entangled. I am convinced he was summoned regarding financial exigencies; surely nothing else could explain it?"

She paused, allowing the hypothesis to settle, before pivoting to another theory. "Or perhaps it concerns Miss Williams. By the way, I suspect it does, simply because he appeared so conscious when I mentioned her name. Maybe she is ill in town; nothing is more plausible, for I have long held the notion that she is constitutionally frail. I would stake my reputation on it being about Miss Williams. Yet, it is unlikely he is currently distressed financially—he is a man of prudence, and surely he has cleared the estate by now. Could it be his sister is worse at Avignon and has recalled him? His precipitous departure suggests exactly that."

Mrs. Jennings wished him relief from all his troubles and a suitable wife into the bargain, her mind oscillating between these scenarios with equal fervor.

Elinor, however, possessed a different calculus. While genuinely concerned for Colonel Brandon's welfare, she found the duration and intensity of her mother's amazement unjustified. In her estimation, the circumstances did not warrant such sustained speculation. Furthermore, her own wonder was consumed by a far more pressing enigma: the extraordinary silence maintained by her sister and Mr. Willoughby regarding their relationship. This silence grew increasingly bizarre and incompatible with their known dispositions as the days passed. Why did they refuse to acknowledge openly to her mother and herself what their consistent behavior toward one another clearly indicated?

Elinor could rationalize that marriage might not be immediately feasible; though Willoughby was independent, his fortune was modest, rated by Sir John at six or seven hundred pounds annually. He lived beyond his means, frequently lamenting his poverty. Yet, this strategic secrecy, which effectively concealed nothing, remained inexplicable. It contradicted their general principles so thoroughly that a nagging doubt occasionally surfaced in Elinor's mind concerning the reality of their engagement—a doubt sufficient to halt any direct inquiry to Marianne.

Nothing, however, expressed their mutual devotion more eloquently than Willoughby's conduct. To Marianne, he offered the distinct tenderness of a lover's heart; to the wider family, he displayed the affectionate attentiveness of a devoted son and brother. The cottage at Barton seemed to constitute his true home; he spent more hours there than at Allenham. Even when no formal engagement drew them to the Park, his morning exercises invariably concluded there, where he would spend the remainder of the day beside Marianne, with his favorite pointer resting at her feet.

One evening, approximately a week after Colonel Brandon's departure, Willoughby's heart appeared unusually open to the sentiments surrounding him. When Mrs. Dashwood casually mentioned her intention to improve the cottage in the spring, he vigorously opposed any alterations to a place he deemed perfect.

"What!" he exclaimed. "Improve this dear cottage! No. *That* I will never consent to. Not a stone must be added to its walls, nor an inch to its size, if my feelings are to be respected."

"Do not be alarmed," Miss Dashwood reassured him gently. "Nothing of the sort will occur; my mother lacks the funds to undertake such a project anyway."

"I am heartily glad of it," he cried. "May she always remain poor, provided she cannot employ her wealth in a manner less offensive to you."

"Thank you, Willoughby," Elinor replied. "Be assured, I would not sacrifice a single sentiment of your local attachment, nor that of anyone I love, for all the improvements in the world. Whatever surplus remains when I reconcile my accounts in the spring, I would prefer to lay it idle rather than expend it in a way so painful to you. However, are you truly so attached to this place that you perceive no defects in it?"

"I am," he affirmed. "To me, it is faultless. Indeed, I regard it as the sole architectural form wherein happiness is attainable. Were I wealthy enough, I would instantly demolish Combe and reconstruct it precisely according to this cottage's blueprint."

"With dark, narrow stairs and a smoking kitchen, I presume," Elinor noted dryly.

"Yes!" he cried, his tone eager. "With every detail intact—in no convenience or inconvenience should the slightest variation be perceptible. Only then, under such a roof, might I perhaps find happiness at Combe comparable to what I have experienced at Barton."

Elinor smiled. "I flatter myself that even under the disadvantages of superior rooms and a broader staircase, you will eventually find your own residence as flawless as you deem this one."

"There are certainly circumstances," Willoughby conceded, "that might greatly endear it to me; but this place holds one claim on my affection that no other can possibly share."

Mrs. Dashwood glanced with evident pleasure at Marianne, whose expressive eyes were fixed intently on Willoughby, betraying a profound understanding of his words.

"How often," Willoughby continued, lowering his voice to address Marianne directly, "did I wish, when I was at Allenham twelve months ago, that Barton Cottage were inhabited! I never passed within view of it without admiring its situation and grieving that no one dwelt therein. How little did I anticipate that the very first intelligence I would receive from Mrs. Smith upon returning to the countryside would be that Barton Cottage was taken! I felt an immediate satisfaction and interest in the event, attributable only to a prescient knowledge of the happiness I would derive from it. Must it not have been so, Marianne?"

Resuming his previous volume, he addressed Mrs. Dashwood again. "And yet, you would ruin this house? You would strip it of its simplicity through imaginary improvement! This dear parlor, where our acquaintance first commenced and where countless happy hours have since been shared, you would degrade to the status of a mere entrance hall, forcing everyone to traverse the room that has historically offered more genuine comfort and accommodation than any spacious apartment in the world could provide."

Mrs. Dashwood reiterated her assurance that no such alterations would be attempted.

"You are a good woman," he replied warmly. "Your promise brings me ease. Extend it further, and it will bring me joy. Assure me not only that your house will remain unchanged, but that I shall always find you and yours as immutable as your dwelling. Promise that you will continue to regard me with the kindness that has rendered everything belonging to you so precious to me."

The promise was granted readily, and Willoughby's demeanor throughout the evening radiated both affection and contentment.

"Shall we see you tomorrow for dinner?" Mrs. Dashwood asked as he prepared to depart.

"I decline the invitation for the morning," he responded smoothly. "We must walk to the Park to call on Lady Middleton."

He agreed to join them by four o'clock.

The following day, Mrs. Dashwood visited Lady Middleton, accompanied by two of her daughters. Marianne excused herself from the party under a trivial pretext of employment, leaving her mother content with the belief that Willoughby had promised to call upon them during their absence. Upon their return from the park, they discovered Willoughby's curricle and servant waiting at the cottage, confirming Mrs. Dashwood's conjecture. However, the moment they crossed the threshold, the scene unfolded that no foresight had prepared them for. Hardly had they stepped into the passage when Marianne emerged from the parlour, visibly distraught, her handkerchief clutched to her eyes. Without offering a greeting, she fled upstairs. Alarmed, Mrs. Dashwood and Elinor followed immediately into the room Marianne had vacated. Inside, they found only Willoughby, leaning against the mantelpiece with his back turned. As they entered, he spun around, his countenance betraying the same profound emotion that had overwhelmed his sister.

"Is anything the matter with her?" Mrs. Dashwood cried upon entering. "Is she ill?"

"I hope not," Willoughby replied, attempting to project cheerfulness, though a forced smile quickly followed. "In fact, it is I who may rather expect to be ill—for I am now suffering under a very heavy disappointment!"

"Disappointment?"

"Yes. I am unable to keep my engagement with you. Mrs. Smith has this morning exercised the privilege of riches upon a poor dependent cousin by sending me on urgent business to London. I have just received my dispatches and taken my farewell of Allenham; and, by way of exhilaration, I have come to take my farewell of you."

"To London! And are you going this morning?"

"Almost this moment."

"This is very unfortunate. But Mrs. Smith must be obliged; and her business will not detain you from us long, I hope."

Willoughby flushed as he answered. "You are very kind, but I have no idea of returning to Devonshire immediately. My visits to Mrs. Smith are never repeated within the twelvemonth."

"And is Mrs. Smith your only friend? Is Allenham the only house in the neighbourhood to which you will be welcome? For shame, Willoughby, can you wait for an invitation here?"

His color deepened further. With his eyes fixed on the floor, he could only mutter, "You are too good."

Mrs. Dashwood exchanged a look of surprise with Elinor, who felt equal astonishment. A heavy silence descended upon the room. Breaking the quiet, Mrs. Dashwood spoke first. "I have only to add, my dear Willoughby, that at Barton Cottage you will always be welcome. I will not press you to return here immediately, for only you can judge how far that might please Mrs. Smith; and on this head, I shall be no more disposed to question your judgment than to doubt your inclination."

"My engagements at present," Willoughby replied, his voice trembling slightly, "are of such a nature—that... I dare not flatter myself—" He trailed off.

Mrs. Dashwood was too stunned to speak, and another pause ensued. Willoughby broke it with a faint, strained smile. "It is folly to linger in this manner. I will not torment myself any longer by remaining among friends whose society it is impossible for me now to enjoy."

With hurried efficiency, he took leave of them all and departed. They watched him mount his carriage; within a minute, it vanished from sight. Mrs. Dashwood felt too overwhelmed for words and instantly withdrew to the adjoining room, seeking solitude to process the alarm this sudden departure had ignited. Elinor's uneasiness matched her mother's. She replayed the interaction with growing anxiety and distrust. Willoughby's behavior in taking leave—their shared embarrassment, his affected cheerfulness, and above all, his refusal to accept her mother's invitation—was profoundly disturbing. His reluctance was so unlike a lover, so alien to his own character, that it shook her foundations. One moment she feared no serious design had ever existed on his part; the next, she imagined some catastrophic quarrel had erupted between him and Marianne. The distress in which Marianne had retreated was precisely what a serious dispute would cause, yet considering the intensity of Marianne's love, a quarrel seemed almost inconceivable. Regardless of the specifics, her sister's affliction was undeniable. Elinor thought with tender compassion of that violent sorrow, fearing Marianne was not merely venting pain as a relief, but feeding and encouraging it as a duty.

Half an hour later, her mother returned. Though her eyes were red-rimmed, her countenance remained composed. "Our dear Willoughby is now several miles from Barton, Elinor," she said, sitting down to her work. "And with how heavy a heart does he travel?"

"It is all very strange. So suddenly to be gone! It seems but the work of a moment. Last night he was with us so happy, so cheerful, so affectionate? And now, after only ten minutes' notice—gone, without intending to return! Something more than what he confessed to us must have occurred. He did not speak, he did not behave like himself. You must have seen the difference as clearly as I. What can it be? Could they have quarreled? Why else such unwillingness to accept your invitation?"

"It was not inclination that he lacked, Elinor; I could plainly see that. He had not the *power* to accept it. I have thought it all over, I assure you, and I can perfectly account for everything that initially seemed strange to both of us."

"Can you, indeed!"

"Yes. I have explained it to myself in the most satisfactory way—but you, Elinor, who love to doubt where you can, it will not satisfy you, I know. Yet you shall not talk me out of my trust in it. I am persuaded that Mrs. Smith suspects his regard for Marianne, disapproves of it—perhaps because she holds other designs for him—and on that account is eager to remove him. The business she sends him to transact is likely an invention designed to dismiss him. This is what I believe to have happened. Furthermore, he is aware that she disapproves of the connection; he dares not, in his current dependent state, confess his engagement to Marianne, and thus feels obliged to submit to her schemes and abscond from Devonshire temporarily. You will tell me, I know, that this may or may not have occurred; but I will listen to no cavil unless you can offer a more satisfactory explanation. And now, Elinor, what have you to say?"

"Nothing, for you have anticipated my answer."

"Then you would have said it might or might not have happened. Oh, Elinor, how incomprehensible your feelings are! You prefer to take evil upon credit rather than good. You look out for misery for Marianne and guilt for poor Willoughby rather than an apology for the latter. You are resolved to deem him blameable simply because he took leave with less affection than his usual behavior suggests. Is no allowance to be made for inadvertence, or for spirits depressed by recent disappointment? Are no probabilities to be accepted merely because they are not certainties? Is nothing due to the man whom we all hold in such high esteem, and for whom there is no reason to feel ill-disposed? To the possibility of motives unanswerable in themselves, though unavoidably secret for a while? And after all, what exactly do you suspect him of?"

"I can hardly tell myself. But suspicion of something unpleasant is the inevitable consequence of such an alteration in his demeanor. There is great truth, however, in what you have now urged regarding the allowances we ought to make for him, and it is my wish to be candid in my judgment of everyone. Willoughby may undoubtedly have very sufficient reasons for his conduct, and I will hope that he does. But it would have been more characteristic of Willoughby to acknowledge them at once. Secrecy may be advisable; still, I cannot help wondering at its practice by him."

"Do not blame him for departing from his character where the deviation is necessary. But you really do admit the justice of what I have said in his defense? I am happy—and he is acquitted."

"Not entirely. It may be proper to conceal their engagement—if they are engaged—from Mrs. Smith, and if so, it must be highly expedient for Willoughby to remain away from Devonshire for the present. But this is no excuse for concealing it from us."

"Concealing it from us! My dear child, do you accuse Willoughby and Marianne of concealment? This is strange indeed, especially given that your eyes have been reproaching them daily for their incautiousness."

"I want no proof of their affection," Elinor insisted, "but of their engagement, I do."

"I am perfectly satisfied of both."

"Yet not a syllable has been said to you on the subject by either of them."

"I have not needed syllables where actions have spoken so plainly. Has not his behavior toward Marianne and all of us for at least the last fortnight declared that he loves and considers her his future wife, and that he feels for us the attachment of the nearest relation? Have we not perfectly understood each other? Has not my consent been daily sought by his looks, his manner, his attentive and affectionate respect? My Elinor, is it possible to doubt their engagement? How could such a thought occur to you? How is it to be supposed that Willoughby, persuaded as he must be of your sister's

Marianne would have deemed herself utterly inexcusable had she possessed the strength to sleep the first night following her departure from Willoughby. Yet, shame held no sway over her; she rose from her bed in a state of profound exhaustion that rendered the concept of disgrace irrelevant. Sleep had eluded her entirely, replaced by a vigil of weeping that drained her spirit until she emerged with a splitting headache, mute, and refusing sustenance. She inflicted upon her mother and sisters a constant, gnawing agony, rejecting all attempts at solace with a stubborn intensity. Her sensibility was a weapon of mass destruction, potent and unyielding.

By breakfast's end, she retreated into the solitude of Allenham, wandering the village grounds to indulge in the bitter juxtaposition of past joy and present ruin. The evening mirrored the morning's despair. She subjected herself to a ritual of grief, playing every melody she had once shared with Willoughby, staring fixedly at the musical lines he had penned for her until her heart grew so heavy that further sorrow seemed impossible to extract. This nourishment of mourning was her daily diet. Hours were consumed at the pianoforte, alternating between singing and sobbing, her voice frequently silenced by the sheer volume of her tears. Literature followed suit; she devoured only those volumes they had once read together, courting the misery inherent in their contrasts. Such violent affliction could not sustain itself indefinitely; within days, it settled into a calmer, deeper melancholy. Nevertheless, her solitary walks and silent meditations continued to trigger eruptions of sorrow as vivid as ever.

No letter arrived from Willoughby, nor did Marianne expect one. Her mother, Mrs. Dashwood, found the silence alarming, while Elinor's unease mounted. However, Mrs. Dashwood possessed a peculiar ability to rationalize ambiguity. "Recall, Elinor," she reasoned, "how frequently Sir John retrieves our mail personally. We have already conceded that secrecy may be prudent; thus, we cannot fault him if their correspondence passes through his hands." Elinor acknowledged the logic yet remained unsettled. A solution existed, direct and simple: she proposed asking Marianne outright whether she was engaged to Willoughby. "From you, her mother, so kind and indulgent, such a query could not offend," Elinor argued. "She was wont to be unreserved, especially with you."

"I would never dare ask such a question," Mrs. Dashwood retorted, her romantic delicacy overriding common sense. "Suppose they are not engaged; what devastation would such an inquiry cause? It would be ungenerous. I shall never deserve her trust again if I force a confession of what she intends to conceal. I know Marianne's heart; she loves me dearly, and she will reveal the truth when circumstances permit. I would never coerce the confidence of anyone, certainly not a child, for a sense of duty prevents denying her wishes." Elinor viewed this excessive generosity as a failure of judgment regarding her sister's youth, urging the matter further, but in vain. Common prudence drowned in Mrs. Dashwood's sentimentalism.

Days passed before Willoughby's name was spoken before Marianne by her family. Sir John and Mrs. Jennings lacked such nicety; their witticisms only compounded her suffering. Then, one evening, Mrs. Dashwood, picking up a volume of Shakespeare, remarked, "We have never finished *Hamlet*, Marianne; our dear Willoughby departed before we could conclude it. We shall resume when he returns..." "Months!" Marianne cried, startled. "No—nor many weeks." Mrs. Dashwood regretted her slip, but it pleased Elinor, for it elicited a response from Marianne that betrayed her certainty of Willoughby's intentions and knowledge of his plans.

About a week after his departure, Marianne was persuaded to join her sisters for their customary walk, abandoning her habit of evading companionship. Previously, if they headed toward the downs, she fled to the lanes; if they discussed the valley, she climbed the hills, ensuring her absence. But Elinor's disapproval of such isolation proved effective. They traversed the road through the valley, mostly in silence, for Marianne's mind remained untethered, and Elinor, content with securing her presence, refrained from further interrogation.

Beyond the valley's entrance, where the landscape shifted from wild richness to open expanse, a familiar stretch of road appeared—the path taken upon their arrival at Barton. Stopping to survey the vista, a new element entered the scene: a man on horseback approaching them. Within minutes, he was identified as a gentleman. Instantly, Marianne rapturously declared, "It is he; it is indeed—I know it is!" Hastening to meet him, she was intercepted by Elinor's cry, "Indeed, Marianne, I think you are mistaken. It is not Willoughby. The stature is insufficient, and the bearing lacks his distinctive air."

"He possesses it! He possesses it!" Marianne insisted, convinced. "His air, his coat, his horse. I knew precisely when he would arrive." Walking eagerly ahead, she ignored Elinor's growing certainty that her hope was misplaced. As they closed the distance to within thirty yards, Marianne's gaze faltered. Her heart sank. Abruptly turning, she hurried backward, only to be detained by her sisters' voices. A third voice, nearly as recognizable as Willoughby's, joined theirs, pleading for her to stay. With surprise, she turned to behold Edward Ferrars.

He was the sole individual in the world capable of forgiveness for failing to be Willoughby, the only one capable of extracting a smile from her. Yet, she dispelled her tears to offer him one, momentarily forgetting her own disappointment in her sister's happiness. Dismounting and handing his horse to his servant, he walked back with them to Barton, ostensibly visiting them. He was greeted with cordiality by all, but Marianne displayed a warmth of regard surpassing even Elinor's. To Marianne, however, this reunion felt like a continuation of the inexplicable coldness she had observed at Norland. On Edward's part, there was a distinct deficiency of the demeanor a lover should exhibit. He appeared confused, scarcely registering the pleasure of the encounter, devoid of rapture or gaiety. He spoke little, offering only forced responses to questions, and bestowed no marks of affection upon Elinor.

Marianne watched and listened with mounting astonishment, eventually developing a nascent dislike for Edward. Every feeling, inevitably, circled back to Willoughby, whose manners formed a stark, unfavorable contrast to those of his supposed rival. After a brief pause following the initial greetings, Marianne asked if Edward had come directly from London. "No," he replied, "I have been in Devonshire for two weeks."

"Two weeks?" she repeated, shocked that he had remained in the same county as Elinor for so long without prior contact. He looked somewhat distressed adding that he had stayed with friends near Plymouth. "Have you recently visited Sussex?" Elinor inquired. "Yes, I was at Norland about a month ago."

"And how does dear, dear Norland appear?" Marianne cried. "Dear, dear Norland," Elinor responded, "it probably resembles its usual self at this time of year. The woods and paths are heavily blanketed with dead leaves."

"Oh!" Marianne exclaimed, her voice filled with longing. "With what transporting sensation have I formerly witnessed them falling! How I delighted, as I walked, to see them driven in showers about me by the wind! What feelings the season, the air, altogether inspire! Now, there is no one to regard them. They are seen merely as a nuisance, swept hastily aside and banished from sight."

"It is not everyone," Elinor noted, "who shares your passion for dead leaves."

"No," Marianne admitted, "my feelings are rarely shared, rarely understood. But sometimes they are." As she spoke, she sank into a reverie for a moment before rousing herself. "Now, Edward," she said, drawing his attention to the landscape, "here lies Barton Valley. Look upward and strive for tranquility. Observe those hills! Have you ever seen their equals? To the left stands Barton Park, amidst those woods and plantations. You may discern the end of the house. And there, beneath that distant hill rising with such grandeur, lies our cottage."

"It is a beautiful country," he replied, "though these valleys must be filthy in winter."

"How can you conceive of filth with such sights before you?"

"Because," he smiled, "among the other objects before me, I perceive a very dirty lane."

"How strange!" Marianne murmured to herself as they proceeded. "Do you find an agreeable neighborhood here? Are the Middletons pleasant people?"

"Not all of them," Marianne answered, her voice low. "We could not be more unfortunately situated."

"Marianne!" her sister cried. "How can you say so? How can you be so unjust? They are a very respectable family, Mr. Ferrars, and have behaved in the friendliest manner toward us. Have you forgotten, Marianne, how many pleasant days we owe them?"

"No," Marianne whispered, "nor how many painful moments."

Elinor paid no heed to this remark, redirecting her focus to their guest and attempting to sustain conversation by discussing their current residence and its amenities, extorting occasional questions and comments from him. His coldness and reserve mortified her severely; she was vexed and half-angry. Yet, resolving to govern her behavior based on the past rather

Mrs. Dashwood's initial surprise at Edward's arrival dissolved instantly upon recognizing the sheer inevitability of his presence; in her estimation, his journey to Barton was the most logical progression of events imaginable. However, her fleeting astonishment gave way rapidly to a profound, enduring joy, her countenance softening with a warmth that rendered any prior reserve or coldness utterly obsolete. Before he had even crossed the threshold, the barriers of his shyness began to crumble under the weight of her genuine affection, completely overwhelmed by the captivating magnetism of her hospitality. Indeed, a man possessed of such feelings for either of her daughters found it impossible to withhold his devotion from her as well. Under the influence of her welcoming spirit, Edward quickly shed his defensive armor, his affections rekindling toward the entire household until his concern for their welfare became undeniably evident. Yet, despite this outward restoration, a palpable heaviness lingered within him. He praised the house, marveled at the view, and displayed attentiveness and kindness, but the underlying gloom remained. The entire family sensed this discrepancy, and Mrs. Dashwood, interpreting his despondency as a result of his mother's stinginess, sat down to dinner with a simmering indignation directed at all selfish parents.

"What are Mrs. Ferrars's current designs for you, Edward?" she asked once the meal concluded and the group gathered around the fire. "Are you still destined to be a reluctant orator against your own inclinations?"

"No," Edward replied, shaking his head. "I trust my mother has finally conceded that I possess neither the talent nor the desire for a public career."

"But how, then, is your reputation to be secured? For you must achieve distinction to satisfy your family's expectations. With no inclination for expenditure, no affinity for strangers, no profession, and no assurance, establishing a name seems a formidable task."

"I shall make no attempt," Edward stated firmly. "I harbor no wish to be distinguished, and I have ample reason to believe I never shall. Thank heavens! I cannot be coerced into genius or eloquence."

"You lack ambition, I am aware," Mrs. Dashwood noted gently. "Your aspirations appear entirely moderate."

"As moderate as those of the rest of humanity, I suspect," Edward countered. "Like everyone else, I wish for perfect happiness, though, naturally, in my own fashion. Greatness will not procure it for me."

"Remarkable that it wouldn't!" Marianne exclaimed. "What relevance do wealth or grandeur hold to happiness?"

"Grandeur holds little significance," Elinor corrected calmly, "but wealth plays a substantial role."

"Elinor, for shame!" Marianne protested. "Money can only generate happiness where nothing else exists. Beyond a modest competency, it offers no genuine satisfaction regarding the self."

"Perhaps," Elinor smiled, "we arrive at the same conclusion. Your 'competency' and my 'wealth' are remarkably similar; without them, according to the world's current standards, we would both agree that every form of external comfort is absent. Your ideals are simply nobler than mine. Come, what constitutes your competency?"

"Approximately eighteen hundred, perhaps two thousand pounds a year; certainly not more than that."

Elinor laughed softly. "Two thousand a year! One is my fortune! I suspected exactly how this conversation would conclude."

"And yet, two thousand annually is a truly moderate income," Marianne declared. "A family cannot reasonably be sustained on less. I assure you, I am not extravagant in my demands. A proper staff, perhaps two carriages, and hunting horses cannot be maintained on a smaller sum."

Elinor smiled again, hearing her sister so vividly describe their projected expenses at Combe Magna.

"Hunting horses!" Edward repeated. "But why must you have them? Not everyone hunts."

Marianne flushed slightly as she replied, "But most people do."

"I wish," Margaret interjected, striking upon a novel idea, "that someone would grant us all a substantial fortune individually!"

"Oh, that they would!" Marianne cried, her eyes sparkling with animation, her cheeks glowing with the radiant delight of such imagined bliss.

"We are all unanimous in that wish, I suppose," Elinor remarked, acknowledging the universal desire despite the scarcity of actual wealth.

"Oh dear!" Margaret sighed dreamily. "How happy I should be! I wonder what I would do with it!"

Marianne appeared equally uncertain, though her enthusiasm suggested otherwise.

"I should be quite puzzled to spend such a vast fortune myself," Mrs. Dashwood observed, "if my children were all to be wealthy without my assistance."

"You must commence your improvements on this house," Elinor advised. "Your difficulties will soon vanish."

"What magnificent dispatches would travel from this family to London in such an event!" Edward mused. "What a glorious day for booksellers, music merchants, and print shops! You, Miss Dashwood, would issue a general commission for every new print of merit to be sent your way. As for Marianne, knowing her magnanimous soul, there would not be sufficient music in London to content her. And books! Thomson, Cowper, Scott—she would purchase them repeatedly, buying up every copy to prevent them falling into unworthy hands. She would acquire every volume instructing her on how to admire an ancient, twisted tree. Would you not, Marianne? Forgive me if I am being impertinent, but I wished to demonstrate that I have not forgotten our old debates."

"I delight in being reminded of the past, Edward—whether melancholy or joyful, I cherish recalling it—and you will never offend me by discussing former times. You are entirely correct in guessing how I would allocate my funds; at least, a portion of my liquid assets would certainly be invested in expanding my collection of music and literature."

"And the remainder of your fortune would be distributed as annuities to the authors or their heirs."

"No, Edward, I have other plans."

"Perhaps, then, you would bestow it as a reward upon the individual who penned the most capable defense of your cherished maxim—that no one can fall in love more than once in their lifetime. Your stance on this matter remains unchanged, I presume?"

"Undoubtedly. By my age, opinions are sufficiently fixed. It is unlikely I shall encounter anything to alter them."

"Marianne is as steadfast as ever, you see," Elinor noted. "She has undergone no significant alteration."

"She has merely matured somewhat beyond her previous vivacity," Edward added.

"Nay, Edward," Marianne retorted, "you need not reproach me. You are not particularly cheerful yourself."

"Why should you assume so!" he replied, letting out a sigh. "Yet, cheerfulness has never been an intrinsic component of my character."

"Nor do I consider it a defining trait of Marianne's," Elinor observed. "I would hardly classify her as a lively girl. She is intensely earnest, fervent in all her pursuits—she speaks frequently and always with animation—but she is rarely genuinely merry."

"I believe you are correct," Edward admitted, "yet I have consistently categorized her as a spirited young woman."

"I have frequently caught myself committing such errors," Elinor confessed, "suffering from a total misapprehension of character in various aspects. I imagine people to be far more gregarious or somber, more ingenious or obtuse than they truly are, and I struggle to pinpoint the origin of this deception. Sometimes one relies on their own declarations, and very often on the assessments of others, failing to allow time for deliberation and independent judgment."

"But I believed it was proper, Elinor," Marianne insisted, "to be guided exclusively by the opinions of others. I thought our faculties of judgment were bestowed merely to serve the verdicts of our neighbors. This has always been your doctrine, surely."

"No, Marianne, never. My philosophy has never sought the subjugation of the intellect. All I have ever endeavored to influence is conduct. You must not conflate my meaning. I admit guilt in wishing you to accord our acquaintanceship generally with greater attention; but when have I advised you to adopt their sentiments or conform to their judgment in serious matters?"

"You have failed to convert your sister to your plan of universal civility," Edward told Elinor. "Do you make no progress?"

"On the contrary," Elinor replied, casting an expressive glance at Marianne.

"My judgment aligns entirely with yours on this issue," Edward stated, "though I fear my practice adheres much closer to my sister's. I never intend to cause offense, yet I am so painfully shy that I often appear negligent when I am merely held back by my innate awkwardness. I have frequently speculated that nature must have intended me for the company of the humble, for I feel so profoundly ill-at-ease among strangers of refinement!"

"Marianne possesses no shyness to excuse any neglect on her part," Elinor pointed out.

"She knows her own worth too well for feigned shame," Edward responded. "Shyness arises solely from a sense of inferiority in some respect. If I could convince myself that my manners were perfectly effortless and graceful, I would cease to be shy."

"But you would remain reserved," Marianne countered, "and that is worse."

Edward startled. "Reserved! Am I reserved, Marianne?"

"Yes, exceedingly."

"I do not comprehend you," he replied, flushing. "Reserved! In what manner? What am I to tell you? What could possibly lead you to suppose this?"

Elinor looked surprised at his visible distress but, attempting to dismiss the subject with a laugh

Elinor observed the profound despondency in her brother's demeanor with a growing, gnagging anxiety. His recent visit had yielded her only a fraction of the solace she craved, while his apparent enjoyment of the encounter seemed woefully diminished. It was painfully obvious that he was suffering; yet, she desperately needed it to be equally evident that he still regarded her with the specific, unwavering affection she was certain she had once inspired. Until now, the persistence of his interest had remained precarious, his reserve contradicting moments of animation that suggested otherwise.

The following morning, Edward joined Elinor and Marianne in the breakfast room before the household had risen. Marianne, ever eager to facilitate their union, quickly excused herself to retreat upstairs. However, barely halfway up the stairs, she heard the parlor door swing open. Turning, she found Edward emerging.

"I am proceeding to the village to inspect my horses," he announced, noting their tardiness for breakfast. "I shall return shortly."

Upon his reappearance, Edward offered renewed praise for the surrounding countryside. His excursion had revealed numerous advantageous perspectives of the valley, and the village itself, situated at a significantly higher elevation than their cottage, provided a commanding overview of the entire region that delighted him immensely. This topic naturally captured Marianne's attention. She began to articulate her own admiration for the vistas and queried him in detail regarding the specific features that had struck him most forcibly.

Edward interjected abruptly. "You must not inquire too deeply, Marianne. Remember, I possess no expertise in the picturesque. Were we to descend into particulars, my ignorance and lack of taste would surely offend you. I tend to label hills 'steep' when they ought to be described as 'bold'; I call surfaces 'strange and uncouth' when they should be termed 'irregular and rugged'; and I note distant objects as 'out of sight,' whereas they should remain indistinct through the soft haze of the atmosphere. You must accept such limited appreciation as I can honestly offer. I declare this a magnificent country—the hills are steep, the woods teem with fine timber, and the valley appears comfortable and secure, dotted with rich meadows and several neat farmhouses. It perfectly aligns with my definition of a fine country because it unites aesthetic beauty with practical utility. I daresay it is picturesque as well, simply because you admire it; I can readily imagine it filled with rocks, promontories, grey moss, and brambles, though these details escape my notice entirely. I know nothing of the picturesque."

"It seems only too true," Marianne conceded. "Yet, why do you boast of such limitations?"

"I suspect," Elinor interjected calmly, "that in avoiding one form of affectation, Edward has fallen prey to another. Believing that many individuals feign a deeper reverence for nature's beauties than they truly possess, and disgusted by such pretension, he adopts an attitude of greater indifference and less discernment than he actually holds. He is fastidious and cultivates an affectation of his own."

"Indeed," Marianne agreed. "Admiration of landscape has devolved into mere jargon. Everyone pretends to feel and attempts to describe with the taste and elegance of the original definers of picturesque beauty. I detest jargon of every sort, and often I have suppressed my feelings precisely because I lacked a vocabulary capable of describing them without resorting to worn-out, meaningless clichés."

"I am convinced," Edward stated firmly, "that you genuinely experience all the delight of a fine prospect that you claim to feel. In return, however, my sister must permit me to feel no more than I express. I enjoy a fine view, but not on picturesque principles. I dislike crooked, twisted, or blasted trees; I prefer them tall, straight, and flourishing. I hold no affection for ruined, tattered cottages. I am not fond of nettles, thistles, or heather blossoms. I derive far greater pleasure from a cozy farmhouse than from a watchtower, and a company of tidy, content villagers pleases me infinitely more than the finest bandits in existence."

Marianne gazed at Edward with astonishment and pity for her sister, while Elinor merely smiled. The discussion ceased there, leaving Marianne in thoughtful silence until a new distraction seized her focus. Seated beside Edward, she watched as he accepted his tea from Mrs. Dashwood. His hand moved directly across hers, making a ring adorned with a plait of hair conspicuously visible upon one of his fingers.

"I have never seen you wear a ring before, Edward!" she exclaimed. "Is that Fanny's hair? I recall her promising to send you some. Yet, I would have thought her hair was darker."

Marianne spoke impulsively, voicing her genuine thoughts, but upon witnessing the sharp pain etched onto Edward's face, her own regret at her lack of tact proved overwhelming. He flushed deeply, casting a fleeting glance toward Elinor before replying, "Yes; it is my sister's hair. The setting inevitably alters its perceived shade, you understand."

Elinor had met his gaze and appeared equally conscious. Instantly, she realized the hair belonged to her, feeling as certain of this fact as Marianne did. The divergence lay solely in their interpretations: where Marianne viewed it as a voluntary gift from her sister, Elinor knew it must have been acquired through some theft or deception unknown to herself. Though not in the mood to consider it an insult, Elinor affected total indifference, immediately shifting the conversation to another topic. Internally, however, she resolved to seize every opportunity to examine the hair closely, ensuring beyond all doubt that its color matched her own exactly.

Edward's embarrassment persisted for some time, eventually dissolving into a state of profound absent-mindedness. He remained unusually grave throughout the rest of the morning. Marianne harshly chastised herself for her remark, though her forgiveness might have arrived sooner had she understood how little offense it had caused her sister.

Before midday, they were visited by Sir John and Mrs. Jennings. Having heard news of a gentleman's arrival at the cottage, they came to survey the guest. With the aid of his mother-in-law, Sir John was not long in deducing that the surname Ferrars began with an 'F.' This discovery prepared a future vein of mockery directed at the devoted Elinor—a jest that nothing short of their recent acquaintance with Edward could have prevented from being launched immediately. As it happened, she gleaned only the extent of their insight, grounded in Margaret's prior instructions, through a series of highly suggestive glances.

Sir John never failed to extend invitations to the Dashwoods, either to dine at the park the following day or to share tea that evening. On this occasion, seeking to maximize the entertainment value for his visitor and fulfill his duty to contribute to their amusement, he proposed securing them for both engagements.

"You *must* drink tea with us tonight," he declared, "for we shall be entirely alone. And tomorrow, you must absolutely dine with us, for we shall be hosting a large party."

Mrs. Jennings reinforced the necessity. "And who knows but you may raise a dance," she added. "And that will certainly tempt *you*, Miss Marianne."

"A dance!" Marianne cried out. "Impossible! Who is to dance?"

"Who? Why, yourselves, the Careys, and the Whitakers, to be sure. What! Did you think nobody could dance because a certain individual, who shall remain nameless, has departed?"

"I wish with all my soul," Sir John proclaimed loudly, "that Willoughby were among us again."

This comment, coupled with Marianne's sudden blush, ignited fresh suspicions within Edward. "And who is Willoughby?" he asked quietly to Miss Dashwood, to whom he was seated. She offered him a brief response, but Marianne's countenance was far more expressive. Edward gathered sufficient evidence to comprehend not only the subtext of others' remarks but also those nuances of Marianne's expression that had previously baffled him.

Once their visitors departed, Edward immediately circled around her and whispered, "I have been guessing. Shall I reveal my hypothesis?"

"What do you mean?"

"Shall I tell you?"

"Certainly."

"Well then," he continued, "I guess that Mr. Willoughby hunts."

Marianne was taken aback and flustered, yet she could not suppress a smile at the quiet wit of his manner. After a brief pause, she said, "Oh, Edward! How can you?—But the time will come, I hope... I am sure you will like him."

"I do not doubt it," he replied, somewhat startled by her earnestness and warmth. Had he not assumed it to be a harmless jest intended to foster her general acquaintanceship, founded on nothing more substantial than a vague rumor between Mr. Willoughby and herself, he would never have dared to mention it.

Edward remained at the cottage for seven days, a duration that felt agonizingly brief given his evident reluctance to depart. Mrs. Dashwood pressed him daily to extend his stay, yet he moved with the grim determination of a soldier accepting a transfer to a hostile front, seemingly resolved to leave precisely when his morale among his hosts peaked. For the final forty-eight hours, his spirits, though volatile, showed signs of stabilization; he grew increasingly attached to the cottage and its grounds, sighing heavily at the mere suggestion of departure, claiming his schedule was entirely free, and expressing genuine confusion regarding his destination once he vacated their presence. Despite this professed uncertainty, his exit was non-negotiable. No week had evaporated so rapidly; he insisted repeatedly on its fleeting nature, uttering sentiments that betrayed a profound emotional shift while his actions remained stubbornly fixed on departure. He harbored no fondness for Norland and despised the prospect of returning to London, yet he was trapped in a binary orbit between the two, compelled to choose one over the other regardless of his personal cost. He valued their kindness above all earthly rewards, finding his greatest solace in their proximity, yet he was destined to sever those ties within a week, defying their pleas and his own inclinations, stripped of any temporal constraints.

Elinor attributed the inexplicable rigidity of Edward's behavior to his mother's influence, a convenient scapegoat whose character remained sufficiently obscure to explain any eccentricity in her son's conduct. Though disappointed and occasionally irritated by his inconsistent demeanor toward her, she maintained a charitable disposition, readily granting him the leniency and understanding that had previously been wrung from her with difficulty in service to Willoughby. His lack of vitality, transparency, and consistency was dismissed as symptoms of his compromised independence and his acute awareness of Mrs. Ferrars' manipulative designs. The brevity of his visit and the steely resolve with which he planned his departure stemmed from the same shackles: a fettered inclination and an unavoidable necessity to appease his mother. The ancient, grinding conflict between filial duty and personal will, pitting parent against child, was the root cause of all his distress. She yearned desperately to know when this struggle would conclude, when Mrs. Ferrars would reform and her son would finally possess the liberty to pursue happiness. Denied such answers, she sought refuge in renewing her faith in Edward's enduring affection, recalling every gesture of regard he had offered during his stay at Barton, and particularly the constant, tangible token he wore upon his finger—a flattering testament to his devotion.

"I believe, Edward," Mrs. Dashwood remarked over breakfast on the final morning, "that you would derive greater happiness from adopting a profession to occupy your time and lend substance to your ambitions. While this might impose certain inconveniences on your friends, limiting the hours you can dedicate to them, you would gain a significant advantage in at least one regard: you would know precisely where to go when you leave us."

"I assure you," Edward replied, "that I have contemplated this very issue with the frequency you suggest. It remains, and likely will forever remain, a crushing misfortune that I possess no obligatory duties, no profession to command my attention or grant me a semblance of autonomy. Unfortunately, my own fastidiousness, compounded by the expectations of my family, has rendered me what I am: an idle, helpless entity. We could never reconcile our choices regarding a career path. I consistently favored the clergy, a preference I hold to this day. Yet, it lacked the requisite flash for my family's tastes. They advocated for the military, a path far too ostentatious for my constitution. The legal profession was deemed sufficiently genteel; numerous young men residing in the Temple cut a fine figure in high society, traversing the city in sophisticated carriages. However, I possessed no aptitude for the law, even in its less abstract applications, which my family endorsed. Regarding the navy, it certainly enjoyed fashionable cachet, but I was already too advanced in age when the discussion initially arose. Ultimately, since there was no pressing necessity for me to acquire a profession—I could be equally dashing and extravagant without a uniform as with one—idleness was declared the most advantageous and honorable course. A young man of eighteen is rarely so driven by the desire for occupation as to resist the solicitations of his friends to accomplish nothing. Consequently, I was enrolled at Oxford and have remained properly idle ever since."

"The logical conclusion of which," Mrs. Dashwood countered, "is that since leisure has failed to cultivate your own contentment, your children will be raised to pursue as myriad careers, employments, and trades as Columella's catalog suggests."

"They shall be raised," he stated with a somber gravity, "to be as dissimilar to myself as humanly possible. In sentiment, in action, in circumstance, in every conceivable aspect."

"Enough, enough," interjected Mrs. Dashwood, dismissing his gloom. "This is merely an overflow of immediate despondency, Edward. You are in a melancholic frame of mind and assume that anyone unlike yourself must necessarily be miserable. Remember that the agony of separation from friends is universal, transcending education and station. Recognize your own source of happiness. You require nothing but patience—or, if you prefer a more seductive term, call it hope. Your mother will secure for you, in due course, the independence you crave; it is her duty, and it shall inevitably become her joy to ensure your entire youth is not squandered in discontent. Who knows what a few months might achieve?"

"I suspect," Edward replied flatly, "that I may challenge many months to produce any positive outcome for me."

This despondent turn of mind, though concealed from Mrs. Dashwood, cast a pall over the impending farewell, leaving an unsettling residue on Elinor's psyche that demanded considerable effort and time to suppress. Determined to master her emotions and avoid appearing more devastated than the rest of her family, she rejected the strategy employed by Marianne on similar occasions: the amplification and fixation of sorrow through seclusion, silence, and idleness. Their methods differed radically, yet both were perfectly calibrated to their respective objectives.

The moment Edward crossed the threshold, Elinor retreated to her drawing table, immersing herself in work until late afternoon. She neither sought nor avoided mentions of his name, displaying an engagement with the household's general affairs that rivaled her previous intensity. While this conduct may not have diminished her internal grief, it successfully prevented its escalation and spared her mother and sisters undue anxiety.

Such behavior, diametrically opposed to Marianne's, held no merit in the latter's estimation, just as Marianne's apparent faultlessness seemed lacking to Elinor. The discipline of self-command proved effortless for Marianne; with passions of such ferocity, restraint was impossible, whereas with calm affections, it carried no virtue. That her sister's affections were indeed tranquil, Marianne dared not deny, though she burned with shame to admit it. Conversely, she provided striking evidence of the potency of her own feelings by continuing to love and respect her sister, despite the mortifying realization of their fundamental differences.

She did not isolate herself from her family, nor did she retreat into the house to avoid them, nor did she lie awake indulging in nocturnal rumination. Instead, Elinor discovered that every day afforded sufficient leisure to contemplate Edward and his enigmatic conduct, exploring every nuance her fluctuating mood permitted—from tender concern and pity to approval, censure, and doubt. There were frequent intervals, often dictated by the absence of her mother and sisters or the nature of their occupations, where conversation was effectively silenced, producing the effects of solitude. Her mind was invariably liberated; her thoughts could not be tethered elsewhere. Thus, the past and future concerning a subject of such profound interest inevitably occupied her foreground, forcing her attention and monopolizing her memory, reflection, and imagination.

One morning, shortly after Edward's departure, a reverie interrupted by the arrival of guests pulled her from her musings. She happened to be entirely alone. The closing of the small gate leading to the green court in front of the house caught her eye, directing her gaze to the window. She observed a substantial procession approaching the door. Among the group were Sir John and Lady Middleton, along with Mrs. Jennings, accompanied by two strangers—a gentleman and a lady—completely unfamiliar to her. Seated near the window, she watched as Sir John, spotting her, abandoned the remainder of the party to perform the formalities of knocking. Stepping directly onto the lawn, he compelled her to unlatch the casement to address him, despite the narrow distance between the door and the window making simultaneous speech nearly impossible without cross-hearing.

"Well," he announced cheerfully, "we have procured some strangers for you. How do you fare with them?"

"Hush! They will overhear you."

"Never mind if they do. It is merely the Palmers. Charlotte is remarkably lovely, I can assure you. You may observe her if you glance in this direction."

Certain she would glimpse her within moments without granting such permission, Elinor politely requested exemption.

"Where is Marianne? Has she fled because we have arrived? I perceive her instrument is open."

"She is walking, I believe," Elinor replied.

At that juncture, Mrs. Jennings joined them, possessing insufficient patience to await the opening of the door before broadcasting her news. She hurried to the window, shouting, "How do you do, my dear? How fares Mrs. Dashwood? And where are your sisters? What! All alone! You will delight in a bit of company to accompany you. I have brought my other son and daughter to pay you a visit. Simply consider the suddenness of their arrival! I thought I detected

The Miss Dashwoods crossed the threshold of the drawing-room the following day, entering at one door just as Mrs. Palmer burst through the opposite entrance. The visitor arrived with a bounce in her step and a smile that masked the underlying tension of their impending departure. She greeted them with exaggerated warmth, clasping each hand in turn and expressing profound delight at their presence.

"I am so glad to see you!" she exclaimed, settling herself between Elinor and Marianne. "For it is such a wretched day I feared you wouldn't come, which would be disastrous given we leave again tomorrow. We simply must go; the Westons arrive next week, you know. It was entirely impulsive on our part—we knew nothing until the carriage pulled up to the door, and then Mr. Palmer asked if I'd accompany him to Barton. He is dreadfully amusing! He never reveals anything unless provoked. I'm terribly sorry we can't extend our stay, though I trust we'll reunite in town shortly."

Elinor and Marianne politely terminated this line of speculation. "Not to town?" Mrs. Palmer shrieked, laughing. "I shall be utterly disappointed if you don't. I could procure the finest residence imaginable for you, right next door to ours in Hanover Square. You must come, truly. I'd be delighted to act as chaperone whenever Mrs. Dashwood feels unable to venture into society." They thanked her profusely yet firmly resisted her entreaties.

"Oh, my dear," Mrs. Palmer cried to her husband, who had just entered the room, "you must assist me in persuading the Miss Dashwoods to spend this winter in town." Her spouse offered no response; after a perfunctory bow to the ladies, he launched into a tirade against the weather. "How repugnant it all is!" he declared. "Such conditions render everything and everyone loathsome. Confinement indoors generates as much ennui as the rain outside; it breeds a hatred for one's acquaintances. What the devil is wrong with Sir John for lacking a billiard room? How few comprehend true comfort! Sir John is as obtuse as the sky."

The remainder of the party filtered in shortly thereafter. "I fear, Miss Marianne," Sir John remarked, "you haven't managed your customary stroll to Allenham today." Marianne cast a somber glance and remained silent. "Oh, cease your coquetry before us," Mrs. Palmer retorted. "We are privy to the truth, I assure you. Moreover, I admire your discernment immensely; I find him exceedingly handsome. We reside but a short distance from him in the countryside, perhaps ten miles, I daresay."

"Much closer to thirty," her husband interjected.
