"Ach, well! Negligible variance. I've never visited his estate, though rumors suggest it's a charming retreat."
"As vile a location as I've ever encountered," Mr. Palmer countered flatly.

Marianne maintained a perfect facade of silence, though her features betrayed her keen interest. "Is it truly unsightly?" Mrs. Palmer persisted. "Then it must be some other locale that possesses such allure, presumably."

Once seated in the dining room, Sir John noted with regret that only eight souls occupied the table. "My dear," he addressed his wife, "it is vexing that we are so few. Why didn't you invite the Gilberts to join us today?"
"Didn't I inform you, Sir John, prior to your inquiry, that it was impossible? They dined with us yesterday."
"You and I, Sir John," Mrs. Jennings interjected gently, "ought not to adhere to such rigid formalities."
"Then you would be profoundly uncivilized," Mr. Palmer snapped.
"My love, you contradict everyone," his wife replied with her characteristic laughter. "Are you aware you're being quite rude?"
"I wasn't aware I was contradicting anyone by labeling your mother ill-bred."
"Aye, you may revile me as you please," the kindly elderly lady conceded. "You've secured Charlotte from my grasp, and I cannot reclaim her. Thus, I hold the upper hand." Charlotte erupted in hearty laughter at the notion of her husband's inability to dismiss her, exclaiming that she cared not for his irritability, for they were destined to coexist forever.

It was impossible to encounter someone more relentlessly cheerful or determinedly content than Mrs. Palmer. Her husband's calculated indifference, insolence, and perpetual dissatisfaction failed to wound her; conversely, his scolding and vitriol served only to divert her amusement. "Mr. Palmer is so droll!" she whispered conspiratorially to Elinor. "He is perpetually in a foul mood." Elinor, after brief observation, hesitated to credit him with genuine, unadulterated malice or rudeness. Perhaps his temperament had soured due to discovering, like many men, that an inexplicable bias toward beauty had landed him with a remarkably foolish wife. Yet, she recognized this error was too ubiquitous for any sensible man to suffer lasting injury from it. Rather, it appeared to stem from a desire for distinction—a wish to appear superior to others. The motive was commonplace enough to warrant no wonder, though the method, while successful in establishing his superiority through ill-breeding, was unlikely to attract anyone beyond his wife.

"Oh, my dear Miss Dashwood," Mrs. Palmer resumed shortly, "I have a significant favor to request of you and your sister. Will you spend Christmas at Cleveland? Pray, do—and come while the Westons are present. You cannot conceive how joyful I shall be! It will be absolutely delightful!—My love," turning to her husband, "do you not yearn for the Miss Dashwoods to visit Cleveland?"
"Certainly," he replied with a sneer—"I came to Devonshire with no other purpose."
"There now," she triumphed, "you see Mr. Palmer anticipates you; thus, you cannot refuse." Both ladies eagerly and resolutely declined her invitation.

"But indeed you must and shall come. I am certain you will adore it above all else. The Westons will be with us, making it purely delightful. You cannot imagine how sweet Cleveland is; moreover, we are so lively now, for Mr. Palmer is constantly traversing the countryside campaigning against the election, attracting so many diners I've never witnessed before—it's enchanting! Alas, poor fellow! It is exhausting for him, as he is compelled to win everyone's affection." Elinor struggled to maintain composure as she acknowledged the hardship of such an obligation.

"How charming it will be," Charlotte chimed in, "once he enters Parliament! Won't it? How I shall laugh! It will be absurd to see all his correspondence addressed to him with 'M.P.' appended. But do you know, he insists he'll never frank for me? He declares he won't. Don't you, Mr. Palmer?"
Mr. Palmer ignored her completely.
"He cannot bear writing, you understand," she continued, "he finds it utterly shocking."
"No," he retorted, "I never uttered anything so irrational. Do not attribute all your linguistic abuses to me."
"There now; witness how droll he is. This is invariably his manner! Occasionally he refuses to speak to me for half a day, then erupts with something so witty regarding trivial matters."

She startled Elinor significantly upon returning to the drawing-room by inquiring whether she did not find Mr. Palmer excessively likable. "Certainly," Elinor replied smoothly; "he appears very agreeable."
"Well—I am so relieved you feel that way. I thought you would, for he is so pleasant; and Mr. Palmer is excessively pleased with you and your sisters, I can assure you. You cannot guess how disappointed he will be if you decline Cleveland.—I cannot fathom why you should object." Elinor was once again compelled to decline, shifting the topic to halt her entreaties. She suspected that, residing in the same county, Mrs. Palmer might offer a more detailed account of Willoughby's general character than the Middletons' limited acquaintance permitted. Eager to secure confirmation of his virtues sufficient to dispel Marianne's fears, she initiated inquiries about whether they saw much of Mr. Willoughby at Cleveland and if they were intimate with him.

"Oh dear, yes; I know him extremely well," Mrs. Palmer responded. "Though I never actually spoke to him, indeed; I've merely glimpsed him frequently in town. Somehow, fate conspired so I never stayed at Barton while he was at Allenham. Mama spotted him here once previously, but I was with my uncle at Weymouth. Nevertheless, I presume we would have encountered him often in Somersetshire had it not been unluckily arranged that we never shared the countryside. He spends very little time at Combe, I believe; but even if he did, I doubt Mr. Palmer would visit him, for he opposes the government, you know, plus it's so remote. I know precisely why you inquire about him; your sister intends to marry him. I am monstrously pleased, for then I shall have her as a neighbor."

"Upon my word," Elinor replied, "you seem to know considerably more about the matter than I do, provided you have grounds to anticipate such a union."
"Don't feign ignorance; everyone discusses it. I assure you I learned of it during my transit through town."
"My dear Mrs. Palmer!"
"Upon my honor, I did.—I encountered Colonel Brandon Monday morning in Bond Street, just before we departed town, and he informed me directly."
"You astonish me greatly. Colonel Brandon telling you! Surely you must be mistaken

The Palmers departed for Cleveland the following day, leaving the households of Barton Park to their own devices. Yet this solitude proved fleeting. Before Elinor could fully process the peculiarities of Charlotte's unexplained happiness or Sir John's simplistic display of competence—a performance that highlighted the frequent absurdity of marital dynamics—Sir John and Mrs. Jennings mobilized their social machinery to introduce her to fresh acquaintances.

During a morning excursion to Exeter, they encountered two young ladies whom Mrs. Jennings identified as distant relatives. This connection was sufficient for Sir John to issue an immediate invitation to the Park, contingent only upon the conclusion of their current engagements in Exeter. These obligations dissolved instantly before the allure of the invitation. Upon Sir John's return, Lady Middleton was thrown into a state of genuine alarm. She was to host two strangers she had never seen, regarding whose elegance—or even basic gentility—she possessed zero evidence. The assurances of her husband and mother held no weight in her pragmatic assessment. Compounding the insult was the fact that they were her relations; consequently, Mrs. Jennings' attempts at consolation were logically flawed, advising her daughter not to worry about their fashionability, as they were all cousins and destined to endure one another. Despite the impossibility of preventing their arrival, Lady Middleton resigned herself to the situation with the practiced philosophy of a well-bred woman, reserving her only outlet for issuing a gentle reprimand to her husband approximately six times daily.

The sisters arrived with an appearance that was neither ungenteel nor unfashionable. Their attire was sharp, their manners civil, and they displayed an effusive delight in the house and its furnishings. Furthermore, possessing a profound, almost pathological affection for children, they secured Lady Middleton's favorable opinion within the hour. She declared them delightful girls, a sentiment that constituted enthusiastic admiration for her ladyship. Sir John's confidence in his own discernment soared at this animated praise, prompting him to rush to the cottage to inform the Dashwood sisters of the Miss Steeles' arrival and to assure them of their status as the sweetest girls in existence. However, such hyperbolic commendation yielded little intelligence; Elinor knew that the title of "sweetest" was distributed liberally across England among women of varying forms, faces, tempers, and intellects. Sir John insisted the entire family accompany him to the Park to view his guests. A benevolent, albeit socially oblivious, man, he found it painful to keep even a third cousin to himself.

"Do come now," he urged, his voice rising in pitch. "Pray come—you must come—I declare you shall come. You cannot imagine how you will like them. Lucy is monstrously pretty, so good-humored, and agreeable! The children are already clinging to her as if she were an old acquaintance. Both sisters are dying to see you; they heard in Exeter that you are the most beautiful creatures in the world, and I have confirmed this truth to them, adding significantly more. You will be delighted, I am sure. They have filled the entire carriage with toys for the children. How can you be so cross as to refuse? After all, you are my cousins, in a fashion. You are mine, and they are my wife's, so the bloodline connects us."

Despite his fervor, Sir John failed to persuade them immediately. He secured only a promise that they would call at the Park within a day or two, leaving the Dashwoods in stunned silence at their indifference. He then retreated to boast anew of the Miss Steeles' charms to his wife, repeating the cycle of mutual flattery.

When the scheduled visit finally occurred, the inspection revealed little merit in the eldest sister, who was nearly thirty and possessed a face that was plainly devoid of sensibility. In contrast, the younger sister, barely twenty-three, exhibited considerable beauty. Her features were pleasing, accompanied by a sharp, quick eye and a brisk demeanor that, while lacking actual grace, conferred distinct presence. Their manners were notably civil, and Elinor quickly granted them credit for a modicum of sense, observing the constant, judicious effort they expended to please Lady Middleton. With the children, they were in a state of perpetual ecstasy, extolling their beauty, courting their attention, and indulging their whims. Any spare moments were consumed by admiring Lady Middleton's activities or copying patterns from her latest dresses, which had previously captivated them.

For those who court through such foibles, a fond mother—who, in her pursuit of praise for her offspring, becomes the most rapacious of humans—is also the most credulous. Her demands are exorbitant, yet she accepts anything. Thus, the excessive affection and endurance shown by the Miss Steeles toward Lady Middleton's children were received without suspicion. Lady Middleton watched with maternal complacency as her cousins subjected her progeny to impertinent encroachments and mischievous tricks. She observed their sashes loosened, their hair tugged behind their ears, their workbags rifled, and their knives and scissors stolen, interpreting these acts as a reciprocal enjoyment. The only surprise she registered was that Elinor and Marianne sat so composedly by, refusing to participate in the chaos.

"John is in such spirits today!" she exclaimed, watching Sir John snatch Miss Steele's pocket-handkerchief and toss it out the window. "He is full of monkey tricks."

Shortly thereafter, upon the second son violently pinching the same lady's finger, she fondly remarked, "How playful William is!"

"And here is my sweet little Annamaria," she added, tenderly embracing a three-year-old who had remained silent for the previous two minutes. "She is always so gentle and quiet—never was there such a quiet little thing!"

Unfortunately, in bestowing these embraces, a pin in her ladyship's head-dress lightly scratched the child's neck. This minor injury elicited screams of such violence that they could hardly be surpassed by any creature professionally designated as noisy. The mother's consternation was extreme, yet it paled compared to the alarm of the Miss Steeles. Every resource of affection was deployed to assuage the suffering infant. She was seated in her mother's lap, bombarded with kisses, while one Miss Steele knelt to bathe the wound with lavender water and the other stuffed her mouth with sugar plums. Such rewards for tears rendered the child too wise to stop screaming. She continued to shriek and sob lustily, kicking her brothers whenever they attempted to touch her, rendering all united soothing efforts futile until Lady Middleton recalled a similar incident from the previous week where apricot marmalade had successfully treated a bruised temple. The same remedy was eagerly proposed, and a slight cessation of screams upon hearing it gave rise to cautious hope. The child was carried out in her mother's arms to retrieve the medicine. As the two boys elected to follow, despite earnest entreaties to remain, the four young ladies were left in a silence the room had not known for hours.

"Poor little creatures!" Miss Steele murmured as soon as they departed. "It might have been a very sad accident."

"Yet I hardly know how," cried Marianne, "unless it had been under totally different circumstances. But this is the usual method of heightening alarm where there is nothing genuinely alarming in reality."

"What a sweet woman Lady Middleton is!" declared Lucy Steele.

Marianne remained silent; it was impossible for her to utter words she did not feel, regardless of the occasion's triviality. Consequently, the burden of fabricating polite lies fell entirely upon Elinor. When called upon, she spoke of Lady Middleton with greater warmth than she truly felt, though with far less enthusiasm than Miss Lucy.

"And Sir John too," the elder sister cried. "What a charming man he is!"

Once again, Miss Dashwood's commendation, being merely simple and just, lacked éclat. She merely observed that he was perfectly good-humored and friendly.

"And what a charming little family they have! I never saw such fine children in my life.—I declare I quite doat upon them already, and indeed I am always distractedly fond of children."

"I should guess so," Elinor replied with a smile, "based on what I have witnessed this morning."

"I have a notion," said Lucy, "that you consider the little Middletons rather too indulged; perhaps they are the epitome of excess. But it is so natural in Lady Middleton. For my part, I love to see children full of life and spirit; I cannot bear them if they are tame and quiet."

"I confess," Elinor replied, "that while I reside at Barton Park, I never think of tame and quiet children with any abhorrence."

A brief pause followed, broken by Miss Steele, who seemed eager for conversation and spoke rather abruptly. "And how do you like Devonshire, Miss Dashwood? I suppose you were very sorry to leave Sussex."

Caught off guard by the familiarity of the inquiry, Elinor admitted she was.

"Norland is a prodigiously beautiful place, is it not?" Miss Steele added.

"We have heard Sir John admire it excessively," Lucy interjected, seemingly feeling the need to apologize for her sister's boldness.

"I think everyone *must* admire it," Elinor corrected, "whoever has seen the place; though it cannot be supposed that anyone can estimate its beauties as we do."

"And did you have many smart beaux there? I suppose you do not have so many in this part of the world; for my part, I think they are a vast addition always."

"But why should you assume," Lucy said, looking ashamed of her sister's presumption, "that there are not as many genteel young men in Devonshire as in Sussex?"

"Nay, my dear, I'm

Marianne, possessing a constitution of spirit that brooked no impertinence, vulgarity, or mediocrity, was currently in a frame of mind where the Miss Steeles' presence was not merely unwelcome but actively repulsive. Her icy reception served as a rigid barrier, effectively halting their attempts at familiarity. Yet, it was precisely this rejection that catalyzed a strategic shift in their approach, specifically from Lucy Steele, who seized every opening to bridge the gap with a calculated display of ease and frankness. Lucy possessed a natural acuity; her observations were sharp and often witty, rendering her an agreeable conversationalist for brief intervals. However, Elinor quickly discerned that this charm was unmoored from intellect. Lucy's mind remained stagnant, devoid of the cultivation that would elevate her wit into wisdom. Her ignorance regarding basic facts was impossible to mask, regardless of Elinor's diplomatic efforts to appear magnanimous. While Elinor felt a pang of pity for wasted potential, she viewed Lucy's moral deficiencies with cold detachment. The girl's relentless flattery, her obsessive attentiveness, and her transparent insincerity revealed a profound lack of rectitude. To associate with someone who combined ignorance with duplicity offered no enduring satisfaction.

"You will find my question peculiar," Lucy began one afternoon as they traversed the path from the Park to the cottage, "but pray, are you personally acquainted with your sister-in-law's mother, Mrs. Ferrars?"

Elinor perceived the oddity immediately, her expression reflecting the query's absurdity. "I have never met Mrs. Ferrars," she replied.

"Indeed!" Lucy exclaimed, her eyes narrowing with feigned surprise. "I marvel at that. One assumes you encountered her during visits to Norland. Consequently, you cannot describe her character?"

"No," Elinor responded, carefully withholding her true assessment of Edward's mother and resisting the urge to indulge what appeared to be idle curiosity. "I know nothing of her."

"I am certain you deem me strangely intrusive," Lucy continued, watching Elinor closely. "Yet, perhaps there are reasons. I trust I may venture further; however, I hope you grant me the justice of believing I possess no intent to be rude."

Elinor offered a polite dismissal, and they walked in silence for several minutes until Lucy broke the quiet. "I cannot abide the notion that you view me as curiously impertinent. I would sooner endure anything than offend someone whose esteem is so valuable as yours. I would place absolute faith in you; indeed, I would welcome your counsel on managing this precarious position. Nevertheless, there is no need to burden you. I regret you do not know Mrs. Ferrars."

"I regret that I do *not*," Elinor stated, her astonishment palpable. "If knowing my opinion of her could benefit *you*, I would share it. But truly, I fail to comprehend why you, seemingly unrelated to that family, would inquire so seriously about her character."

"I suppose you are right, and I do not wonder at your reaction. But if I were to reveal the truth, your surprise would diminish. Mrs. Ferrars is irrelevant to me for the moment—the timing of our connection depends entirely on her whims. Soon, however, we shall be intimately linked." She lowered her gaze, adopting an air of bashful innocence, casting a single, assessing glance at Elinor to gauge her reaction.

"Good heavens!" Elinor cried. "What do you imply? Are you acquainted with Mr. Robert Ferrars? Could you possibly be?" The prospect of such a sister-in-law held no delight for her.

"No," Lucy corrected smoothly. "Not Mr. *Robert* Ferrars—I have never laid eyes on him. But," she fixed her gaze upon Elinor, "to his eldest brother."

What surged through Elinor was a mixture of pain and disbelief so potent it threatened to overwhelm her. She turned to Lucy, stunned, unable to decipher the motive behind such a declaration. Though her complexion flushed, she maintained her composure, refusing to succumb to hysteria or fainting.

"You have cause to be surprised," Lucy continued. "Naturally, you knew nothing prior. He never hinted at this to you or your family; it was intended as a profound secret, guarded faithfully by me to this hour. No relation knows except Anne, whom I would never have informed had I not relied utterly on your discretion. I realized my inquiries about Mrs. Ferrars seemed bizarre and required explanation. I am confident Mr. Ferrars will not be displeased that I have entrusted you with this, given his high regard for your entire family—he views you and the other Miss Dashwoods as his own sisters."

She paused. Elinor remained silent, her mind reeling. Eventually, she forced herself to speak, masking her turmoil with a calm demeanor. "May I inquire if your engagement is longstanding?"

"We have been engaged for four years."

"Four years!"

"Yes."

Though shaken, Elinor struggled to accept the reality. "I was unaware you were even acquainted until recently."

"Our acquaintance predates this significantly. He was a pupil under my uncle's care for a considerable period."

"My uncle!"

"Yes; Mr. Pratt. Have you never heard him discuss Mr. Pratt?"

"I believe I have," Elinor admitted, summoning strength as her emotions intensified. "He spent four years with my uncle, residing at Longstaple near Plymouth. It was there our acquaintance began, as my sister and I frequented my uncle's home. Our engagement was formed there, though not until a year after he ceased his studies. Yet, he remained almost constantly with us thereafter. I was reluctant to proceed without my mother's approval, but my youth and affection blinded me to prudence. Though you may not know him as well as I, Miss Dashwood, you must recognize his capability to inspire sincere devotion."

"Certainly," Elinor replied mechanically. After a pause, she added, bolstered by a renewed conviction in Edward's honor and Lucy's deceit, "Engaged to Mr. Edward Ferrars! I confess I am so astounded that I must assume a mistake of identity. We cannot refer to the same Mr. Ferrars."

"We refer to no other," Lucy declared, smiling confidently. "Mr. Edward Ferrars, eldest son of Mrs. Ferrars of Park Street and brother of your sister-in-law, Mrs. John Dashwood, is the individual in question. You must concede I am unlikely to err regarding the name of the man upon whom my happiness rests."

"It is strange," Elinor murmured, trapped in agonizing confusion, "that I have never heard him mention your name."

"No; given our station, it was inevitable. Our primary concern has been secrecy. You knew nothing of me or my family, eliminating any necessity to introduce me. Furthermore, his terror of his sister discovering anything ensured he never mentioned it."

Silence fell between them. Elinor's sense of security crumbled, yet her self-control held firm. "Four years you have waited," she said firmly.

"Yes; and heaven knows how much longer we may endure. Poor Edward! It drains his spirit completely." Pulling a small miniature from her pocket, she added, "To avoid error, please examine this portrait. It fails to capture his true essence, yet I trust you cannot mistake the subject. I have possessed it for over three years."

She handed the image to Elinor. Upon viewing it, any lingering doubt evaporated. It was undeniably Edward's face. She returned it immediately, acknowledging the likeness.

"I have never reciprocated with my portrait," Lucy lamented, "which vexes me greatly, as he has been so eager to obtain it! I intend to send it the first opportunity."

"You act wisely," Elinor replied calmly.

They walked a short distance in silence before Lucy resumed. "I am convinced you will guard this secret faithfully, for you understand its critical importance. My mother would never approve; she is excessively proud, and I possess no fortune."

"I sought no confidence from you," Elinor stated. "Yet, you accord me fair credit. Your secret is safe with me. However, pardon my surprise at such an unnecessary disclosure. Surely, you realize my knowledge adds no protection to your secret."

As she spoke, Elinor scrutinized Lucy's face, searching for cracks in the facade, but found none.

"I feared you would deem I was overstepping boundaries," Lucy said. "Though we have not been personally acquainted long, I have known your family by reputation for years. Seeing you, I felt an immediate kinship. Moreover, in this instance, I believed transparency owed to you following my persistent inquiries about Edward's mother. I am so unfortunate as to lack anyone whose counsel I can seek. Anne is the sole confidante, yet she possesses no judgment; she harms me more than helps. She lacks discretion, and I remain terrified she will betray me. Consider the panic I endured yesterday when Sir John mentioned Edward's name, fearing Anne would expose everything. The mental anguish I endure for Edward's sake over these four years is unimaginable. Everything hangs in suspense; we meet barely twice a year. I wonder my heart remains intact."

She produced a handkerchief, but Elinor felt no compassion.

"Occasionally," Lucy continued, dabbing her eyes, "I consider breaking the engagement entirely." She looked directly at Elinor. "Yet, at other times, I lack the resolve. I cannot bear the thought of causing him misery, nor can I endure the loss of someone so dear to me. What counsel would you offer, Miss Dashwood? What would you do?"

Elinor's reliance on Lucy's word was absolute; in this instance, the probability of deceit was zero. To imagine that Lucy would fabricate a falsehood of such magnitude, driven by no tangible gain, was logically impossible. Every piece of circumstantial evidence—their shared past at Mr. Pratt's, Edward's recent melancholic demeanor near Plymouth, his evident dissatisfaction with his prospects, his ambiguous conduct toward Elinor, and the startling intimacy Lucy displayed regarding Norland and the Dashwoods—conspired to form an irrefutable chain of facts. The letter, the ring, the portrait; these were not mere whispers but solid proof that Edward had wronged her. For a fleeting moment, fueled by righteous indignation, Elinor allowed herself to focus solely on her own betrayal. Yet, the tide of her reasoning quickly shifted.

Had Edward been consciously deceiving her? Had he feigned affection he did not possess? Or was his engagement to Lucy merely a contract of convenience rather than a union of hearts? Elinor dismissed the notion that his current feelings for Lucy were genuine. Whatever sentiment existed in the past, it was dead. His heart belonged entirely to hers—a truth confirmed by the collective observation of her mother, sisters, and Fanny during their days at Norland. It was not a delusion born of her own vanity. He loved her. This realization acted as a powerful solvent, dissolving the sharp edges of her anger. How much easier forgiveness became when framed as pity for a man who had squandered his own happiness.

Edward's error in staying at Norland, allowing his influence over her to grow beyond propriety, was indefensible. But if he had harmed her, he had inflicted far greater damage upon himself. While Elinor might eventually recover her tranquility, Edward's outlook seemed bleak. Could he truly find contentment with Lucy Steele? With his known integrity, delicacy, and cultivated mind, could he settle for a partner described as illiterate, artful, and selfish? The infatuation of youth might have blinded him to these flaws, but four years of rational maturity would surely have exposed them. Meanwhile, Lucy's immersion in inferior society and frivolous pursuits had likely eroded the very simplicity that once complemented her beauty. Furthermore, if familial opposition had previously weighed heavily on a hypothetical marriage between Edward and Elinor, the disparity in status and fortune now made such a match virtually impossible.

These reflections, arriving in painful succession, transformed Elinor's grief. She wept not for her own loss, but for his. Convinced that she bore no blame for their separation and believing Edward retained her esteem, she summoned the strength to conceal the crushing blow from her mother and Marianne. When she rejoined them at dinner, barely two hours after her hopes extinguished, her composure was flawless. To the outside observer, there was no trace of mourning; yet secretly, Elinor navigated a labyrinth of obstacles destined to sever her forever from her love, while Marianne dwelt on the perceived perfection of a man whose heart she believed wholly possessed.

This necessity of secrecy, though taxing, proved to be a relief. Spared the agony of explaining her pain to those who would only amplify it with their own sorrow, and protected from the harsh judgment Marianne and her mother might level against Edward based on their excessive partiality for Elinor, she found solace in isolation. Their counsel offered no aid; their tears would only deepen her distress. Alone, bolstered by her own good sense, Elinor maintained an unshakeable firmness and an invariable cheerfulness that belied the poignancy of her regret.

Despite the initial discomfort of her first conversation with Lucy, Elinor felt an urgent desire to revisit the topic. She needed further particulars of their engagement, a clearer understanding of Lucy's true sentiments, and above all, she sought to demonstrate to Lucy that she viewed the matter strictly as a friend. Elinor feared her earlier agitation had cast doubt on this neutrality. Lucy's apparent jealousy was evident; Edward's high praise of Elinor, coupled with his willingness to confide such a vital secret to someone he had barely known, suggested a rivalry. Even Sir John's jesting remarks carried weight. Given Elinor's certainty of Edward's love, Lucy's jealousy was a natural reaction, and her very confession served as proof of it. Why else reveal the affair unless to warn Elinor of Lucy's superior claims and instruct her to stay away?

Resolved to act with honor and to minimize contact with Edward, Elinor nonetheless determined to reassure Lucy that her own heart remained unwounded. Since she could expect no new information that would cause further pain, she trusted her ability to recount the particulars with equanimity. However, opportunity was scarce. The weather rarely permitted walks where privacy could be secured, and their evening gatherings at the park or cottage were orchestrated by Sir John and Lady Middleton for entertainment, not discourse. The hostess and her husband viewed such meetings as occasions for eating, drinking, laughter, and noisy games like cards or "Consequences," leaving no room for private conversation.

Two such gatherings had passed without success until one morning Sir John visited the cottage, requesting that the entire party dine with Lady Middleton at the Park. Citing his obligation to attend the club in Exeter, he noted that Lady Middleton would otherwise face solitude, save for her mother and the Miss Steeles. Elinor recognized this as a strategic opening. A mixed company, guided by the tranquil presence of Lady Middleton rather than her husband's boisterous direction, offered a safer environment for private talk. Accepting the invitation, along with Margaret (with maternal permission) and a reluctant Marianne (persuaded by her mother's inability to tolerate seclusion), the group departed.

Lady Middleton was relieved of her anticipated loneliness, but the gathering proved as insipid as Elinor predicted. The conversation lacked novelty, offering nothing of interest in the dining room or drawing room. Children trailed behind them, rendering the drawing room inaccessible for any serious discussion. Only upon removing the tea things did they retreat to the card table. Elinor wondered at her own optimism regarding the possibility of conversation amidst the park's distractions. Everyone rose for a game of Casino.

"I am glad," Lady Middleton remarked to Lucy, "that you are not finishing poor little Annamaria's basket tonight. Working filigree by candlelight must strain your eyes. We shall make amends to the dear little angel tomorrow, and I hope she will not mind the delay."

This cue was sufficient for Lucy to recall her duties instantly. "Indeed, you are greatly mistaken, Lady Middleton," Lucy replied promptly. "I am merely awaiting confirmation of whether you can manage without me, for I would not disappoint the little angel for all the world. If you require me at the card table, I intend to complete the basket after supper."

"You are very kind. I hope it does not harm your eyes. Will you ring for some working candles? My poor little girl would be terribly disappointed if the basket remains unfinished tomorrow, especially since I promised it would be done."

Lucy immediately moved her worktable closer and resumed her seat with alacrity, her cheerful demeanor suggesting that crafting a filigree basket for a spoiled child brought her no lesser delight than any other pursuit. Lady Middleton then proposed a rubber of Casino. All objected except Marianne, who, disregarding the conventions of general civility, exclaimed, "Your Ladyship will excuse me—I detest cards. I shall retire to the piano-forte; I have not touched it since it was tuned." Without further ceremony, she turned and walked to the instrument. Lady Middleton looked visibly relieved that she had never uttered such a blunt remark.

"Marianne can never stay away from that instrument for long, ma'am," Elinor interjected, smoothing over the offense. "Nor do I wonder; it is the finest-toned piano-forte I have ever heard."

With the remaining five drawing their cards, Elinor seized the moment. "Perhaps," she continued, "if I should cut out, I could assist Miss Lucy Steele in rolling her papers. There is still so much work remaining on the basket that it seems impossible for her to finish it alone this evening. I should be delighted to contribute if she permits me a share."

"Indeed, I shall be immensely obliged for your help," Lucy cried. "I find there is more to do than I anticipated, and it would be dreadful to disappoint dear Annamaria."

"Oh! That would be terrible," Miss Steele agreed. "Dear little soul, how I adore her!"

"You are very kind," Lady Middleton said to Elinor. "Since you enjoy the work, perhaps you would prefer not to cut in until the next rubber, or shall you take your chance now?"

Elinor joyfully accepted the first proposal. Through a touch of address that Marianne could never muster, she achieved her goal while pleasing Lady Middleton. Lucy made room for her with ready attention, and the two rivals sat side by side at the same table, harmoniously collaborating on the same task.

Nearby, the piano-forte played on. Marianne, lost in her own music and reverie, had forgotten everyone else's existence. Seizing the cover of the instrument's noise, Elinor judged it safe to introduce the subject of her concern without risking eavesdropping at the card table.

"I feel unworthy of the confidence you've placed in me," Elinor began, her voice steady but measured, "if I possess no desire to sustain it, nor any further curiosity regarding its source. Therefore, I offer no apology for raising this matter anew."

"Thank you!" Lucy cried, her warmth genuine. "You've broken the ice; you've set my heart at ease. I was terrified I'd offended you back on Monday."

"Offend me?" Elinor replied with absolute sincerity. "Could you possibly imagine such an intent? To suggest I hold a grudge against you would be absurd. What motive could you have for trusting me that wasn't honorable and flattering?"

"And yet," Lucy countered, her sharp eyes narrowing with a mixture of defiance and vulnerability, "your manner seemed cold, displeased. It made me uneasy. I've been tormenting myself ever since, blaming myself for the liberty of troubling you with my affairs. But I'm relieved to know it was merely my own insecurity. Knowing you don't blame me... knowing you listen to what occupies my thoughts every moment of my life... it is a profound consolation."

"It was a relief to you," Elinor acknowledged, "to confess your situation and receive assurance that you'll never regret it. Your position is precarious; you're surrounded by obstacles. You'll require every ounce of mutual affection to endure them. Mr. Ferrars relies entirely on his mother."

"He possesses only two thousand pounds of his own," Lucy continued, her voice dropping slightly. "It would be madness to marry on that sum alone. Yet, I could surrender every prospect of wealth without a sigh. I've grown accustomed to a meager income; I could struggle through poverty for him. But I love him too dearly to be the instrument of his deprivation, robbing him of whatever his mother might bestow if he marries solely to please her. We must wait. Perhaps for years. For most men, this would be an alarming prospect, but Edward's affection and constancy are invincible. I know that."

"That conviction is everything to you," Elinor observed carefully, shielding her face from any hint of skepticism. "And he shares that trust in yours. Had your reciprocal attachment faltered—as it often does between lovers separated by four years—their situation would indeed be pitiable."

Lucy looked up, hopeful. "Edward's love has faced rigorous testing. Our prolonged separation since our engagement has been severe, yet it has held firm. I would be unpardonable to doubt it now. He has never given me cause for alarm."

Elinor suppressed a sigh. "You claim to be jealous by nature," she noted, "and given his immersion in society versus your seclusion, plus our constant distance, you were prone to suspicion. Any slight change in his demeanor, any drop in spirits unaccounted for, any excessive attention paid to another lady, or any visible unhappiness at Longstaple would have revealed the truth instantly. You insist you couldn't be deceived."

"All this," Elinor thought, *is charming rhetoric, but it deceives neither of us.*

"So," she asked aloud after a pause, "what are your intentions? Are you resigned to waiting for Mrs. Ferrars's death—a bleak and shocking ultimatum—or is her son prepared to submit to the tedious years of suspense rather than risk her wrath by revealing the truth?"

"If we could be certain it would only be temporary! But Mrs. Ferrars is headstrong and proud. Her initial fury would likely secure everything for Robert. The mere thought of that, for Edward's sake, extinguishes any inclination toward rash action."

"And for your own sake, too," Elinor added gently. "Or are you pushing your altruism beyond reason?"

Lucy fell silent, staring at Elinor. "Do you know Mr. Robert Ferrars?"

"Not at all. Never saw him. But I suspect he's unlike his brother—silly and a great coxcomb."

"A great coxcomb!" Miss Steele interjected, her ear catching the phrase amidst a lull in Marianne's piano playing. "Oh, they're discussing their favorite beaux, surely."

"No, sister," Lucy corrected sharply. "Our favorite beaux are *not* great coxcombs."

"I can vouch for it," Mrs. Jennings laughed heartily. "Miss Dashwood's beau is one of the most modest, well-behaved young men I've ever encountered. As for Lucy, she's such a sly little creature; it's impossible to guess whom she favors."

"Oh," Miss Steele said, casting a significant glance around the room, "I daresay Lucy's beau is equally modest and well-behaved as Miss Dashwood's."

Elinor flushed involuntarily. Lucy bit her lip, glaring at her sister. A heavy silence descended upon the group until Lucy broke it in a lowered tone, mindful of Marianne's powerful concerto.

"Honestly, I've devised a scheme recently to bring matters to a resolution. I must share this secret, for you are a party concerned. Given your acquaintance with Edward, you know he prefers the clergy to any other profession. My plan is for him to take holy orders as soon as possible. Then, through your influence—which I trust you'd exercise out of friendship for him, and perhaps some regard for me—your brother might be persuaded to grant him the Norland rectory. I understand it's a lucrative living, and the current incumbent is unlikely to survive long. This would provide sufficient income for us to marry, leaving time and chance to handle the rest."

"I would always be happy," Elinor replied, "to demonstrate my esteem for Mr. Ferrars. However, do you not realize my influence would be superfluous? He is the brother of Mrs. John Dashwood—that connection alone is ample recommendation to her husband."

"But Mrs. John Dashwood would strongly oppose Edward entering the ministry."

"Then I suspect my intervention would achieve little."

Another long silence ensued. Finally, Lucy sighed deeply. "I believe the wisest course is to dissolve the engagement immediately. We are besieged by difficulties on every side. While it would render us miserable temporarily, we might ultimately be happier. But you will not offer your counsel, Miss Dashwood?"

"No," Elinor smiled, masking her agitation. "On such a subject, I certainly will not. You know well that my opinion holds no weight unless it aligns with your desires."

"You wrong me," Lucy declared with grave solemnity. "I hold no one's judgment in higher esteem than yours. If you were to say, 'I advise you by all means to terminate your engagement with Edward Ferrars; it will serve both your happiness,' I would resolve to act immediately."

Elinor blushed at the insincerity of the future bride-to-be. "Such a compliment would deter me from offering any opinion had I formed one. It elevates my influence far too high; the power to sever two souls so tenderly bound is beyond the reach of an indifferent observer."

"'Tis precisely because you are an indifferent person," Lucy retorted with pique, emphasizing the word, "that your judgment carries such weight. If you were biased by personal feelings, your opinion would be worthless."

Elinor deemed it wiser to remain silent, fearing they might escalate into an unsuitable display of intimacy. She resolved never to revisit the topic. Thus, another lengthy pause followed, and Lucy ended it first.

"Will you be in town this winter, Miss Dashwood?" she asked with practiced cheerfulness.

"Certainly not."

"I'm sorry," the other replied, her eyes lighting up. "It would have brought me such joy to meet you there! But I daresay you'll go anyway. After all, your brother and sister will invite you."

"It won't be within my power to accept their invitation if they do."

"How unfortunate!" Lucy lamented. "I relied on meeting you there. Anne and I are traveling late January to visit relations who've urged us to come for years! But I go solely to see Edward. He'll be there in February; otherwise, London holds no charm for me. I lack the spirit for it."

Soon, Elinor was summoned to the card table by the conclusion of the first rubber, ending the intimate discourse. Both women submitted to the interruption without reluctance; nothing had been said to diminish their previous cordiality. Elinor seated herself at the cards with a melancholy certainty: Edward possessed no affection for the woman destined to be his wife, and lacked even the chance of marital happiness that sincere affection on her part might have secured. Self-interest alone drove Lucy to maintain an engagement she knew he found exhausting.

From that point forward, Elinor never revived the subject. When Lucy inevitably reintroduced it, eager to report every letter from Edward, Elinor responded with calm detachment, dismissing the conversation as soon as civility permitted. She viewed such talks as an indulgence Lucy did not deserve and dangerous to herself.

The Steeles' visit to Barton Park extended far beyond the initial invitation. Their favor grew; they became indispensable. Sir John refused to entertain the idea of their departure. Despite their numerous, long-standing engagements in Exeter and the pressing necessity to return weekly, they were prevailed upon to stay nearly two months, participating enthusiastically in the grand festivities requiring private balls and lavish dinners to proclaim their significance.

January brought a shift in the atmospheric pressure of Barton Cottage, mirroring the internal climate of its inhabitants. Mrs. Jennings, though accustomed to drifting between the homes of her children and friends, maintained a permanent anchor in a residence near Portman Square. As the frost thickened, she directed her thoughts toward this haven and, with sudden decisiveness, summoned the elder Misses Dashwood to join her.

Elinor, failing to register the storm clouds gathering in Marianne's expression—the animated intensity that betrayed a fervent desire to comply—immediately issued a polite yet absolute refusal. She framed it as a unified maternal duty: they could not abandon their mother during the coldest season. Mrs. Jennings, taken aback, doubled down with characteristic vigor. "Oh, Lord!" she exclaimed, her voice rising in pitch. "Your mother can spare you in a heartbeat! I beg you to grant me this favor; I have set my heart upon it. Do not imagine you will be a burden. I shall send Betty in the coach—I trust my purse can handle that—and we three shall travel comfortably in my chaise. Should you tire of following my lead in town, simply tag along with one of my daughters. I am confident your mother will agree; after all, I have successfully dispatched my own offspring, making me the ideal custodian for yours. Unless I manage to marry one of you before I'm done, it won't be my fault. Rest assured, I shall advocate strongly for you among the eligible youth."

Sir John, sensing an opening, interjected with a conspiratorial whisper. "I suspect Miss Marianne would welcome such a scheme if her elder sister would join. It is a tragedy that she is denied pleasure solely due to Miss Dashwood's caution. I urge you both to depart for London once you've grown weary of Barton, keeping Miss Dashwood entirely in the dark."

"Nonsense," Mrs. Jennings retorted, clapping her hands. "I shall be ecstatic with Miss Marianne's company regardless of Elinor's presence. The more the merrier! Besides, it is far more comforting for them to remain together; if they grow bored of me, they can confide in one another and mock my eccentricities behind my back. But one or both must come. Good heavens, how can I endure another winter poking about alone, deprived of Charlotte? Come, Miss Marianne, seal the bargain with a handshake. If Miss Dashwood changes her mind later, so much the better."

Marianne responded with radiant warmth. "Thank you, madam, sincerely. Your invitation secures my gratitude forever. To accept would bring me nearly the greatest happiness I possess. Yet, my mother—my dearest, kindest mother—I acknowledge the validity of Elinor's plea. Were her peace compromised by our absence... Oh, no! Nothing could tempt me to leave her. It must not be a struggle."

Mrs. Jennings reiterated her assurance that Mrs. Dashwood could spare them effortlessly. Elinor, now fully comprehending her sister's state—a mind consumed by the desperate longing to reunite with Willoughby—ceased her direct opposition. Instead, she deferred the matter to their mother, anticipating no support for her efforts to block a journey she deemed detrimental to Marianne's sensibilities and personally inconvenient for herself. She knew Mrs. Dashwood would champion any endeavor Marianne desired, unable to instill caution regarding an affair where she had never learned to trust. Furthermore, Elinor dared not reveal her own aversion to London, knowing that Marianne, despite her fastidious nature, would overlook every indignity of Mrs. Jennings' company in pursuit of this singular goal. Such blindness to social friction proved the overwhelming power of Marianne's obsession, a phenomenon Elinor found impossible to reconcile with her sister's usual refinement.

When informed of the invitation, Mrs. Dashwood, convinced the trip promised immense amusement and perceiving the depth of Marianne's yearning through her daughter's distracted gaze, refused to entertain the idea of decline. "We shall go," she declared cheerfully. "Margaret and I shall benefit equally. In your absence, we shall retreat into our books and music, living quietly and happily. Upon your return, you will find Margaret transformed! I have alterations planned for your bedrooms that can now proceed without disruption. It is imperative that women of your station acquaint themselves with the manners and amusements of London. You will be under the care of a motherly, kind-hearted woman, and you will undoubtedly encounter your brother. Despite his faults—or those of his wife, considering his lineage—I cannot bear the thought of you remaining estranged from him."

"Though you act with your customary zeal for our happiness," Elinor countered gently, "there remains an objection I deem insurmountable." Marianne's face fell instantly. "And what," Mrs. Dashwood asked, feigning innocence, "does my prudent Elinor propose? Is it the cost?"

"My concern is this," Elinor stated firmly. "While I hold Mrs. Jennings' heart in high regard, her society offers neither genuine pleasure nor social consequence."

"That is true," her mother conceded, "yet you will rarely interact with her in isolation; you will almost invariably appear alongside Lady Middleton."

"If Elinor is deterred by her distaste for Mrs. Jennings," Marianne interjected, her voice sharp with defiance, "it need not hinder *my* acceptance. I harbor no such scruples and could endure every unpleasantness with minimal effort."

Elinor suppressed a smile at this display of indifference toward the social graces she had tirelessly cultivated in Marianne. Resolving internally that she could not permit her sister to navigate London's complexities alone, nor allow Mrs. Jennings to dominate Marianne's domestic hours unchecked, she decided to accompany them. This resolve was bolstered by the knowledge that Edward Ferrars, according to Lucy Steele, would not arrive in town until February, allowing ample time to conclude their visit early.

"I insist you *both* go," Mrs. Dashwood announced, dismissing Elinor's reservations as nonsense. "London awaits, and the prospect of your reunion promises delight. Perhaps Elinor, if she could anticipate enjoyment, would foresee pleasures in expanding her acquaintance with her sister-in-law's family."

Elinor, wishing to soften her mother's reliance on the Ferrars connection before the inevitable revelation of Lucy's deception, seized the moment. "I admire Edward Ferrars greatly and shall always welcome seeing him," she said calmly. "However, regarding the rest of the family, it is a matter of perfect indifference to me whether I am ever introduced to them."

Mrs. Dashwood smiled enigmatically, offering no comment. Marianne gazed upward in astonishment, realizing too late that Elinor had spoken her mind. After further discussion, the decision was finalized: the invitation was accepted.

News of the departure filled Mrs. Jennings with jubilation, accompanied by numerous pledges of kindness. Sir John was equally delighted; for a man terrified of solitude, acquiring two new companions in London was a triumph. Even Lady Middleton managed a performance of delight, stretching her social muscles to accommodate the occasion. The Miss Steeles, particularly Lucy, experienced a euphoria unknown to them, their spirits soaring at the intelligence.

Elinor submitted to the arrangement with less resistance than anticipated. Regarding her own attendance, she felt a profound unconcern. Observing her mother's evident satisfaction and Marianne's exuberant restoration to her former animation—elevated to a gaiety surpassing her usual baseline—Elinor could not fault the outcome. Marianne's joy bordered on manic agitation; her only tether to composure was the impending separation from her mother, a thought that induced excessive grief at the moment of parting. Mrs. Dashwood shared this sorrow, while Elinor remained the sole observer who viewed the separation as anything less than eternal.

The party departed in the first week of January. The Middletons followed shortly thereafter, while the Miss Steeles remained stationed at the park, intending to leave only upon the family's return.

Elinor could not reconcile the image of herself seated beside Mrs. Jennings in a carriage, embarking on a journey to London as her guest, with the reality of their acquaintance. Their association had been brief, their dispositions diametrically opposed, and only days prior had Elinor voiced strenuous objections to such a measure. Yet, driven by the same youthful ardor that animated Marianne and her mother, those objections had been swept aside. Even as doubts flickered in her mind regarding Willoughby's constancy, Elinor watched the radiant anticipation filling Marianne's soul and beaming from her eyes. In stark contrast, Elinor's own prospects appeared bleak and cheerless. She envied Marianne's animating hope, wishing fervently to possess the same object of desire, the same possibility of triumph.

Time, however, was a sharp-edged instrument; a mere handful of days would reveal Willoughby's true intentions. Given Marianne's eagerness to depart, it was evident she believed him already in town. Elinor resolved to gather every scrap of intelligence regarding his character, observing his behavior toward her sister with relentless scrutiny to determine his nature and motives before further meetings occurred. Should her findings prove unfavorable, she intended to awaken her sister to the truth; should they prove favorable, she would strive to suppress all selfish comparisons and banish every regret that might dim her joy in Marianne's happiness.

Three days passed on the road. Marianne's conduct during the travel served as a testament to her future compliance and companionship with Mrs. Jennings. She remained silent, lost in meditation, speaking only when a picturesque vista elicited an exclamatory delight addressed solely to her sister. To compensate for this reserve, Elinor assumed the role of civility, attending meticulously to Mrs. Jennings, conversing, laughing, and listening whenever opportunity arose. Mrs. Jennings, in turn, showered them with kindness, anxious for their comfort, save for her frustration that she could not dictate their meals or extract a preference for salmon over cod.

They arrived in London at three o'clock on the third day, relieved to escape the carriage's confinement and eager for the luxury of a roaring fire. The house was elegant, furnished with taste, and the sisters were installed in a spacious apartment. Above the mantelpiece hung a landscape painted in colored silks—a relic of Charlotte's seven-year tenure at a prestigious academy. Dinner was not due for two hours, prompting Elinor to sit and compose a letter to her mother. Moments later, Marianne joined her.

"I am writing home, Marianne," Elinor stated. "Had you not better defer your correspondence for a day or two?"

"I am *not* going to write to my mother," Marianne replied sharply, clearly intent on avoiding further inquiry.

Elinor said nothing. Instantly, the deduction crystallized: Marianne was writing to Willoughby. The conclusion, though tinged with uncertainty, brought a surge of pleasure to Elinor, who resumed her letter with renewed vigor. Marianne's note was dispatched in minutes; it was brief, folded, sealed, and directed with frantic speed. Elinor discerned a prominent 'W' in the address. Before the task was complete, Marianne rang the bell, instructing the footman to deliver the letter via the two-penny post. The evidence was irrefutable.

Marianne's spirits soared, yet a palpable flutter agitated her, failing to bring genuine joy to her sister. This agitation intensified as evening fell. She barely touched her dinner and, upon returning to the drawing-room, seemed to strain her ears against the sound of every approaching carriage. Elinor found solace in Mrs. Jennings's absorption in her own room, which shielded her from witnessing the scene. When tea was served, Marianne had already been thwarted by several knocks at neighboring doors until a resounding rap echoed unmistakably from their own threshold. Elinor felt certain it signaled Willoughby's arrival. Marianne sprang up and hastened toward the door.

Silence reigned. Unable to endure it, she opened the door and stepped onto the landing, listening intently for half a minute before retreating into the room, trembling with the conviction that she had heard him. Overcome by ecstatic emotion, she exclaimed, "Oh, Elinor, it is Willoughby, indeed it is!" poised to throw herself into his embrace, when Colonel Brandon appeared.

The shock was too profound to withstand calmly, and Marianne fled the room. Elinor felt a pang of disappointment, yet her respect for Colonel Brandon ensured his welcome. She was acutely wounded that a man so fond of her sister perceived only her grief and despair in his presence. She realized immediately that he had noticed Marianne's departure, marked by such astonishment and concern that he momentarily forgot the dictates of civility toward herself.

"Is your sister ill?" he asked.

Elinor answered with distress, attributing Marianne's behavior to headaches, low spirits, and exhaustion, listing plausible causes for her erratic conduct. He listened with earnest attention but, seemingly recalling himself, offered no further comment on the subject, shifting instead to express his pleasure at seeing them in London, asking routine questions about their journey and departed acquaintances. In this subdued manner, lacking mutual interest, they conversed, both listless and distracted.

Elinor desperately wished to inquire if Willoughby was in town but feared causing him pain by questioning his rival. Finally, seeking conversation, she asked if he had been in London since their last meeting.

"Yes," he replied, with visible embarrassment, "almost ever since. I visited Delaford once or twice for a few days, but it has never been in my power to return to Barton."

This response, delivered with such hesitation, resurrected the memory of his abrupt departure from that estate and the unease it had caused Mrs. Jennings. Elinor feared her question had betrayed a curiosity far exceeding her actual feelings.

Mrs. Jennings soon entered. "Oh! Colonel," she cried with her customary boisterous cheerfulness, "I am monstrous glad to see you—sorry I could not come before—beg your pardon, but I have been forced to look about me and settle my affairs. It has been ages since I was at home, and you know one always has a world of little oddities to attend to after an absence. Besides, I have had Cartwright to settle with. Lord, I have been as busy as a bee ever since dinner! But pray, Colonel, how came you to conjure out that I should be in town today?"

"I had the pleasure of hearing it at Mr. Palmer's, where I have been dining."

"Oh, you did; well, and how do they all do at their house? How does Charlotte do? I warrant you she is a fine size by this time."

"Mrs. Palmer appeared quite well, and I am commissioned to tell you that you will certainly see her tomorrow."

"Ay, to be sure, I thought as much. Well, Colonel, I have brought two young ladies with me—you see, that is, you see but one of them now, but there is another somewhere. Your friend, Miss Marianne, too—which you will not be sorry to hear. I do not know what you and Mr. Willoughby will do between you about her. Ay, it is a fine thing to be young and handsome. Well! I was young once, but I never was very handsome—worse luck for me. However, I got a very good husband, and I don't know what the greatest beauty can do more. Ah! poor man! he has been dead these eight years and better. But Colonel, where have you been to since we parted? And how does your business go on? Come, come, let's have no secrets among friends."

He responded to her inquiries with his accustomed mildness, offering no satisfying answers. Elinor then prepared the tea, forcing Marianne to reappear. Upon her entrance, Colonel Brandon grew more thoughtful and silent than before, and Mrs. Jennings could not persuade him to remain long. No other visitors arrived that evening, and the ladies unanimously agreed to retire early.

Marianne rose the next morning with restored spirits and a joyful demeanor. The previous evening's disappointment seemed erased by the anticipation of the day's events. Hardly had they finished breakfast when Mrs. Palmer's barouche halted at the door. Soon she burst into the room, laughing, so delighted to see them that it was difficult to discern whether she derived more pleasure from meeting her mother or the Miss Dashwoods. She was surprised by their visit to town, though she had suspected it; she was angered that they accepted her mother's invitation after declining her own, yet she claimed she could never forgive them had they not come.

"Mr. Palmer will be so happy to see you," she said. "What do you think he said when he heard of your coming with Mama? I forget what it was now, but it was something so droll!"

After an hour of what her mother termed "comfortable chat"—essentially a barrage of inquiries into Mrs. Jennings's circle punctuated by baseless laughter on Mrs. Palmer's part—the latter proposed visiting some shops where she had business. Mrs. Jennings and Elinor readily agreed, having their own purchases to make. Marianne, initially reluctant, was persuaded to join them.

Throughout their outing, she remained constantly vigilant. In Bond Street, her eyes scanned the crowd in perpetual search. In every shop, her mind drifted from the items before them, fixated on something else entirely. Restless and dissatisfied everywhere, Elinor could never elicit her sister's opinion on any purchase, regardless of its relevance. Marianne derived

"If this open weather persists," Mrs. Jennings remarked as they gathered for breakfast the following morning, "Sir John will be displeased to depart from Barton next week. It is a grievous loss for men of sport to forfeit a single day of pleasure. Poor fellows! I always feel a pang of sympathy for them; they seem to carry the disappointment so heavily."

"That is precisely the case," Marianne exclaimed, her voice buoyant. She moved toward the window to inspect the day as she spoke. "I hadn't considered *that* angle. This fine weather will keep countless sportsmen entrenched in the countryside." It was a fortunate realization; her entire demeanor brightened instantly. "It is truly ideal for *them*," she continued, settling into her chair with a radiant expression. "One can only imagine the sheer delight they derive from it. However" (a flicker of anxiety crossed her features), "such conditions cannot endure indefinitely. Given the season and the preceding deluge, we shall undoubtedly see a sharp decline in precipitation. Frosts are imminent, likely severe. Perhaps within a day or two; this unnatural mildness is unsustainable—nay, it may freeze tonight!"

"At any rate," Elinor interjected, keen to obscure her sister's acute foresight from Mrs. Jennings' gaze, "I daresay Sir John and Lady Middleton will be back in town by the end of next week."

"Ay, my dear, I warrant it. Mary always gets her own way."

"And now," Elinor observed internally, "she will dispatch a letter to Combe via today's post." Whether she did or not, the missive was penned and dispatched with a discretion that evaded all of Elinor's surveillance. Regardless of the truth, and though Elinor harbored no profound contentment regarding the matter, she remained composed so long as Marianne displayed high spirits. Marianne was spirited; elated by the mildness of the air and even more so by the prospect of a frost. The morning was consumed with distributing visiting cards to acquaintances of Mrs. Jennings to announce their presence in London. Throughout the process, Marianne remained fixated on meteorological indicators, scrutinizing wind direction, cloud formations, and atmospheric shifts, constructing elaborate scenarios of climatic alteration.

"Do you not perceive it is colder than this morning, Elinor?" Marianne asked. "There appears to be a distinct shift. I struggle to keep my hands warm even within my muff. Yesterday was different, I believe. The clouds seem to be dispersing; the sun will emerge momentarily, promising a clear afternoon."

Elinor felt a mixture of diversion and pain. Marianne persisted, interpreting every flicker of flame in the hearth and every nuance of the morning sky as definitive proof of an impending frost. The Miss Dashwoods possessed no greater cause for dissatisfaction with Mrs. Jennings' domestic arrangements or circle of associates than with her treatment of them, which remained consistently benevolent. Every aspect of her household management operated on a generous scale. Aside from a select group of elderly city friends whom, to Lady Middleton's regret, she had never severed ties with, she avoided social engagements that might unsettle her young charges. Delighted to find herself in a more comfortable position than anticipated, Elinor was willing to trade the lack of genuine entertainment provided by their evening gatherings—which, whether hosted at home or elsewhere, devolved into mere card games—with the security of her current station. Such affairs offered little amusement.

Colonel Brandon, holding a general invitation, visited almost daily. He came ostensibly to admire Marianne and converse with Elinor. While Elinor often derived greater satisfaction from his company than from any other daily occurrence, she viewed with deep concern his unwavering attachment to her sister. She feared it was intensifying. It pained her to witness the earnestness with which he observed Marianne; his spirits were notably lower than they had been at Barton.

Approximately a week after their arrival, it became confirmed that Willoughby had also reached London. His calling card rested on the table when they returned from their morning drive.

"Good heavens!" Marianne cried. "He has been here while we were out."

Elinor, relieved to confirm his presence in London, ventured cautiously, "Be assured, he will call again tomorrow."

Marianne seemed barely to register her words. Upon Mrs. Jennings' entry, Marianne snatched the card and fled the room. This incident, while lifting Elinor's spirits, ignited in her sister a renewed, overwhelming agitation. From this moment, her mind knew no peace; the anticipation of encountering him hourly rendered her incapable of focusing on anything else. She insisted on remaining behind the next morning while the others departed. Elinor's thoughts raced with possibilities unfolding in Berkeley Street, yet a fleeting glance at her sister upon their return sufficed to reveal that Willoughby had paid no second visit.

Just then, a note was delivered and placed on the table.

"For me!" Marianne shouted, hastening forward.

"No, madam, for my mistress."

Unconvinced, Marianne seized the envelope immediately. "It is indeed addressed to Mrs. Jennings; how vexatious!"

"You are expecting a letter, then?" Elinor asked, unable to remain silent further.

"Yes, a little—not much." After a brief pause, she added, "You harbor no confidence in me, Marianne."

"Nay, Elinor, this rebuke from *you*—from one who trusts no one!"

"Me!" Elinor replied, flustered. "Indeed, Marianne, I have nothing to disclose."

"Nor I," Marianne answered with vigor. "Our predicaments are identical. Neither of us possesses anything to share; you because you refuse to communicate, and I because I conceal nothing."

Distressed by this accusation of reserve—a trait she could not legitimately deny—Elinor struggled to urge greater candor from her sister. Soon, Mrs. Jennings appeared, and the note was handed to her. She read it aloud. It was from Lady Middleton, informing them of their arrival in Conduit Street the previous night and inviting her mother and cousins to join them the following evening. Business obligations for Sir John and a severe illness for Lady Middleton had prevented them from visiting Berkeley Street. The invitation was accepted.

However, as the appointed hour approached, Elinor encountered significant difficulty persuading her sister to attend. Though civility demanded their joint presence, Marianne, having seen nothing of Willoughby, was disinclined toward external amusement and reluctant to risk his appearing again in her absence.

Upon the conclusion of the evening, Elinor realized that relocation does not fundamentally alter human nature. Although scarcely established in town, Sir John had managed to assemble nearly twenty youths and entertain them with a ball. This was an affair Lady Middleton disapproved of. In the countryside, an impromptu dance was acceptable; in London, where the reputation for elegance was paramount and fragile, it was reckless to allow it to be known that Lady Middleton had hosted a modest gathering of eight or nine couples, accompanied by two violins and a meager sideboard repast.

Mr. and Mrs. Palmer attended. Having not seen them since their arrival, and mindful of avoiding any appearance of undue familiarity with his mother-in-law, Mr. Palmer kept his distance. On their entrance, he cast a slight, unreadable glance their way, seemingly unaware of their identities, offering only a nod to Mrs. Jennings from across the room.

Marianne scanned the apartment upon entering. It was sufficient—he was not there. She seated herself, equally devoid of desire to receive or impart pleasure.

After approximately an hour, Mr. Palmer approached the Miss Dashwoods to express his surprise at finding them in town, noting that Colonel Brandon had been informed of their arrival at his residence and had quipped amusingly upon learning of their visit.

"I presumed you were both still in Devonshire," he said.

"Did you?" Elinor replied.

"When do you intend to return?"

"I do not know." Thus concluded their exchange.

Never in her life had Marianne been so reluctant to dance, nor so exhausted by the exertion. She complained bitterly upon returning to Berkeley Street.

"Aye, aye," Mrs. Jennings conceded. "We understand the cause perfectly. Had a certain individual, whose name shall remain unnamed, been present, you would not have felt a fraction of that fatigue. Truthfully, it was not very becoming of him to fail to meet you when invited."

"Invited!" Marianne shrieked. "My daughter Middleton informed me of it, for it appears Sir John encountered him somewhere in the street this morning."

Marianne said no more, but her countenance betrayed profound hurt.

Impatient to undertake some action that might alleviate her sister's distress, Elinor resolved to write the following morning to her mother. Hoping to awaken maternal fears for Marianne's health and thereby secure the long-delayed inquiries, she was driven even more urgently to this course by observing, after breakfast the next day, that Marianne was once again composing a letter to Willoughby. She could not conceive it was addressed to anyone else.

Around midday, Mrs. Jennings departed alone on business, allowing Elinor to begin her letter immediately. Marianne, too restless for occupation and too anxious for conversation, paced between windows or sat by the fire in somber contemplation. Elinor wrote earnestly to her mother, detailing recent events, voicing her suspicions of Willoughby's infidelity, and pleading with every argument of duty and affection for her mother to demand an account of Marianne's true situation regarding him.

Her letter was scarcely finished when a knock signaled a visitor, and Colonel

Nothing occurred during the next three or four days to make Elinor regret her decision to consult her mother; Willoughby neither appeared nor sent word. By the conclusion of this period, the sisters were engaged to accompany Lady Middleton to a gathering, an event from which Mrs. Jennings was excused due to the indisposition of her youngest daughter. For this occasion, Marianne, utterly dispirited and indifferent to her own presentation, prepared with a demeanor suggesting equal apathy toward attendance or absence. Following tea, she sat by the drawing-room fire until Lady Middleton's arrival, remaining motionless and lost in internal turmoil, oblivious to her sister's proximity. Only when summoned to the threshold did she start as though forgetting the expectation entirely.

Upon reaching the destination, they alighted from the carriages as the line permitted, ascended the stairs, and heard their names announced audibly from landing to landing before entering a hall brilliantly illuminated and packed with guests. The atmosphere was stiflingly hot. Having paid their obligatory courtesy to the hostess, they mingled in the throng, absorbing the discomfort their presence inevitably exacerbated. After a brief interval of minimal interaction, Lady Middleton seated herself at a game of Cassino. Since Marianne lacked the spirit to move, Elinor and her sister secured seats nearby. Long had they not occupied these positions before Elinor spotted Willoughby, standing mere yards away in animated discussion with a strikingly fashionable young woman. He quickly caught her gaze, offering a bow without attempting speech or approaching Marianne, though he clearly saw her, and resumed his discourse. Elinor instinctively glanced at Marianne to gauge her reaction. It was then that Marianne first perceived him; her entire countenance flushed with sudden joy, and she would have surged toward him had her sister not seized her arm.

"Good heavens!" Marianne cried, "He is there—he is there! Why does he not look at me? Why cannot I speak to him?"

"Compose yourself," Elinor urged urgently, "and do not broadcast your emotions to every guest. Perhaps he has not yet registered your presence."

This reassurance was futile; Marianne could neither believe it nor achieve composure. She sat in an agony of impatience that distorted every feature. Finally, Willoughby turned and regarded them both. Marianne sprang up, invoking his name with affectionate emphasis, and extended her hand. He approached, directing his words primarily to Elinor as if deliberately avoiding Marianne's gaze, determined not to acknowledge her posture, and inquired hurriedly after Mrs. Dashwood and the duration of their stay in town. Elinor, stripped of all presence of mind by such a direct address, remained speechless. Yet her sister's feelings erupted instantly. Her face burned crimson, and in a voice trembling with profound emotion, she demanded, "Good God! Willoughby, what is the meaning of this? Have you not received my letters? Will you not shake hands with me?"

He could no longer evade contact, yet her touch seemed painful to him; he held her hand for only a fleeting moment. Throughout this exchange, he visibly struggled to regain control. Elinor observed his features and noted a gradual shift toward tranquility. After a brief pause, he spoke calmly. "I took the liberty of calling in Berkeley Street last Tuesday and deeply regretted failing to find you and Mrs. Jennings at home. I trust my card was not misplaced."

"But have you not received my notes?" Marianne shrieked in wild anxiety. "Some error must exist—some terrible mistake. What is the meaning? Tell me, Willoughby; for heaven's sake, explain."

He offered no reply. His complexion altered, and all prior embarrassment resurfaced. However, upon catching the eye of the young lady with whom he had previously conversed, he felt compelled to exert himself anew. Recovering his composure, he stated, "Yes, I received the notification of your arrival in town, which you were so kind as to send," delivered a slight bow, and hastened away to join his companion.

Marianne, now pallid and unable to stand, sank back into her chair. Anticipating imminent fainting, Elinor attempted to shield her from public view while administering lavender water. "Go to him, Elinor," Marianne commanded as soon as she could speak. "Force him to come to me. I must see him again—I must speak to him instantly. I cannot rest; I shall find no peace until this is clarified. Some dreadful misunderstanding must be resolved. Go to him this moment."

"How can that be achieved?" Elinor countered gently. "No, my dearest Marianne, you must wait. This is not the setting for explanations. Wait until tomorrow."

With difficulty, she restrained Marianne from pursuing him personally. Persuading her sister to curb her agitation and maintain an appearance of composure until they could speak privately proved impossible; Marianne continued to succumb incessantly to her misery, emitting exclamations of wretchedness. Shortly thereafter, Elinor observed Willoughby exiting the room via the staircase. Informing Marianne of his departure, she argued that further communication was impossible that evening, using this as leverage to urge calm. Instantly, Marianne begged Elinor to plead with Lady Middleton to take them home, claiming she was too miserable to endure another minute. Though Lady Middleton was mid-game, she displayed sufficient politeness to acquiesce immediately, transferring her cards to a friend. They departed as soon as a carriage could be located.

Scarcely a word was exchanged during the return journey to Berkeley Street. Marianne existed in a silent agony, too overwhelmed even for tears. Fortunately, Mrs. Jennings had not yet returned, allowing the sisters to proceed directly to their private chambers, where hartshorn briefly restored Marianne's faculties. Undressed and tucked into bed, she expressed a desire for solitude. Elinor withdrew, leaving her sister to her grief while awaiting Mrs. Jennings' return, granting herself the rare opportunity to reflect on the past.

She could not doubt that a genuine engagement had once subsisted between Willoughby and Marianne, nor that Willoughby was now weary of it. Regardless of Marianne's lingering hopes, Elinor attributed such behavior solely to a fundamental change in sentiment, rejecting notions of mistake or misapprehension. Her indignation would have been far greater had she not witnessed the evident embarrassment that suggested a consciousness of his own misconduct, preventing her from believing him capable of playing with her sister's affections without ulterior motives. While absence might have dulled his regard and convenience prompted him to suppress it, the existence of such regard in the past remained undeniable to her.

Regarding Marianne, Elinor reflected with deepest sorrow on the acute pain of their current reunion and the even severer suffering that awaited them in its inevitable aftermath. In contrast, her own situation appeared more tenable; though separated from Edward in the future, she could continue to esteem him, finding support in her mind. Conversely, every circumstance seemed to conspire to intensify Marianne's misery, sealing her fate in a final, irreconcilable separation from Willoughby.
