The January morning arrived with a biting chill, the sky a slate of unyielding gray that offered no promise of warmth until the fire was kindled. Before the maid had even struck a spark, Marianne knelt by the window-seat, her back rigid against the cold stone, clutching a quill that moved only as fast as her tears allowed. She wrote to Willoughby, drafting a final plea in a state of frantic desperation.

Elinor, roused from sleep by the sharp, ragged sounds of her sister's distress, entered the room. Seeing Marianne's posture—the hunched shoulders, the trembling hand—she observed her for a moment with a silent, piercing anxiety before speaking in a tone calibrated to maximum consideration.

"Marianne," Elinor said softly, "may I ask—"

"No, Elinor," Marianne interrupted, her voice devoid of the usual lilt, stripped bare by exhaustion. "Ask nothing. You will know all soon enough."

A strange, brittle calmness held her speech for a heartbeat, a tactical pause before the dam broke. Immediately, the excessive affliction returned, washing over her in waves. Minutes passed before she could resume writing, her pen halting repeatedly as fresh torrents of grief threatened to drown her ink. Each interruption was proof of her conviction: this was likely the last correspondence she would ever send to him.

Elinor offered every ounce of quiet support she possessed, yet Marianne, driven by a nervous irritability that bordered on hysteria, pleaded with her not to speak a single word. In such a volatile state, proximity was dangerous; Marianne's mind was a storm that required constant movement. She wandered the house like a ghost, avoiding every eye, seeking solitude in every corner until breakfast.

At the table, Marianne consumed nothing. Elinor, realizing that pity or pressure would only exacerbate the situation, shifted her focus entirely to Mrs. Jennings. She became the sole anchor for the older woman's attention, deflecting any gaze toward her grieving daughter-in-law.

The meal dragged on, a customary ritual for Mrs. Jennings, until the servants cleared the table for the afternoon work. Just as they settled around the common table, a letter was delivered. Marianne snatched it from the servant's hand, her complexion draining to a deathly pallor. Without a sound, she fled the room.

Elinor watched her departure and understood instantly: the handwriting, the timing—it was from Willoughby. A sickness seized her own heart, rendering her dizzy and unable to lift her head. Her legs trembled violently, threatening to betray her weakness before Mrs. Jennings. However, the older lady, oblivious to the gravity of the moment, interpreted Marianne's flight as a reaction to a trivial jest. She chuckled, measuring lengths of worsted for her rug, and remarked cheerfully, "Upon my word, I never saw a young woman so desperately in love in my life! My girls were nothing to her, yet they were foolish enough to dream. But Miss Marianne? She is quite an altered creature. I hope, from the bottom of my heart, he won't keep her waiting much longer. It is grievous to see her so ill and forlorn. Pray, when are they to be married?"

Elinor, though drained of spirit, felt compelled to dismantle this delusion. Forcing a smile that didn't reach her eyes, she replied, "And have you really, Ma'am, convinced yourself that my sister is engaged to Mr. Willoughby? I thought it a mere joke, but such a serious inquiry implies more. I must beg you not to deceive yourself further. Nothing would surprise me more than to hear of their impending marriage."

"For shame!" Mrs. Jennings exclaimed, her laughter ringing out. "How can you talk so? Don't we all know it must be a match? They were over head and ears in love the moment they met. Did I not see them together in Devonshire every day? Did I not know your sister came to town specifically to buy wedding clothes? Come, come, this won't do. Because you are so secretive, you assume everyone lacks sense, but I can assure you, it has been whispered throughout the town for ages. I tell everyone, and so does Charlotte."

"Indeed, Ma'am," Elinor said, her voice grave. "You are mistaken. You are doing a very unkind thing by spreading this report, and I fear you will find yourself believing nothing of it."

Mrs. Jennings laughed again, dismissing Elinor's warning, while Elinor, lacking the strength for further debate, retreated to their chamber. Upon entering, she found Marianne collapsed on the bed, nearly suffocating under the weight of her grief. Several letters lay scattered beside her, and she clutched one tightly in her fist. Elinor approached silently, seated herself on the mattress, took Marianne's hand, and kissed it repeatedly. Then, overwhelmed, she broke into tears, a cascade of emotion that matched Marianne's intensity.

Silence fell between them, filled only by the shared agony. Eventually, Marianne handed over the bundle of letters, burying her face in her handkerchief as she screamed with raw pain. Elinor, recognizing that such devastation needed to run its course, waited until the initial paroxysm subsided. Only then did she turn to the letter from Willoughby.

"My Dear Madam," the letter began, the formal address a stark contrast to the cruelty within. "I have just had the honor of receiving your letter, for which I beg to return my sincere acknowledgments. I am much concerned to find there was anything in my behavior last night that did not meet your approbation; and though I am quite at a loss to discover in what point I could be so unfortunate as to offend you, I entreat your forgiveness of what I can assure you to have been perfectly unintentional. I shall never reflect on my former acquaintance with your family in Devonshire without the most grateful pleasure, and flatter myself it will not be broken by any mistake or misapprehension of my actions. My esteem for your whole family is very sincere; but if I have been so unfortunate as to give rise to a belief of more than I felt, or meant to express, I shall reproach myself for not having been more guarded in my professions of that esteem. That I should ever have meant more you will allow to be impossible, when you understand that my affections have been long engaged elsewhere, and it will not be many weeks, I believe, before this engagement is fulfilled. It is with great regret that I obey your commands in returning the letters with which I have been honored from you, and the lock of hair, which you so obligingly bestowed on me."

"I am, dear Madam, Your most obedient humble servant, JOHN WILLOUGHBY."

Elinor read the words, and the implications crashed upon her. Though she suspected the truth before opening the envelope, she had not anticipated such brazen indifference. This was not merely a rejection; it was a calculated dismantling of dignity. Willoughby had departed from every honorable sentiment, violating the basic decorum of a gentleman. Instead of offering regret or explaining his withdrawal, he reduced their entire history to a misunderstanding of his "esteem," casually discarding her letters and her hair as if they were worthless debris. Every line was an insult, proclaiming a man steeped in hardened villainy.

She paused, stunned by the audacity, reading it again and again. Each pass confirmed her abhorrence. The bitterness in her heart grew so fierce that she dared not speak, terrified that articulating the horror of his betrayal would wound Marianne further. She reframed the event internally: this was not a loss of potential joy, but an escape from the most irremediable evil—a lifetime tethered to a principless man. It was a deliverance, a blessing.

Lost in meditation on the depravity of the mind that dictated such a letter, and contrasting it with the purity of the person she truly cared for, Elinor forgot her sister's immediate torment. She forgot the three unread letters on Marianne's lap and the passage of time. When she finally heard a carriage rumble up to the door, she rushed to the window, astonished to see Mrs. Jennings's chariot arriving far earlier than scheduled.

Determined not to abandon Marianne, even in her own despair, Elinor excused herself from accompanying the older lady, citing her sister's indisposition. Mrs. Jennings, ever good-natured, accepted the excuse readily. Once the carriage departed, Elinor returned to the bedroom. She found Marianne attempting to rise, her movements unsteady. Elinor caught her just in time to prevent a fall onto the floor. Days of starvation and sleeplessness had taken their toll; now that the feverish suspense had broken, the physical consequences manifested as an aching head, a hollow stomach, and profound nervous faintness.

Elinor quickly fetched a glass of wine. As Marianne drank, her color returned slightly, allowing her to whisper, "Poor Elinor! How unhappy I make you!"

"I only wish," Elinor replied gently, "there were anything I *could* do to comfort you."

These words, simple and sincere, proved too much for Marianne. Overcome by anguish, she cried out, "Oh! Elinor, I am miserable, indeed," before her voice dissolved into sobs.

Elinor could no longer stand idly by. "Exert yourself, dear Marianne," she urged, her voice firm. "If you would not destroy yourself and those who love you, think of your mother. Think of her misery

Mrs. Jennings arrived at their chamber with the urgency of a soldier responding to a distress signal, bypassing the formalities of announcement to stride inside. Her expression was etched with genuine alarm.

"How do you do, my dear?" she asked, her voice modulated with a practiced, compassionate cadence directed at Marianne, who had turned her face away, refusing to engage.

"Is she holding up, Miss Dashwood? Poor creature! She looks critically compromised. No surprise there. Indeed, the facts are irrefutable. He is to be wed imminently—to a woman of negligible character! I possess zero tolerance for such conduct. Mrs. Taylor relayed the intelligence to me mere moments ago, sourced from a confidante of Miss Grey herself; absent that confirmation, I would have dismissed it as fabrication. I nearly collapsed upon hearing it. 'Well,' I declared, 'if this holds water, he has treated a young lady of my acquaintance with abominable cruelty. May his new wife torment his soul until the end of days.' Such sentiments shall remain my constant refrain, my dear. You may rely on it. I cannot comprehend why men persist in this behavior. Should I encounter him again, I shall administer a beating he has not endured in decades. Yet, there is solace, my dear Miss Marianne: he is not the sole eligible male in existence. With your visage, you shall never lack suitors. Alas, poor thing! I shall cease disturbing her further; she requires immediate catharsis to purge these emotions. Fortunately, the Parrys and Sandersons arrive tonight, providing necessary distraction."

She departed, moving on tiptoes, operating under the assumption that auditory stimuli would exacerbate her young friend's condition.

To Elinor's astonishment, Marianne resolved to dine with the household. Elinor counseled against it, yet Marianne insisted, "I shall descend; I can endure it, and the commotion will serve as a buffer." Satisfied that her sister was momentarily governed by such pragmatic reasoning, though doubting her ability to sustain the meal, Elinor offered no further objection. Adjusting Marianne's attire as best she could while the latter remained recumbent, she prepared to escort her to the dining hall upon summons.

Seated there, though appearing profoundly wretched, Marianne consumed more sustenance and displayed greater composure than anticipated. Had she attempted speech or registered half of Mrs. Jennings' well-intentioned but tactically flawed attentions, this equilibrium would have fractured. Instead, she remained mute; the abstraction of her thoughts shielded her from awareness of the surrounding chaos.

Elinor, acknowledging Mrs. Jennings' benevolence despite its frequent distressing and occasionally absurd manifestations, offered the requisite acknowledgments and reciprocated civilities that Marianne lacked the capacity to provide. Observing Marianne's unhappiness, Mrs. Jennings concluded that every available resource should be deployed to alleviate her state. Consequently, she treated her with the indulgent affection reserved for a favored child on the eve of departure. Marianne was assigned the prime seat by the fire, tempted with every delicacy in the house, and subjected to a barrage of current events. Had Elinor not witnessed the somber countenance of her sister acting as a brake on mirth, she might have been entertained by Mrs. Jennings' efforts to cure romantic disappointment with confections, olives, and a robust hearth. However, once the relentless repetition of these gestures forced themselves upon Marianne's consciousness, she could endure no longer. With a sharp exclamation of *Misery* and a gesture commanding her sister to desist, she rose abruptly and fled the room.

"Poor soul!" cried Mrs. Jennings immediately upon her exit. "How grievous it is to witness! I declare she departs unfinished regarding her wine and the dried cherries! Lord! Nothing appears to benefit her. Were I privy to anything she desired, I would scour the entire town for it. Well, it is peculiar that a man should treat such a lovely girl so poorly! But when capital flows freely from one side and is virtually nonexistent on the other, Lord bless you! they disregard such trifles entirely—"

"The lady in question—Miss Grey, I believe you termed her—is affluent?"

"Fifty thousand pounds, my dear. Have you encountered her? Described as smart and stylish, though lacking conventional beauty. I recall her aunt, Biddy Henshawe; she wed a man of considerable means. The entire family possesses wealth. Fifty thousand pounds! And by all accounts, it will not materialize until absolutely necessary, for they claim he is utterly depleted. No wonder! Dashing about in his curricle and hunting mounts! Well, discourse is futile. Yet, when a young man, regardless of identity, courts a pretty girl and pledges matrimony, he lacks justification for renouncing his vow solely due to financial decline and the availability of a richer prospect. Why not, in such an instance, liquidate his equine assets, lease his estate, dismiss his staff, and implement a comprehensive restructuring immediately? I wager Miss Marianne would have waited for circumstances to align. But that strategy fails in the modern era; no aspect of pleasure can be relinquished by the youth of this generation."

"Do you ascertain the nature of Miss Grey? Is she reputed amiable?"

"I have heard no malice regarding her; indeed, she is rarely discussed, save that Mrs. Taylor remarked this morning Miss Walker hinted to her that Mr. and Mrs. Ellison would welcome Miss Grey's marriage, noting their inability to coexist harmoniously."

"And who constitute the Ellisons?"

"Her guardians, my dear. Though now of age, she may elect her own path; and what a selection she has made!—What now," pausing briefly, "your afflicted sister has retired to her chambers, presumably to lament in solitude. Can we procure anything to console her? Poor dear, it seems cruel to permit isolation. Well, shortly we shall host acquaintances, offering slight amusement. What shall we play? She detests whist, I know; does she prefer any round games?"

"Dear madam, such kindness is superfluous. Marianne, I suspect, will not emerge from her room again this evening. I shall endeavor to persuade her to retire early, for she clearly requires repose."

"Aye, that proves optimal. Permit her to designate her own supper and retire. Lord! No wonder she has appeared so debilitated and dejected over the past fortnight; this matter has undoubtedly loomed over her head during that interval. And thus, the letter received today finalized the tragedy! Poor soul! I assure you, had I suspected the truth, I would not have jesting about it for all my fortune. Yet, you understand, how could I divine such a development? I assumed it was merely a commonplace love letter, knowing young people delight in being mocked regarding such affairs. Lord! How distressed Sir John and my daughters will be upon learning this! If I possessed my faculties, I might have visited Conduit Street en route home to inform them. I shall see them tomorrow."

"It would be unnecessary for you to caution Mrs. Palmer and Sir John against mentioning Mr. Willoughby or alluding to the preceding events before my sister. Their inherent benevolence will intuitively recognize the brutality of feigning knowledge of the matter in her presence. The fewer words spoken to me concerning this subject, the more my feelings will be spared, as you, my dear madam, will readily concede."

"Oh! Lord! Yes, I truly do. It must be dreadful for you to hear it discussed; and regarding your sister, I assure you, I would not utter a single word to her for the world. You observed I remained silent throughout dinner. Neither would Sir John, nor my daughters, for they are all remarkably thoughtful and considerate, particularly if I offer a hint, which I certainly shall. Personally, I believe the less discourse devoted to such matters, the better; the sooner it blows over and vanishes. What utility does talking ever serve, you know?"

"In this specific affair, it serves only harm; potentially more so than in many analogous cases, for it has been accompanied by circumstances rendering it unfit for public conversation for the sake of all involved. I must accord this justice to Mr. Willoughby—he has breached no explicit engagement with my sister."

"Law, my dear! Cease defending him. No explicit engagement indeed! After touring her extensively through Allenham House and selecting the very chambers wherein they were to reside henceforth!"

For her sister's sake, Elinor could not press the subject further, hoping it was not required for Willoughby's benefit; though Marianne might suffer greatly, he could gain little by enforcing the absolute truth. Following a brief silence, Mrs. Jennings, with her innate hilarity, erupted anew.

"Well, my dear, 'tis a veritable adage regarding an ill wind; it shall prove advantageous for Colonel Brandon. He shall secure her eventually; ay, he shall. Mark my words, if they are not united by Midsummer. Lord! How he shall chuckle over this tidings! I trust he arrives tonight. It matters not to one whether it is a superior match for your sister. Two thousand a year, free of debt or deduction—save for the minor love-child, indeed; ay, I forgot her; yet she may be apprenticed out at minimal cost, and then what significance does it hold? Delaford is a charming locale, I can attest; precisely what I term a pleasant, old-fashioned setting, brimming with comforts and conveniences; completely enclosed by substantial garden walls adorned with the finest fruit trees in the nation; and such a mulberry tree in one corner! Lord! How Charlotte and I stuffed ourselves the sole

The morning sun filtered through the curtains, yet Marianne awoke to a darkness that had settled deep within her soul. The night had offered her more rest than she dared expect, but the quality of that sleep was irrelevant; the consciousness of misery remained, sharp and unyielding, exactly as it had been when she closed her eyes.

Elinor, observing her sister's state, endeavored to draw her out, encouraging her to articulate the turmoil consuming her. Before breakfast was served, they had circled the tragedy of Willoughby's departure repeatedly. Elinor offered steady conviction and affectionate counsel, a constant anchor in the storm, while Marianne oscillated between impetuous despair and fleeting rationalizations. One moment, she could envision Willoughby as a victim of circumstance, as innocent and unfortunate as herself; the next, she found herself stripped of all consolation by the sheer impossibility of his innocence. Her emotional landscape shifted violently: sometimes she was utterly indifferent to the prying eyes of the world, at other times she sought total isolation, and occasionally she possessed the energy to resist scrutiny with fierce determination. Yet, in one crucial aspect, she remained consistent: whenever possible, she avoided the presence of Mrs. Jennings, and when avoidance was impossible, she maintained a rigid, defensive silence. Her heart had calcified against the possibility that Mrs. Jennings could comprehend her sorrow with anything resembling genuine compassion.

"No, no, no," Marianne cried, her voice trembling with suppressed rage. "She cannot feel. Her kindness is merely a performance; her good-nature is not tenderness. All she desires is gossip, and she clings to me solely because I provide her with a spectacle."

Elinor had long anticipated this distortion in her sister's judgment. She understood that Marianne, possessing excellent abilities and a noble disposition, was nonetheless plagued by an irritable refinement of mind and an inflated sense of the delicacy required by high sensibility. Like many of the clever and good, Marianne was neither reasonable nor candid. She projected her own internal compass onto others, expecting identical opinions and feelings, and she judged the motives of the world strictly by the immediate impact their actions had upon her own fragile psyche.

This psychological rigidity manifested clearly later that morning. While the sisters sat together in their private chamber after breakfast, a specific incident occurred that drove Marianne's contempt for Mrs. Jennings to new depths. Through her own weakness, Mrs. Jennings had inadvertently become a source of fresh anguish for Marianne, despite acting with the purest intentions.

Mrs. Jennings entered the room holding a letter, her countenance bright with the false assurance that she was delivering solace.

"Now, my dear," she announced, her smile radiant, "I bring you something I am certain will do you good."

Marianne heard the trap spring shut. In a single heartbeat, her imagination conjured a vision of redemption: a letter from Willoughby, brimming with tenderness and contrition, explaining every ambiguity, offering satisfaction and proof of his fidelity. Instantly following this epistolary salvation, she pictured Willoughby himself bursting into the room, eager to reinforce the written words with the eloquent pleading of his gaze. The entire edifice of her shattered hopes collapsed in the next moment. Instead of Willoughby, the handwriting of her mother appeared before her.

Until that instant, her mother's letters had been unwelcome intrusions; now, in the acuity of her disappointment, they became instruments of torture. The contrast between the ecstasy of her fabricated hope and the harsh reality of her mother's letter made her feel as though she had never truly suffered until this exact second.

The cruelty of Mrs. Jennings was such that no language Marianne could muster in her most eloquent moments could adequately express it. She could offer only a torrent of tears, streaming from her eyes with violent passion—a reproach so completely lost on its recipient that Mrs. Jennings, after expressing pity, withdrew, still urging Marianne to read the letter of comfort. When Marianne finally calmed enough to examine the document, it offered little solace. Every page was dominated by Willoughby's name. Her mother, still firmly convinced of their engagement and relying with undiminished warmth on his constancy, had only responded to Elinor's inquiries by imploring Marianne to be more open toward them both. Written with such apparent tenderness, such feigned affection for Willoughby, and such a naive conviction of their shared future happiness, the letter induced agony rather than peace.

An overwhelming urge to return home seized Marianne anew. Her mother, paradoxically, became dearer to her than ever before, precisely because of the excess of her misplaced confidence in Willoughby. Marianne was wild with urgency to depart. Elinor, equally torn between the merits of staying in London versus returning to Barton, offered no personal counsel save for patience until their mother's instructions arrived. Eventually, she secured her sister's agreement to await further news.

Mrs. Jennings departed earlier than usual, unable to find peace until the Middletons and Palmers could mourn as deeply as she did. Refusing Elinor's offer of company, she left alone to spend the remainder of the morning elsewhere. Elinor, burdened by a heavy heart and acutely aware of the pain she was about to inflict, sat down to write to her mother, detailing the events and begging for direction. Meanwhile, Marianne, having joined them in the drawing-room upon Mrs. Jennings' exit, remained fixed at the table where Elinor wrote. She watched the progress of her sister's pen, grieving for the hardship of the task, and grieving even more profoundly for the effect these words would inevitably have on their mother.

For approximately fifteen minutes, they existed in this suspended state of quiet tension, when a sudden rap at the door shattered the atmosphere. Marianne, whose nerves were currently too frayed to withstand abrupt noises, jumped.

"Who can this be?" Elinor exclaimed, startled. "So early! I thought we had finally found safety."

Marianne moved to the window, peering out with visible irritation.

"It is Colonel Brandon!" she declared, her voice laced with vexation. "We are never safe from *him*."

"He will not enter, as Mrs. Jennings is absent," Elinor reasoned.

"I will not trust in that assumption," Marianne retorted, retreating to her own room. "A man who possesses nothing to occupy his own time demonstrates no conscience regarding his intrusion upon the privacy of others."

Fate, however, proved her conjecture correct, albeit based on a profound misunderstanding. Colonel Brandon *did* enter. Elinor, convinced that his presence was driven by solicitude for Marianne—and seeing that genuine concern etched into his disturbed, melancholy features and his anxious, brief inquiry after her well-being—could not forgive her sister for dismissing him so lightly.

"I encountered Mrs. Jennings in Bond Street," Brandon began after the initial greetings, "and she encouraged me to come. I was readily persuaded, moreover, because I believed it probable that I might find you alone, which was my primary desire. My object—my wish, my sole wish in seeking this audience—I hope, I believe it is—to serve as a means of providing comfort. No, I must correct myself: not present comfort, but conviction, lasting conviction for your sister's mind. My regard for her, for yourself, and for your mother... will you permit me to demonstrate this by relating certain circumstances? Nothing but a very sincere regard, nothing but an earnest desire to be useful, I think justifies me. Though, considering the many hours I have spent convincing myself of my own correctness, is there not some reason to fear I may be wrong?" He paused, searching for the right footing.

"I understand," Elinor replied, her tone shifting from defensiveness to desperate curiosity. "You have something to tell me concerning Mr. Willoughby that will illuminate his character further. Your disclosure would constitute the greatest act of friendship one could show Marianne. My gratitude would be immediate upon hearing any information tending toward that end, and hers must eventually follow. Pray, pray, let me hear it."

"You shall; and, to be brief, when I left Barton last October..." He hesitated, realizing the complexity of the tale. "But this will give you no idea. I must go further back. You will find me a very awkward narrator, Miss Dashwood; I hardly know where to commence. A short account of myself, I believe, is necessary, and it *shall* be concise. On such a subject," he sighed heavily, "can I have little temptation to be diffuse."

He paused for a moment to gather his thoughts, then, with another sigh, resumed his narrative.

"You have undoubtedly entirely forgotten a conversation—"he glanced at her, noting the futility of expecting memory—"between us one evening at Barton Park. It was the evening of a dance, in which I alluded to a lady I had once known, remarking that she resembled, in some measure, your sister Marianne."

"Indeed," Elinor answered, surprised. "I have *not* forgotten it."

Brandon looked pleased by this recollection. "If I am not deceived by the uncertainty and partiality of tender recollection, there is a very strong resemblance between them, both in mind and person. The same warmth of heart, the same eagerness of fancy and spirit. This lady was one of my nearest relations, an orphan from infancy, under the guardianship of my father. Our ages were nearly identical, and from our earliest years we were playmates and friends. I cannot recall a time when I did not love Eliza; and my affection for her, as we matured, was such that, judging from my current forl

When Elinor recounted the particulars of the conversation to her sister, the impact was not the serene resolution she had anticipated. Marianne did not question the veracity of the words; she absorbed them with a rigid, silent submission, offering no defense for Willoughby and allowing her tears to confirm the impossibility of his innocence. Yet, while this behavior signaled to Elinor that the truth had finally struck home—evidenced by Marianne ceasing to avoid Colonel Brandon, addressing him with a newfound, albeit pained, respect, and displaying a calmer demeanor—Elinor saw no corresponding reduction in her sister's suffering. Marianne's mind had settled, but it rested in a profound, gloomy dejection. The knowledge of Willoughby's moral collapse weighed heavier on her spirit than his abandonment; the image of Miss Williams' misery, coupled with the terrifying suspicion of what his intentions toward her once truly were, consumed her thoughts. She retreated into a silence so absolute it inflicted more agony on Elinor than any confession could have.

Mrs. Dashwood, receiving and responding to her daughter's frantic correspondence, felt a sting of humiliation mixed with despair. To speak of fortitude in the face of such betrayal felt like a cruel mockery. She resolved, however, that Marianne's individual comfort was secondary to her preservation. Returning to Barton meant constant exposure to the memory of Willoughby, a place where every corner held the ghost of the man who had broken her heart. Therefore, she urged her daughters to extend their stay in London, a city where a variety of distractions might, by sheer chance, divert Marianne's gaze from her own ruin. In the crowded anonymity of the capital, the risk of encountering Willoughby was negligible; his reputation was now so tarnished that no friend would introduce them, and negligence could not expose them to surprise. Furthermore, a letter from her son-in-law indicated the arrival of the Ferrars family in mid-February, necessitating a prolonged urban residence. Though Marianne internally rebelled against this exile, recognizing it as a mistake born of maternal error that stripped her of her mother's direct sympathy, she submitted. For Elinor, the arrangement offered a perverse solace: while it threatened her own happiness by ensuring further proximity to Edward, it was the lesser of two evils compared to Marianne's potential collapse in Devonshire.

Elinor's vigilance in shielding Marianne from the name of Willoughby proved effective. Neither Mrs. Jennings, Sir John, nor Mrs. Palmer dared utter the forbidden topic before her. Elinor, however, was forced to endure the relentless indignation of her circle daily. Sir John, unable to comprehend how a man of such reputed virtue could fall, declared him a scoundrel and a deceitful dog, vowing never to speak another word to him or even look upon him, even if they were trapped together in Barton Covert for hours. Mrs. Palmer echoed this sentiment with equal fervor, resolving to cut all ties and ensure Combe Magna remained too distant for any future visits. Her sympathy manifested in a gossipy cataloging of Willoughby's new life: the coachmaker constructing his carriage, the painter capturing his likeness, and the warehouse stocking Miss Grey's garments. Amidst this clamorous kindness, Lady Middleton provided a rare refuge. Her calm, polite indifference was a balm to Elinor's oppressed spirits. It was a profound comfort to know at least one person in their circle possessed the breeding to feel no curiosity about the scandal and no anxiety for Marianne's health. By expressing her shock only in brief, ritualistic exclamations—"It is very shocking, indeed!"—Lady Middleton maintained her social dignity while condemning the act, eventually moving on to her own assemblies.

Colonel Brandon's inquiries, delivered with delicate discretion, were always welcome. Having earned the right to discuss Marianne's sorrow through his zealous efforts to soothe it, they shared confidences freely. His greatest reward was the pitying glance Marianne occasionally cast his way and the softening of her voice when she addressed him. These small signs assured Brandon of growing goodwill and gave Elinor hope for the future. Meanwhile, Mrs. Jennings, oblivious to these subtle shifts, grew increasingly convinced that the Colonel's suit was failing. Seeing him as grave as ever, and noting his inability to propose or delegate the task, she began to predict a delay until Michaelmas, then abandoned the idea of marriage entirely, assuming the honors of the mulberry tree and the yew arbour belonged solely to Elinor.

Early in February, barely two weeks after receiving Willoughby's letter, Elinor performed the painful duty of informing Marianne of his marriage. She ensured the news reached her privately, sparing her sister the shock of reading it in the morning newspapers. Marianne accepted the intelligence with resolute composure, shedding no initial tears. Only after a pause did they burst forth, leaving her in a state of wretchedness nearly indistinguishable from her initial despair. With the Willoughbys departing town immediately after the wedding, Elinor hoped to gradually reintroduce her sister to the outdoors.

Coinciding with this somber period, the two Miss Steeles returned to their relatives in Conduit Street, greeted with effusive cordiality by everyone except Elinor. Their presence was a source of acute pain, particularly Lucy's triumphant discovery that Elinor had stayed in London. "I should have been quite disappointed if I had not found you here *still*," Lucy exclaimed, emphasizing the word with malicious delight. She feigned surprise that Elinor hadn't departed sooner, mocking her previous promise to leave within a month, and celebrating her decision to break her word. Elinor, mastering her self-control, masked her resentment with a facade of grace.

Miss Steele, boasting of their journey via post-chaise accompanied by Dr. Davies, played the role of the coquettish debutante. She laughed off accusations that the doctor was her "beau," claiming she thought of him not at all, while her cousin insisted he clearly was. Mrs. Jennings, delighted by the charade, promised to contradict anyone who suggested otherwise, rendering Miss Steele completely happy. The conversation inevitably turned to the Dashwood sisters. When Lucy asked if Elinor would visit her brother and sister upon their arrival, Elinor demurred. Lucy persisted, and when Mrs. Jennings interjected that the visit was only just beginning, Lucy fell silent. Turning to Elinor, Miss Steele expressed regret at not seeing Marianne, citing her recent "nervous headaches" as a barrier to socializing. Elinor politely declined the invitation, explaining that her sister was resting in her dressing gown. Undeterred, Miss Steele declared they would simply go to see her personally. This impertinence pushed Elinor to the brink of losing her temper, but she was rescued from the confrontation by Lucy's sharp reprimand, a habit that, while lacking sweetness, effectively governed her sister's manners.

The morning air was crisp, cutting through the lingering melancholy of the previous days. After significant resistance, Marianne finally capitulated to Elinor's persistent pleas, agreeing to venture out for a mere half-hour. However, she established a rigid boundary: no social calls, no engagements beyond accompanying them to Gray's in Sackville Street, where Elinor was conducting negotiations regarding the exchange of her mother's antique jewelry.

As they reached the threshold, Mrs. Jennings recalled a pending obligation—a visit to a lady at the opposite end of the street—and, lacking personal business at the jeweler's, resolved to attend to her errand first before retrieving her companions. Upon ascending the stairs, the Dashwood sisters encountered a scene of chaotic congestion. The shop floor was packed; there was not a single clerk available to attend to their needs. Forced to wait, they claimed the corner of the counter that promised the shortest queue. Only one customer occupied the space ahead of them, yet Elinor harbored a faint hope that his inherent politeness might expedite the transaction. Alas, his acute eye and refined taste rendered him incapable of courtesy toward others. He was entirely consumed by the specifications of a toothpick case, meticulously debating its dimensions, form, and ornamentation for fifteen minutes. Until he had finalized this trivial acquisition according to his own inventive whims, he afforded the two ladies nothing more than three or four vacant, piercing stares. These glances served to etch into Elinor's memory the visage of a man possessing a robust, natural, and utterly insignificant character, yet dressed in the height of fashionable splendor.

Marianne, spared the torment of feeling slighted by this intrusive scrutiny and the juvenile arrogance of his decision-making process, remained blissfully oblivious. She possessed the fortitude to retreat inward, constructing a mental fortress wherein she could remain as unaware of the absurdities unfolding in Mr. Gray's shop as she would be in her own chamber.

Eventually, the transaction concluded. Ivory, gold, and pearls were assigned their places. The gentleman, having declared the specific date by which his existence could continue without his new toothpick case, slowly donned his gloves. Bestowing one final look upon the Miss Dashwoods—a gaze that demanded admiration rather than offering it—he departed with an air of genuine conceit masked by feigned indifference.

Elinor wasted no time advancing her own business, poised to finalize the deal when another gentleman stepped beside her. Turning, she met with surprise the face of her brother, John Dashwood. Their reunion was marked by a display of affection sufficient to appear dignified amidst the clutter of the shop. John was genuinely relieved to see his sisters; his inquiries concerning their mother were respectful and attentive.

"Elinor," he noted, "Fanny and I have been in town for two days. I intended to call upon you yesterday, but it was impossible; we were compelled to take Harry to see the wild beasts at Exeter Exchange, and the remainder of the day was spent with Mrs. Ferrars. Harry was delighted. This morning, I had planned to stop by if I could spare half an hour, but the initial rush of arriving in town leaves one perpetually occupied. I am here to commission a seal for Fanny. Tomorrow, however, I expect to be able to visit Berkeley Street and introduce myself to your friend, Mrs. Jennings. I understand she possesses considerable fortune. And the Middletons—you must introduce me to them. As my mother-in-law's relatives, I intend to extend every due respect. They are excellent neighbors in the countryside, I gather."

"Excellent indeed," Elinor replied. "Their attentiveness to our comfort and their friendliness exceed description."

"I am immensely gratified to hear this," John exclaimed. "It ought to be so; they are wealthy, they are kin, and every civility required to ease your situation is reasonable to expect. Moreover, you are comfortably settled in your cottage, lacking for nothing! Edward provided a delightful account of the place—the most perfect residence of its kind, he said—and you all seem to thrive beyond measure. It was a profound satisfaction to us to learn this."

A flush of shame rose in Elinor's cheeks at her brother's tone, and she was grateful when Mrs. Jennings's servant arrived to announce that his mistress awaited them outside. Mr. Dashwood escorted them downstairs, introducing them to Mrs. Jennings at the carriage door. Reiterating his hope to visit the following day, he took his leave.

His subsequent visit was formalized. He arrived under the pretext of apologizing for his wife's absence, claiming she was so engrossed with her mother that she lacked the leisure to travel. Mrs. Jennings, however, dismissed such niceties, noting they were all cousins or close relations, and affirmed her intention to visit Mrs. John Dashwood shortly, bringing her sisters along. His demeanor toward the sisters was calmly kind, while his treatment of Mrs. Jennings was excessively deferential. When Colonel Brandon entered moments later, John eyed him with a calculating curiosity, seemingly assessing his net worth before extending equal civility.

After half an hour, John requested that Elinor accompany him to Conduit Street to introduce him to Sir John and Lady Middleton. The weather was exceptionally fair, and she readily agreed. The moment they crossed the threshold, his interrogation commenced.

"Who is Colonel Brandon? Does he possess substantial wealth?"

"Yes; he holds significant property in Dorsetshire."

"I am pleased to hear that. He appears a most gentlemanly individual. I believe, Elinor, I may congratulate you on the prospect of a respectable establishment."

"Me, brother? What do you mean?"

"He favors you. I observed him closely and am convinced of it. What is the extent of his fortune?"

"I estimate approximately two thousand pounds per annum."

"Two thousand a year!" John worked himself into a crescendo of enthusiastic benevolence. "Elinor, I wish with all my heart it were *twice* that amount, for your sake."

"Indeed, I believe you," Elinor responded coolly. "However, I am certain Colonel Brandon harbors no desire to marry *me*."

"You are mistaken, Elinor; profoundly so. Minimal effort on your part secures him. Perhaps he is currently undecided; the modesty of your fortune may cause hesitation, or his friends may counsel against it. Yet, certain attentions and encouragements, which ladies can dispense so effortlessly, will bind him regardless of his inclinations. There is no impediment to your attempting this. It is not to be assumed that any prior attachment exists on your side—in short, you understand that such matters are quite out of the question; the obstacles are insurmountable. You possess too much sense not to recognize this. Colonel Brandon must be the man; and no courtesy shall be withheld on my part to ensure his approval of you and your family. It is a union destined to satisfy everyone. In brief, it is a circumstance that"—he lowered his voice to a conspiratorial whisper—"will be exceedingly welcome to *all parties*." Regaining his composure, he added, "That is, I mean to say—your friends are genuinely eager to see you settled; Fanny especially, for she holds your interests very dear, I assure you. And her mother, Mrs. Ferrars, a woman of excellent nature, would derive immense pleasure from it; she mentioned as much recently."

Elinor declined to offer any response.

"It would be somewhat remarkable, now," he continued, "perhaps even amusing, if Fanny were to settle a brother while I settle a sister simultaneously. And yet, it is not improbable."

"Is Mr. Edward Ferrars," Elinor asked with resolve, "intending to marry?"

"It is not formally settled, though the matter is agitated. He has a most exemplary mother. Mrs. Ferrars, with the utmost generosity, will provide him with an allowance of one thousand pounds per annum should the match proceed. The lady is the Honorable Miss Morton, sole daughter of the late Lord Morton, endowed with thirty thousand pounds. A highly desirable alliance on both fronts, and I harbor no doubt it will occur. One thousand pounds annually is a substantial gift for a mother to bestow permanently, yet Mrs. Ferrars possesses a noble spirit. Another testament to her liberality: upon our arrival in town, recognizing that funds were scarce for us at the moment, she placed banknotes totaling two hundred pounds into Fanny's hands. It was extremely welcome, for we must maintain high expenditures while residing here."

He paused, awaiting her sympathy and agreement. She managed to force the words, "Your expenses, both in town and country, must certainly be considerable; however, your income is substantial."

"Not so large, I presume, as many assume. I do not intend to complain; it is undoubtedly comfortable, and I anticipate improvement. The enclosure of Norland Common, currently underway, is a severe financial drain. Furthermore, I acquired East Kingham Farm within the past half-year. You recall the estate where old Gibson resided. The land was so desirable in every respect, so immediately adjacent to my own holdings, that I felt it my duty to purchase it. I could not bear the thought of letting it pass into other hands. A man must pay for his convenience, and it *has* cost me a vast sum."

"More than its intrinsic value warrants."

"Why, I hope not. I could have resold it the following day for a profit. Regarding the purchase price, however, I faced potential misfortune; the stock market was at a low ebb at that time. Had I not possessed the necessary capital in my banker's accounts, I would have incurred significant losses."

Elinor could only smile.

The next day, Mrs. John Dashwood visited Mrs. Jennings and her daughters, and the visit yielded a surprising result: even the woman hosting her sisters proved worthy of her attention. As for Lady Middleton, she emerged as one of the most charming women in the world. A strange, cold-hearted symmetry existed between the two households; they were drawn together by a mutual attraction born of shared superficiality and a profound lack of genuine understanding. While these mannerisms secured Mrs. John Dashwood's approval from Lady Middleton, they repelled Mrs. Jennings, who viewed her as merely a proud, unaffectionate woman who barely spoke to her husband's sisters. During the fifteen-minute visit to Berkeley Street, Mrs. Jennings noted that Mrs. Dashwood spent at least seven and a half minutes in absolute silence.

Elinor wished intensely to inquire whether Edward was in town, though she chose not to ask. Nothing would have prompted Fanny to volunteer the information until she could confirm that Edward's marriage to Miss Morton was finalized or that her husband's hopes for Colonel Brandon were realized. She believed the couple remained so deeply attached that they required constant separation in word and deed. However, the intelligence Elinor sought soon arrived from another source. Lucy came promptly to claim Elinor's sympathy for her inability to see Edward, who had arrived in town with the Dashwoods. Fear of detection prevented him from visiting Bartlett's Buildings, and though their mutual longing to meet was palpable, they were restricted to correspondence. Yet, Edward ensured they knew of his presence in town by calling at Berkeley Street twice. Twice his calling card was discovered upon their return from morning engagements. Elinor was pleased that he had called, and even more pleased that she had missed him.

The Dashwoods were so delighted with the Middletons that, despite their usual reluctance to host, they decided to invite them to dinner. Soon after forming this acquaintance, they extended an invitation to dine at their residence in Harley Street, where they had leased a fine house for three months. The sisters and Mrs. Jennings were included, and John Dashwood carefully secured Colonel Brandon, who, always eager to be near the Miss Dashwoods, accepted their eager civilities with some surprise but considerable pleasure. They intended to meet Mrs. Ferrars, though Elinor could not ascertain if her sons would attend. Nevertheless, the prospect of meeting her was sufficient to engage Elinor's interest. Though she could now face Edward's mother without the acute anxiety that once plagued such introductions, viewing her with perfect indifference regarding her own merit, her curiosity about Mrs. Ferrars' character remained as vivid as ever.

This anticipation was soon intensified, more powerfully than pleasantly, by news that the Miss Steeles would also be present. Having impressed Lady Middleton so thoroughly, and rendered so agreeable by their assiduity, that though Lucy lacked elegance and her sister was not even genteel, Sir John was as ready as Lady Middleton to invite them to spend a week or two in Conduit Street. Conveniently, the Miss Steeles arranged their arrival to coincide with the Dashwoods' party. While their connection to Mrs. John Dashwood as the nieces of her late brother's caretaker might not have secured them a seat at her table, as Lady Middleton's guests, they were welcome. Lucy, who longed to be personally known to the family to gauge their characters and navigate her own difficulties, had rarely been happier than when she received Mrs. John Dashwood's card. For Elinor, the effect was diametrically opposed. She immediately resolved that Edward, living with his mother, must be invited to a party hosted by his sister, and the thought of seeing him for the first time since their parting in the company of Lucy filled her with dread.

These apprehensions, perhaps not entirely grounded in reason or truth, were alleviated not by Elinor's own reflection but by Lucy's goodwill. Believing she was inflicting a severe disappointment, Lucy informed Elinor that Edward would certainly not be in Harley Street on Tuesday, hoping further to soothe her by persuading her that Edward was absent solely due to his extreme affection for her, which he could not conceal when they were together. The crucial Tuesday arrived, destined to introduce the two young ladies to this formidable mother-in-law. "Pity me, dear Miss Dashwood!" cried Lucy as they ascended the stairs together, for the Middletons arrived immediately after Mrs. Jennings, and they all followed the servant simultaneously. "There is no one here but you who can truly feel for me. I declare I can hardly stand. Good gracious! In a moment I shall behold the person upon whose happiness my entire existence depends—that is to be my mother!" Elinor could have provided immediate relief by suggesting they were about to behold Miss Morton's mother rather than her own, but instead, she assured Lucy with great sincerity that she pitied her, to the utter amazement of Lucy, who, though genuinely uncomfortable, hoped to appear an object of irrepressible envy to Elinor.

Mrs. Ferrars was a small, thin woman, upright to the point of formality in her bearing and serious to the point of sourness in her aspect. Her complexion was sallow; her features were small, devoid of beauty and naturally lacking expression. Only a fortunate contraction of the brow saved her countenance from the disgrace of insipidity, lending it instead the strong characteristics of pride and ill-nature. She was not a woman of many words, proportioning her speech strictly to the number of her ideas. Of the few syllables that escaped her lips, not one fell to the share of Miss Dashwood, whom she eyed with the spirited determination of disliking her at all costs. Elinor could no longer be distressed by such behavior. A few months prior, it would have hurt her exceedingly, but Mrs. Ferrars held no power to distress her now. Indeed, the disparity in her treatment of the Miss Steeles, a difference seemingly engineered to humble them further, only amused Elinor. She could not help smiling at the graciousness displayed by both mother and daughter toward the very person—Lucy Steele, who was particularly distinguished—who, had they known the truth, they would have been most anxious to mortify. Meanwhile, Elinor, possessing comparatively little power to wound them, sat pointedly slighted by both.

Yet, as she smiled at this misapplied graciousness, she could not reflect on the mean-spirited folly from which it sprang, nor observe the studied attentions with which the Miss Steeles courted its continuation, without thoroughly despising all four. Lucy was in a state of exultation at being so honorably distinguished, while Miss Steele desired nothing more than to be teased about Dr. Davies to achieve perfect happiness. The dinner was grand, the servants numerous, and everything bespoke the mistress's inclination for display and the master's ability to sustain it. Despite the ongoing improvements to the Norland estate and the fact that its owner had once been within a thousand pounds of selling at a loss, no symptom of indigence appeared—not even a poverty of conversation, which was considerable. John Dashwood had little of value to say, and his wife even less. This was not peculiar disgrace, however, for most of their visitors suffered from similar disqualifications for agreeability: a lack of sense, natural or acquired; a lack of elegance; a lack of spirits; or a lack of temper.

When the ladies retired to the drawing-room after dinner, this conversational poverty became particularly evident. The gentlemen had supplied the discourse with some variety—politics, enclosing land, and breaking horses—but the topic died quickly thereafter. Only one subject engaged the ladies until coffee was served: the comparative heights of Harry Dashwood and Lady Middleton's second son, William, who were nearly the same age. Had both children been present, the matter might have been settled easily by measurement; but with only Harry there, it devolved into conjectural assertion on both sides. Everyone felt entitled to be equally positive in their opinion and to repeat it ad infinitum. The positions were as follows: the two mothers, though each privately convinced her own son was taller, politely decided in favor of the other. The two grandmothers, with equal partiality but greater sincerity, were equally earnest in supporting their own descendants. Lucy, hardly less anxious to please one parent than the other, deemed both boys remarkably tall for their age and could not conceive of any difference whatsoever. Miss Steele, with greater address, gave credit to each boy as fast as she could. Elinor, having once expressed her opinion in favor of William, thereby offending Mrs. Ferrars and Fanny further, saw no necessity to enforce it with further assertion. Marianne, when called upon, offended everyone by declaring she had no opinion to offer, as she had never considered the matter.

Before leaving Norland, Elinor had painted a very pretty pair of screens for her sister-in-law, which, now mounted and brought home, adorned the current drawing-room. Catching the eye of John Dashwood as he followed the other gentlemen into the room, these screens were officiously handed to Colonel Brandon for his admiration. "These are done by my eldest sister," he said. "As a man of taste, you will, I dare say, be pleased with them. I do not know whether you have ever seen any of her performances before, but she is generally reckoned to draw extremely well." The Colonel, though disclaiming all pretensions to connoisseurship, warmly admired the screens as he would have admired anything painted by Miss Dashwood. With the curiosity of the others naturally aroused, they were circulated for general inspection. Mrs. Ferrars, unaware they were Elinor's work, particularly requested to examine them. After receiving gratifying testimony of Lady Middleton's approval, Fanny presented them to her mother, consider

Elinor's curiosity regarding Mrs. Ferrars was finally quelled. She had uncovered sufficient evidence to render any further alliance between the families strategically unsound. Having witnessed firsthand Mrs. Ferrars's pride, her meanness, and her rigid, prejudiced hostility toward herself, Elinor comprehended the formidable barriers that had previously obstructed Edward's union with her. Yet, paradoxically, these observations also served as a shield; she was grateful for the existence of this greater obstacle, which spared her the agony of depending on Mrs. Ferrars's volatile whims or seeking validation from her judgment. While she did not celebrate Edward's entanglement with Lucy, she resolved that, had Lucy possessed even a fraction of amiability, she *ought* to have rejoiced.

Elinor marveled at the source of Lucy's exuberance. How could such trivial civility elevate Lucy's spirits so profoundly? How could vanity and interest conspire to interpret attention paid solely because she was *not* Elinor as a personal compliment? Lucy's eyes had already betrayed the truth, yet the following morning, Lady Middleton facilitated a private audience in Berkeley Street specifically for Lucy to declare her happiness. The timing proved fortuitous, as a message from Mrs. Palmer shortly thereafter removed Mrs. Jennings from the scene.

"My dear friend," Lucy exclaimed the moment they were alone, "I must discuss my felicity. Can anything compare to Mrs. Ferrars's demeanor yesterday? She was excessively affable. Recall how I dreaded encountering her; yet, the instant I was introduced, her behavior radiated such genuine fondness that it suggested she had instantly taken a fancy to me. Was it not so? You witnessed it all; surely you were struck by it?"

"She was certainly civil to you," Elinor replied cautiously.

"Civil!" Lucy retorted sharply. "Did you perceive nothing beyond mere politeness? I discerned far more. A kindness reserved exclusively for me! No pride, no hauteur—your sister included, displaying equal sweetness and affability!"

Elinor attempted to pivot the conversation, but Lucy persisted, demanding acknowledgment of her reasons for happiness. "Undoubtedly, had they known of your engagement, their treatment would have been equally flattering," Elinor conceded. "However, since that was not the case—"

"I anticipated your response," Lucy interrupted briskly. "Yet, there is no logical basis for Mrs. Ferrars to harbor affection for me unless she genuinely feels it, and her approval is paramount. Do not try to dissuade me from my contentment. I am confident all will conclude favorably, devoid of the difficulties I once feared. Mrs. Ferrars is a charming woman, as is your sister. Both are delightful! I marvel that I have never heard you describe Mrs. Dashwood as agreeable!"

Elinor remained silent, offering no rebuttal. "Are you ill, Miss Dashwood? You appear listless and silent. Are you unwell?"

"I have never been healthier," Elinor stated firmly.

"I am relieved to hear it," Lucy responded warmly. "Though you did not look it. I would be devastated if *you* were ill; you have been my greatest comfort. Heaven knows what I would have endured without your friendship."

Elinor managed a polite response, though she doubted its efficacy. Satisfied, Lucy continued, "I am absolutely convinced of your regard for me. Next to Edward's love, it is my supreme solace. Poor Edward! But now, a positive development arises: we shall meet frequently. Lady Middleton delights in Mrs. Dashwood, ensuring we remain in Harley Street, where Edward spends considerable time with his sister. Furthermore, Lady Middleton and Mrs. Ferrars intend to visit soon. Both women have explicitly stated they would gladly receive me whenever convenient. They are such charming individuals! If you ever convey my sentiments to your sister, you cannot praise her too highly."

Elinor withheld any encouragement for Lucy to reveal these thoughts to her sister. Lucy pressed on, "I would have detected any aversion from Mrs. Ferrars instantly. Had she offered only a perfunctory courtesy, ignoring me thereafter with a cold gaze, I would have despaired immediately. I could not endure such rejection. Where she dislikes, her animosity is invariably violent."

Before Elinor could respond to this declaration of triumph, the door burst open, and the servant announced Mr. Ferrars. Edward entered moments later. The atmosphere shifted instantly into an awkward stalemate. Every countenance reflected the discomfort; each appeared foolish. Edward hesitated, torn between exiting the room and advancing further. They were trapped in a triad without the buffer of a third party—a scenario both had desperately sought to avoid.

The ladies recovered composure first. It was not Lucy's role to assert dominance, nor could she abandon the facade of secrecy. She merely cast a glance of tender affection at him before remaining silent. Elinor, however, faced a more arduous task. Driven by necessity for both his sake and her own, she summoned the resolve to greet him with a demeanor that was nearly natural and open. Through sheer exertion, she smoothed her features, suppressing the sting of injustice and the presence of Lucy's scrutinizing gaze. She assured him of her happiness in seeing him and expressed regret for her absence during his previous call. She maintained her duties as a friend and relative, undeterred by Lucy's watchful eyes.

Her efforts provided Edward with enough reassurance to sit, though his embarrassment surpassed theirs, a disparity justified by the circumstances. His heart lacked Lucy's indifference, and his conscience could not find the ease Elinor possessed. Lucy adopted a demure, fixed posture, contributing nothing to alleviate the tension. Consequently, Elinor became the sole speaker, volunteering details regarding her mother's health and their arrival in town—information Edward should have inquired about but neglected.

Seeking to relieve the pressure, Elinor feigned a mission to fetch Marianne, allowing the others privacy. With remarkable fortitude, she lingered on the landing for several minutes before descending. Once separated, Edward's rapture inevitably faded until Marianne's entrance revitalized the room. Her joy was palpable and vocal. Meeting him with a grasping hand and an expressive voice, she cried, "Dear Edward! This is a moment of profound happiness! It would almost redeem everything!"

Edward reciprocated her kindness within the bounds of propriety, unable to divulge his deeper feelings before such witnesses. After a brief silence, Marianne gazed alternately at Edward and Elinor with tender regret that their shared delight was curtailed by Lucy's unwanted presence. Edward addressed Marianne's pallor, expressing concern for her health in London.

"Oh, do not dwell on me!" she replied with spirited intensity, though tears filled her eyes. "Do not consider *my* health. Elinor is well; that suffices for us both."

This remark offered no comfort to Edward or Elinor, nor did it win Lucy's goodwill, whose expression remained unbenign. "Do you enjoy London?" Edward asked, attempting to steer the conversation.

"Not at all. I anticipated pleasure, yet found none. Your presence, Edward, is the sole consolation it offers. Thank Heaven, you remain exactly as you always were!" She paused, letting the sentiment hang in the air. "I believe, Elinor," she added, "we must entrust Edward with our care upon returning to Barton. Within a fortnight, I presume, we depart. I trust he will not be reluctant to assume this responsibility."

Edward murmured indistinctly, unaware even of his own words. Marianne, interpreting his agitation as confirmation of her own suspicions, swiftly changed the subject. "We spent such a tedious day in Harley Street yesterday! Dull, wretchedly dull! Yet, I have much to discuss with you regarding this matter, which cannot await this moment." With admirable discretion, she deferred her critique of their mutual relatives' increasing disagreeableness and her specific disgust with his mother until they were alone.

"But why were you absent, Edward? Why did you not come?"

"I was engaged elsewhere."

"Engaged! Yet, what significance does an engagement hold when such friends are to be reunited?"

"Perhaps, Miss Marianne," Lucy interjected eagerly, seizing an opportunity to retaliate, "you believe young men disregard engagements if they lack the inclination to honor them, regardless of magnitude."

Elinor bristled with anger, but Marianne remained impervious to the barb. Calmly, she countered, "Not so. Conscience, I assure you, was the sole impediment keeping Edward from Harley Street. Indeed, I posit he possesses the most delicate conscience imaginable—the most scrupulous observer of every obligation, however minor, even when it conflicts with his interests or pleasure. He fears inflicting pain, shatters expectations, and is utterly incapable of selfishness. Edward, this is truth, and I state it plainly. What! Are you never to hear yourself praised? Then you must cease being my friend; for those who accept my love and esteem must submit to my open commendation."

The nature of her praise, in this context, proved particularly ill-suited to the sensibilities of two-thirds of the audience. It was so unexhilarating for Edward that he rose promptly to depart.

"Leaving so soon!" Marianne protested. "My dear Edward, this must not happen." Drawing him aside, she whispered her conviction that Lucy could not remain long. Even this encouragement failed; Edward insisted on leaving. Lucy, who would have prolonged the visit indefinitely, eventually departed.

"What brings her here so frequently?" Marianne remarked upon her exit. "Could she not perceive our desire for her departure? How teasing to Edward

The newspapers, within days of the meeting, proclaimed to the world that Mrs. Thomas Palmer had safely delivered a son and heir. A trivial footnote to the global stage, yet a headline of immense significance to Mrs. Jennings, whose entire schedule underwent a tactical recalibration to accommodate this new development. Her mornings were now dedicated exclusively to Charlotte, and her evenings consumed by the demands of her young charges. Consequently, the Dashwood sisters found themselves consigned to Conduit Street, spending every waking hour in the service of Lady Middleton and the two Miss Steeles. While their personal preference leaned heavily toward the relative comfort of Mrs. Jennings' household, social protocol demanded compliance with the collective will of the hostess. Thus, their time was effectively auctioned off to a clientele that valued their presence as little as they professed to seek it.

Lady Middleton possessed sufficient intellect to recognize that the Dashwoods were undesirable companions; she simply lacked the emotional intelligence to understand why. Conversely, the Miss Steeles viewed them with a calculating jealousy, regarding them as encroachments on territory they intended to monopolize. Though Lady Middleton maintained a veneer of impeccable politeness toward Elinor and Marianne, her internal disposition remained hostile. Their refusal to flatter her or her offspring led her to categorize them as inherently unkind; their penchant for reading fueled her suspicion that they were satirists, a label she applied with the casual cruelty of someone unfamiliar with the actual mechanics of satire. It required no evidence, only the convenient assumption of malice.

The sisters' presence acted as a rigid constraint on the household dynamics. It curbed Lady Middleton's idle tendencies and stifled the performative affection Lucy Steele usually dispensed with such theatrical flair. Lady Middleton felt exposed performing nothing before them; Lucy feared that any attempt at flattery would be met with contemptuous disdain. Miss Steele, however, remained the most composed, her demeanor barely disturbed by their intrusion. Yet, she harbored a specific grievance: a single, detailed exposition of the affair between Marianne and Mr. Willoughby would have secured her loyalty. Instead, she received only pity from Elinor and dismissive remarks on the fickleness of lovers from Marianne. Even a lighthearted jest regarding Dr. Harris failed to bridge the gap; the sisters offered no ammunition for Miss Steele's own self-deprecating humor, leaving her isolated in her own comedy.

Mrs. Jennings, blissfully unaware of these simmering resentments, viewed the arrangement as a triumph. She celebrated nightly escapes from her own stagnant existence, attributing Charlotte's success entirely to her maternal influence. Her reports were exhaustive, minute, and delivered with the fervor of a commander briefing a subordinate, satisfying only Miss Steele's insatiable curiosity. However, one issue persisted: Mr. Palmer's adherence to the prevailing male dogma that all infants are indistinguishable. Despite the undeniable familial resemblance of the newborn to every relative on both sides, the father remained obstinate, refusing to acknowledge the child as anything other than a generic specimen of humanity.

A separate misfortune descended upon Mrs. John Dashwood shortly thereafter. While her sisters visited her in Harley Street, an acquaintance dropped by—a seemingly innocuous occurrence. Yet, human judgment is often hijacked by superficial impressions. Upon learning the Dashwood sisters were visiting, this lady erroneously assumed they resided permanently in Harley Street. Within forty-eight hours, invitations flooded in for a musical gathering. Mrs. John Dashwood was thus trapped in a dilemma of false hospitality. Refusal was impossible; acceptance meant subjecting her sisters to a series of social obligations she could not fulfill, all while maintaining the facade of generosity. The power to disappoint them existed, but the moral weight of leading them on rendered it useless.

Marianne, meanwhile, had succumbed to the rhythm of urban ennui. Going out had become a mechanical reflex, devoid of anticipation or joy. She prepared for engagements with robotic precision, often unaware until the final moment of her destination, deriving no genuine amusement from the spectacle. Her attire, once a source of potential vanity, held zero interest for her. Yet, Miss Steele transformed this indifference into a theater of scrutiny. Within five minutes of their reunion, the younger woman dissected Marianne's wardrobe with forensic intensity, interrogating the cost of every stitch, the quantity of her gowns, and the weekly expenditure on laundry. These invasive inquiries were invariably capped with a patronizing compliment—"Vastly smart!"—which Marianne interpreted not as praise, but as the ultimate insult following a thorough audit of her poverty.

On this particular evening, the carriage arrived with punctual efficiency, a timing that grated on Mrs. John Dashwood, who had hoped for a delay to inconvenience the driver. The musical party itself was a microcosm of British society: a mixture of genuine connoisseurs and the pretentious, featuring performers who rated themselves—and their peers—as the elite of England. Elinor, lacking musical inclination, treated the instruments as mere furniture, her gaze drifting freely across the room. It was there she spotted the man who had previously lectured them on toothpick cases at Gray's Inn. He approached her brother, and moments later, Mr. Dashwood introduced him as Mr. Robert Ferrars.

Robert greeted Elinor with effortless civility, executing a bow that screamed of arrogance. Had Elinor's affection for Edward relied solely on external validation, this display would have sealed her judgment against him. Yet, she recognized the distinction between the two brothers instantly. Robert's emptiness did not diminish her respect for Edward's quiet worth. During a brief conversation, Robert candidly explained the disparity. He attributed Edward's awkwardness not to innate deficiency, but to the tragedy of a private education, contrasting it with his own advantages gained from public schooling. He boasted of his ability to navigate high society effortlessly, framing his brother's struggles as a remediable error of parental judgment. When Elinor hesitated to challenge his view, citing her reservations about Edward's upbringing with the Pratts, Robert continued his monologue on the architectural virtues of cottages. He argued passionately that a well-designed cottage offered equal comfort to a mansion, recounting a successful ball hosted by Lady Elliott where spatial constraints were solved through clever planning. Elinor, recognizing the futility of opposing such confident nonsense, offered silent assent.

Back at home, John Dashwood proposed a solution to the earlier social faux pas. He suggested formally inviting the Dashwood sisters to stay, thereby fulfilling his paternal promise and alleviating his guilt. Fanny, however, interjected with a pragmatic objection: inviting them would alienate Lady Middleton, whose current guests they were. She countered that the Miss Steeles were available and deserving of hospitality, noting their recent kindness to Edward. Furthermore, she hinted that the Miss Steeles might soon depart town, making the opportunity fleeting. Mr. Dashwood, swayed by his wife's reasoning and eager to soothe his conscience, agreed to invite the Steeles immediately. He privately noted that inviting the sisters next year might prove unnecessary, speculating that Elinor would soon be married to Colonel Brandon and Marianne would follow as a guest, rendering the invitation redundant.

"Excellent," Mrs. Jennings announced, her voice dripping with the kind of manufactured excitement that usually preceded a scandal of national proportions. "Mrs. Palmer is recovering rapidly. In fact, she is so well that I have resumed my own domestic routine, finding the Miss Dashwoods eager to reclaim their former station. However, I must interrupt our leisurely resumption of normalcy with intelligence of the highest order."

She paused, allowing the weight of her impending revelation to settle in the drawing room, where Elinor sat alone, her posture rigid with practiced neutrality.

"You must hear this, my dear Miss Dashwood!" Mrs. Jennings continued, leaning forward with the urgency of a courier delivering dispatches from a war zone. "When I visited Mr. Palmer's, Charlotte was in a state of absolute panic regarding the infant. Cries, fretting, pustules everywhere. I examined the child immediately and pronounced it a trivial case of 'red gum,' a diagnosis shared by the nurse. Yet, Charlotte remained unsatisfied. Consequently, Dr. Donovan was summoned. Fortunately, he arrived fresh from Harley Street. Upon inspection, he concurred with our assessment: nothing serious, merely red gum. Satisfied, Charlotte relaxed. Then, as he departed, a thought occurred to me—a sudden, inexplicable impulse to inquire if there were any further developments concerning the young ladies under my care.

Dr. Donovan's demeanor shifted instantly. A smirk, a simper, a grave look—he clearly possessed information he deemed confidential. Finally, in a whisper, he delivered the verdict: *'For fear any unpleasant report should reach the young ladies... I believe there is no great reason for alarm; I hope Mrs. Dashwood will do very well.'*"

"What?" Elinor asked, her composure cracking slightly. "Is Fanny ill?"

"That is precisely what I asked!" Mrs. Jennings laughed, a harsh, triumphant sound. "And then the truth spilled forth. Mr. Edward Ferrars—the very youth I once joked with you about, though I am relieved to note those jokes were devoid of substance—has been secretly engaged to my cousin Lucy for twelve months! Do you comprehend the magnitude of this? Not a soul knew, save for Nancy. It is baffling that such a union could progress undetected. I never witnessed them together, yet the secrecy was absolute, presumably to shield them from Mrs. Ferrars. Until this morning, when Nancy, a well-meaning but tactless individual, inadvertently exposed the entire arrangement.

Nancy reasoned that since everyone adored Lucy, there would be no resistance. She proceeded directly to your sister, who was engrossed in carpet-work, blissfully unaware that she had just informed her brother of plans to match Edward with a lord's daughter. The psychological impact on Mrs. Dashwood's vanity was catastrophic. She descended into violent hysteria, screaming until the noise penetrated her brother's study, where he was composing correspondence for his steward. He rushed downstairs to confront a terrifying scene: Lucy had arrived, oblivious to the storm brewing around her. Your sister, in a rage, drove her into a fainting fit. Nancy collapsed to her knees in tears; your brother paced the floor, paralyzed by indecision. Mrs. Dashwood demanded their immediate departure, forcing your brother to kneel and beg for permission to pack their belongings. Only then did the hysterics subside, prompting a frantic summons for Dr. Donovan.

The carriage waited at the door. Lucy, barely able to stand, and Nancy, equally distressed, were ushered inside as the physician arrived. I confess, I harbor zero patience for your sister's reaction. Despite her fury, I remain convinced this union is inevitable. Consider the economics: Mrs. Ferrars can certainly provide for her son. While Lucy possesses negligible personal fortune, she demonstrates an unparalleled aptitude for resource management. With an allowance of five hundred pounds a year, secured by Mrs. Ferrars, I am confident they could maintain an appearance rivaling anyone living on double that sum. Imagine their potential domestic efficiency in a modest cottage, staffed by two maids and two men. Indeed, I possess a housemaid currently unemployed who would be perfectly suited to their needs."

Mrs. Jennings concluded her monologue, expecting a chorus of agreement or perhaps a display of maternal outrage. Instead, she encountered a momentary silence. Elinor, having collected her scattered thoughts, responded with the measured precision of a surgeon addressing a wound.

"I am gratified to observe that you no longer suspect my attachment to Edward," Elinor noted calmly. "Your recent assumptions regarding my feelings have ceased, which allows me to discuss this affair with the impartiality it requires. Given the absence of Marianne, I feel sufficiently composed to offer a judgment on the conduct of all parties involved."

Internally, Elinor wrestled with the uncertainty of the outcome. Though she desperately wished to envision a future where Edward and Lucy united, her primary concern was the reaction of Mrs. Ferrars and, more critically, Edward's own behavior toward her. She felt profound compassion for him, a sentiment she struggled to extend to Lucy, whom she viewed with minimal sympathy.

Recognizing that Mrs. Jennings could not sustain conversation on any topic other than the scandal, Elinor realized the necessity of preemptive disclosure. She must dismantle Marianne's illusions, acquaint her with the brutal reality, and prepare her to hear the news from others without interpreting it as evidence of Elinor's own lingering affection or resentment toward Edward.

This was a mission fraught with pain. Elinor intended to strip away what she believed to be Marianne's sole source of solace. She planned to reveal details of Edward that might permanently tarnish his image in Marianne's eyes, hoping to induce in her sister a reliving of her own devastation through a parallel of circumstances. Yet, the task was unavoidable.

Elinor approached the confrontation with detachment.
