She made no attempt to dramatize her own suffering, presenting herself instead as a model of the self-command she had cultivated since learning of Edward's engagement. Her narration was lucid and straightforward, devoid of violent agitation or impetuous grief. Such emotional volatility belonged solely to the listener. Marianne listened with horror, her composure fracturing into excessive weeping. Elinor assumed the role of comforter, offering assurances of her own mental stability and a vigorous defense of Edward against all charges save imprudence.

Initially, Marianne rejected both the facts and the defense. In her mind, Edward was a second Willoughby. Acknowledging, as Elinor did, that she had loved him with sincerity, Marianne questioned her own capacity for lesser devotion. Regarding Lucy Steele, she deemed her utterly unamiable and intellectually incapable of securing the affections of a sensible man. She refused to accept that Edward's past affection for her was natural, requiring only empirical observation of human nature to alter her perspective.

Elinor's initial communication established only the existence of the engagement and its duration. Marianne's emotions immediately disrupted the flow of detail, necessitating a shift to soothing her distress and mitigating her resentment. The pivotal inquiry came from Marianne: "How long has this been known to you, Elinor? Has he written to you?"

"I have known it for four months," Elinor replied. "When Lucy first arrived at Barton Park last November, she confided in me regarding her engagement."

Marianne's eyes widened, registering a shock her lips could not vocalize. After a pause filled with disbelief, she exclaimed, "Four months! Have you known of this for four months?"

Elinor confirmed the statement.

"While attending me in all my misery," Marianne accused, "this burden weighed on your heart? And I have reproached you for appearing happy!"

"It was inappropriate for you to know then how profoundly I was the opposite," Elinor stated firmly.

"Four months!" Marianne cried again. "So calm! So cheerful! How have you sustained yourself?"

"By adhering to my duty. My promise to Lucy necessitated secrecy. I owed her the avoidance of hints, and I owed my family and friends the prevention of unnecessary anxiety regarding my welfare."

Marianne appeared deeply affected. "I have frequently wished to undeceive you and my mother," Elinor added. "On occasion, I attempted it; yet, without compromising my trust, I could never have convinced you."

"Four months! And yet you loved him!"

"Yes. But I did not love only him. While the comfort of others held value for me, I was glad to spare them the knowledge of my internal turmoil. Now, I can reflect and speak of it with little emotion. I would not have you suffer on my account; I assure you, I no longer suffer materially. I possess numerous supports. I am not conscious of provoking this disappointment through any imprudence of my own. I have endured it as much as possible without disseminating it further. I acquit Edward of essential misconduct. I wish him happiness. Being certain of his adherence to duty, I am convinced that while he may harbor regret momentarily, he will ultimately reconcile himself to his position. Lucy lacks sense, yet that is the foundation upon which all virtue may be constructed. Ultimately, Marianne, despite the enchantment of a singular, constant attachment and the belief that happiness depends entirely on one specific person, such dependence is neither intended, fitting, nor possible. Edward will marry Lucy. He will wed a woman superior in person and understanding to half the female population. Time and habit will instruct him to forget that he ever deemed another superior to her."

"If such is your reasoning," Marianne countered, "if the loss of what is most cherished can be so readily compensated by something else, your resolution and self-command are perhaps less remarkable than I imagined. They fall more within my comprehension."

"I understand

The public acclaim for Edward's conduct was loud and enthusiastic, yet only Elinor and Marianne possessed the clarity to discern its true weight. They alone understood the narrow margin from which he had saved his honor, and the hollow consolation that remained to him—a mere internal acknowledgment of righteousness amidst the crushing loss of fortune and family. Elinor took pride in his unyielding integrity; Marianne, overwhelmed by pity for his suffering, granted him absolution for every transgression. Though the breach of trust between sisters was healed by this revelation, neither sought to dwell upon it in private. Elinor avoided the subject by principle, fearing that Marianne's fervent reassurances of Edward's enduring love would only anchor those feelings more deeply in her own mind, contrary to her desire to sever them. Conversely, Marianne's resolve crumbled under the weight of the comparison; conversing on the topic only intensified her self-loathing, highlighting the chasm between her own vacillating actions and Elinor's steadfast fortitude.

Marianne felt the sting of this disparity acutely, but not as a catalyst for redemption. Instead, it fueled a relentless tide of self-reproach, a bitter regret that she had never summoned the strength to act sooner. This realization offered only the agony of penitence, devoid of any hope for future correction. Her mental faculties were so eroded by despair that she deemed further effort impossible, a paralysis that only deepened her despondency.

For several days thereafter, no fresh news emerged regarding the turmoil in Harley Street or Bartlett's Buildings. Yet, Mrs. Jennings, armed with sufficient gossip to satisfy her curiosity without further investigation, had resolved from the outset to visit her cousins with inquiries of comfort. Only the unexpected influx of other guests delayed her departure until the third day following the initial disclosure.

This day proved to be a resplendent Sunday, the beauty of the spring sun drawing crowds to Kensington Gardens despite the calendar marking only the second week of March. Mrs. Jennings and Elinor ventured forth, but Marianne, aware that the Willoughbys had returned to town and haunted by the dread of encountering them, elected to remain indoors rather than risk exposure in such a public arena.

Shortly after entering the gardens, an old acquaintance of Mrs. Jennings joined them. Elinor welcomed the distraction, grateful that the woman engaged Mrs. Jennings in continuous chatter, leaving Elinor free for quiet contemplation. For a time, she observed nothing of the Willoughbys, nothing of Edward, and no one capable of stirring her emotions, whether through gravity or levity. Eventually, however, she was surprised to find herself accosted by Miss Steele. Though appearing somewhat bashful, Miss Steele radiated immense delight at the reunion. Encouraged by Mrs. Jennings' evident kindness toward her, Miss Steele temporarily abandoned her own entourage to merge with theirs.

Mrs. Jennings leaned close to whisper urgently, "Extract every detail from her, my dear. She will confess anything if you simply ask. I cannot leave Mrs. Clarke."

Fortune favored Mrs. Jennings' insatiable curiosity and Elinor's equally strong desire for truth, for Miss Steele possessed the peculiar trait of volunteering information without prompting. Without her, nothing would have been revealed.

"I am so delighted to see you," Miss Steele exclaimed, seizing Elinor's arm with familiar familiarity. "I needed to locate you above all others in the world." Lowering her voice conspiratorially, she added, "I presume Mrs. Jennings has heard the whole saga. Is she furious?"

"Not at all, I believe, with you."

"That is a blessing. And Lady Middleton—is *she* enraged?"

"It seems improbable that she could be."

"I am monstrously relieved. Good heavens! I have endured such torment! I have never witnessed Lucy in such a fury. She initially swore she would never trim a single bonnet or perform any service for me again, so long as she drew breath. But now she has completely recovered, and we are as cordial as ever. Observe, she fashioned this bow for my hat and inserted the feather last night. There now, *you* are about to mock me. But why shouldn't I wear pink ribbons? I don't care if it happens to be the Doctor's favorite hue. I am certain, for my part, I would never have deduced his preference if he hadn't explicitly stated it. My cousins have been relentlessly pestering me! I honestly struggle to determine which direction to gaze before them."

She drifted into a tangent where Elinor had no contribution to offer, prompting her to steer the conversation back to its original course.

"Very well, Miss Dashwood," she resumed triumphantly. "People may whisper whatever they please about Mr. Ferrars declaring he would not wed Lucy, but it is absolute nonsense, I assure you. It is a disgrace for such malicious rumors to circulate. Regardless of Lucy's personal sentiments, it was none of the public's business to assert certainty."

"I have never heard such suggestions implied," Elinor replied.

"Oh, truly? Well, they *were* spoken, clearly, by more than one individual. Miss Godby informed Miss Sparks that no rational person could expect Mr. Ferrars to abandon a woman like Miss Morton, possessing a fortune of thirty thousand pounds, for Lucy Steele, who owns nothing. I received this confirmation directly from Miss Sparks. Furthermore, my cousin Richard admitted that when the moment arrived, he feared Mr. Ferrars would retreat. When Edward vanished from our sight for three consecutive days, I was utterly bewildered. In my heart, I believed Lucy had resigned herself to defeat. We departed your brother's residence on Wednesday, and saw nothing of him throughout Thursday, Friday, and Saturday, unsure of his whereabouts. Lucy once attempted to write to him, but her spirits rebelled against the idea. However, this morning he appeared precisely as we returned from church, and the entire truth unfolded. He had been summoned to Harley Street on Wednesday, subjected to counsel from his mother and others, and had publicly declared before them all that he loved no one but Lucy, and that no one but Lucy would he consider marrying. Distressed by these proceedings, he mounted his horse immediately upon leaving his mother's house and rode into the countryside, somewhere or other. He remained at various inns throughout Thursday and Friday, deliberately attempting to regain his composure. After exhaustive deliberation, he concluded that, stripped of his fortune and possessing nothing, it would be cruel to prolong the engagement, as it would inevitably result in her ruin. With only two thousand pounds and no prospect of advancement—and considering his potential entry into the clergy, which would yield merely a curacy—they could not subsist. He could not bear the thought of her settling for less, so he implored her, if she shared his sentiment, to terminate the matter immediately and allow him to fend for himself. I heard him articulate this plainly. It was entirely for *her* sake, based solely on *her* circumstances, that he proposed separation, not out of any waning affection. I swear on my life he uttered not a syllable regarding weariness of her, nor any desire to marry Miss Morton or similar prospects. Naturally, Lucy refused to entertain such reasoning. She responded instantly—with considerable rhetoric concerning sweetness and love—you know, and all that sort of thing—Oh, la! one cannot recount such matters—that she harbored no intention whatsoever of withdrawing, for she could endure life with him on a trifling sum. Whatever meager amount he possessed, she would gladly accept it all, or words to that effect. Consequently, he was ecstatic, discussed their future plans at length, and they agreed he would enter holy orders immediately, postponing their marriage until he secured a benefice. At that juncture, I could hear no further, as my cousin summoned me from below to inform me that Mrs. Richardson had arrived in her carriage to transport one of us to Kensington Gardens. I was compelled to ascend and interrupt them to inquire if Lucy desired to accompany them, but she showed no wish to depart Edward's side. Thus, I hurried upstairs, donned a pair of silk stockings, and descended with the Richardsons."

"I fail to comprehend what you imply by 'interrupting them,'" Elinor remarked. "Were you not all situated in the same room?"

"No, indeed, not us. La! Miss Dashwood, do you genuinely believe people engage in courtship rituals when others are present? Oh, for shame!—You surely possess superior judgment. (Laughing artificially.) No, no; they were secluded in the drawing-room. All I perceived was by eavesdropping at the door."

"How!" Elinor cried. "Have you been recounting to me information acquired solely through spying? I regret knowing this beforehand; I certainly would not have permitted you to relay particulars of a conversation you ought not to have witnessed. How could you behave so unjustly toward your sister?"

"Oh, la! There is nothing improper in *that*. I merely stood at the threshold and heard what reached my ears. I am certain Lucy would have treated me identically; a year or two ago, during the confidences shared between Martha Sharpe and me, she never hesitated to conceal herself in a closet or behind a chimney-piece specifically to overhear our discourse."

Elinor attempted to pivot the discussion elsewhere, but Miss Steele proved incapable of suppressing the topics dominating her mind for more than a few moments.

"Edward speaks of proceeding to Oxford shortly," she noted. "Currently, he lodges at No.—, Pall Mall. What a vindictive woman his mother is, isn't she? Your brother and sister were not particularly kind either. Nevertheless, I shall refrain from criticizing them to *you*; and indeed, they did provide us passage home in their

Two months had elapsed since the Dashwood sisters arrived in town, yet Marianne's impatience to depart grew daily. She sighed for the crisp air, the absolute liberty, and the profound quiet of the countryside, convinced that if any location could restore her equilibrium, it must be Barton. Elinor shared this anxiety, though her resolve was tempered by a pragmatic awareness of the logistical hurdles inherent in such a lengthy journey—a constraint Marianne, caught in the thrall of romantic idealism, refused to acknowledge.

Elinor began to formulate a concrete plan, eventually broaching the subject with their hostess, Lady Middleton. Mrs. Jennings initially resisted with the fervent eloquence of genuine goodwill, reluctant to see her charges leave. However, a new proposal emerged: the Palmers intended to relocate to Cleveland for the Easter holidays. Charlotte Palmer extended a warm invitation for Mrs. Jennings and her guests to accompany them. While this arrangement technically delayed their departure further, it appealed to Elinor's sense of propriety. The invitation was delivered with such polished courtesy by Mr. Palmer himself—and bolstered by the marked refinement in his demeanor toward them following news of Marianne's distress—that Elinor accepted with alacrity.

When she relayed the decision to Marianne, the younger sister reacted with visceral agitation. "Cleveland!" she cried. "No, I cannot go there."

"You forget," Elinor interjected gently, "that its situation is not..."

"But it is in Somersetshire," Marianne interrupted, her voice trembling. "I cannot enter Somersetshire. No, Elinor, you cannot expect me to go there."

Elinor wisely refrained from debating the propriety of suppressing such raw emotions. Instead, she shifted the argument to practicalities, framing the move as the most efficient route to reunite them with their beloved mother. From Cleveland, mere miles from Bristol, the journey to Barton required only a single, albeit arduous, day. Their mother's servant could easily escort them, ensuring they would be home within three weeks. Given the sincerity of Marianne's love for her mother, Elinor reasoned, this tangible benefit would ultimately outweigh the girl's imagined terrors. Indeed, Mrs. Jennings, far from weary of her company, pressed them eagerly to join her return trip from Cleveland. Though Elinor remained steadfast in her design, her mother's consent was quickly secured, and arrangements were finalized. Marianne found solace in drafting a schedule of the remaining hours separating her from Barton.

"Ah! Colonel," Mrs. Jennings addressed him upon his first visit after the decision was made, "I do not know what you and I shall endure without the Miss Dashwoods. They are resolute on returning home from the Palmers. And how forlorn we shall be when I return! Lord, we shall sit and stare at one another as dull as two cats."

Perhaps Mrs. Jennings hoped this vivid sketch of impending ennui would prompt the Colonel to propose a solution to her loneliness. If so, her strategy bore fruit shortly thereafter. As Elinor moved to the window to measure a print for copying, the Colonel followed, engaging her in conversation with a look of distinct significance. The effect on the lady was palpable; though she maintained her honor by changing seats to avoid eavesdropping, positioning herself beside the pianoforte where Marianne played, she could not suppress her gaze from Elinor. The younger sister flushed, her agitation evident, her attention wholly consumed by the Colonel's words.

Later, during a pause in Marianne's practice, fragments of the Colonel's speech drifted to Mrs. Jennings' ears. He seemed to apologize for the inadequacies of his house. This confirmed her suspicions. Wondering at his modesty yet assuming it to be proper etiquette, she listened intently. When the Colonel finally paused, his voice calm but resigned, he declared, "I am afraid it cannot take place very soon."

Astonished and shocked by such an unloving sentiment, Mrs. Jennings nearly exclaimed, "Lord, what should hinder it?" but checked herself, settling for a silent gasp. "This is very strange! Surely he need not wait to be older." Yet, this apparent delay did not seem to wound the Colonel's fair companion. Upon concluding their discussion, Mrs. Jennings distinctly heard Elinor say, with a voice betraying her deep conviction, "I shall always think myself very much obliged to you."

Mrs. Jennings was delighted by this expression of gratitude, marveling that the Colonel could depart with such *sang-froid*, offering no reply to such a declaration. She had not anticipated her old friend to be such an indifferent suitor. In truth, however, the exchange had been entirely different.

"I have heard," the Colonel said with profound compassion, "of the injustice your friend Mr. Ferrars has suffered from his family. If I understand correctly, he has been entirely cast off by them for persevering in his engagement with a very deserving young woman. Have I been rightly informed? Is it so?"

Elinor confirmed it.

"The cruelty, the impolitic cruelty," he replied, his voice thick with feeling, "of dividing, or attempting to divide, two young people long attached to each other, is terrible. Mrs. Ferrars does not know what she may be doing—what she may drive her son to. I have seen Mr. Ferrars two or three times in Harley Street and am much pleased with him. He is not a young man with whom one can be intimately acquainted in a short time, but I have seen enough to wish him well for his own sake, and as a friend of yours, I wish it still more. I understand that he intends to take orders. Will you be so good as to tell him that the living of Delaford, now just vacant as I learned from today's post, is his, if he deems it worthy of acceptance? But perhaps, so unfortunately circumstanced as he is, it may be folly to appear to doubt; I only wish it were more valuable. It is a rectory, but a small one; the late incumbent did not make more than £200 per annum, and though it is certainly capable of improvement, I fear not to such an extent as to afford him a very comfortable income. Such as it is, however, my pleasure in presenting it to him will be very great. Pray assure him of it."

Elinor's astonishment at this commission was nearly as great as if the Colonel had actually proposed marriage. The preferment, which she had deemed hopeless for Edward only two days prior, was suddenly provided to facilitate his union. And *she*—of all people—was tasked with bestowing it. Her emotion was profound, though Mrs. Jennings had misinterpreted its source. Whatever lesser, less pure feelings might have contributed to her stirrings, her esteem for the Colonel's general benevolence and gratitude for his specific friendship were strongly felt and warmly expressed.

She thanked him with all her heart, praised Edward's principles and disposition with the accuracy they deserved, and promised to undertake the commission with pleasure if he truly wished to delegate such an agreeable office. Yet, she could not help thinking that no one could perform it better than he. It was an office from which, unwilling to inflict the pain of accepting an obligation from *her* upon Edward, she would gladly have been spared. But Colonel Brandon, driven by motives of equal delicacy, declined it as well. Still, his desire to have it conveyed through her hands was so evident that she made no further opposition.

Edward, she believed, was still in town, and fortunately, she had learned his address from Miss Steele. She could inform him of the opportunity later that day.

Once settled, the Colonel began discussing his own advantages in securing such a respectable neighbor. It was then, as Mrs. Jennings had suspected, that he mentioned with regret the smallness and indifference of the house. Elinor, dismissing the concern regarding its size, remarked, "The smallness of the house, I cannot imagine any inconvenience to them, for it will be in proportion to their family and income."

By this comment, the Colonel was surprised to discover that she viewed Mr. Ferrars's marriage as the certain consequence of the presentation. He did not suppose the Delaford living could support anyone of his station in marrying, stating so openly.

"This little rectory can do no more than make Mr. Ferrars comfortable as a bachelor; it cannot enable him to marry," he lamented. "I am sorry to say that my patronage ends with this; and my interest is hardly more extensive. If, however, by an unforeseen chance it should be in my power to serve him farther, I must think very differently of him from what I now do, if I am not as ready to be useful to him then as I sincerely wish I could be at present. What I am now doing indeed, seems nothing at all, since it can advance him so little towards what must be his principal, his only object of happiness. His marriage must still be a distant good; at least, I am afraid it cannot take place very soon."

Such was the sentence that, when misunderstood, so justly offended the delicate sensibilities of Mrs. Jennings. Yet, after recounting the true nature of the conversation between Colonel Brandon and Elinor while they stood at the window, the gratitude expressed by the latter upon their parting may perhaps appear, in general, neither less reasonably excited nor less properly worded than if it had arisen directly from an offer of marriage.

"Good heavens," Mrs. Jennings declared, her eyes crinkling with a sagacious, predatory gleam the moment the gentleman withdrew, "I do not ask you what the Colonel has been saying to you. Though, upon my honour, I *tried* to keep out of hearing, I could not help catching enough to understand his business. And I assure you, I have never been better pleased in my life. I wish you joy of it with all my heart."

Elinor bowed, her composure holding firm despite the storm of emotion raging within. "Thank you, ma'am. It *is* a matter of great joy to me; and I feel the goodness of Colonel Brandon most sensibly. There are not many men who would act as he has done. Few possess such a compassionate heart! I have never been more astonished in my life."

"Lord! My dear, you are very modest. I am not the least astonished in the world. I have often thought of late there was nothing more likely to happen."

"You judged from your knowledge of the Colonel's general benevolence; but at least you could not foresee that the opportunity would occur so very soon."

"Opportunity!" Mrs. Jennings repeated, her voice dropping to a conspiratorial whisper. "Oh! As to that, when a man has once made up his mind to such a thing, somehow or other he will soon find an opening. Well, my dear, I wish you joy of it again and again. If ever there was a happy couple in the world, I think I shall soon know where to look for them."

"You mean to go to Delaford after them, I suppose?" Elinor asked, a faint smile touching her lips.

"Aye, my dear, that I do, indeed. And as to the house being a bad one, I do not know what the Colonel would be at. It is as good a one as ever I saw."

"He spoke of its being out of repair."

"Well, and whose fault is that? Why doesn't he repair it? Who should do it but himself?"

Their conversation was abruptly cut short by the servant entering to announce the carriage at the door. Mrs. Jennings prepared to depart, yet lingered with the urgency of a hawk spotting prey.

"Well, my dear, I must be gone before I have had half my talk out. However, we may have it all over in the evening; for we shall be quite alone. I do not ask you to come with me; your mind is surely too full of this matter to care for company, and besides, you must long to tell your sister all about it."

Marianne had already fled the room before the exchange began.

"Certainly, ma'am, I shall tell Marianne of it; but I shall not mention it to anyone else at present."

"Oh! Very well," Mrs. Jennings said, her disappointment palpable. "Then you would not have me tell it to Lucy? For I intend to travel as far as Holborn today."

"No, ma'am, not even Lucy, if you please. One day's delay will not be material; and until I have written to Mr. Ferrars, I think it ought not to be mentioned to anyone else. I shall do *that* directly. It is of paramount importance that no time be lost with him, for he will, of course, have much to attend to regarding his ordination."

This declaration baffled Mrs. Jennings initially. Why write to Mr. Ferrars in such haste? Yet, a moment's reflection sparked a brilliant, mischievous idea. She exclaimed, "Oh, ho! I understand you. Mr. Ferrars is to be the man. Well, so much the better for him. Ay, to be sure, he must be ordained in readiness. I am very glad to find things are so forward between you. But, my dear, is this not rather out of character? Should not the Colonel write himself? Surely, he is the proper person."

Elinor did not grasp the initial premise of Mrs. Jennings's speech, nor did she deem it worthy of inquiry, replying only to the conclusion. "Colonel Brandon is so delicate a man that he rather wished anyone to announce his intentions to Mr. Ferrars than himself."

"And so *you* are forced to do it. Well, *that* is an odd kind of delicacy! However, I will not disturb you (seeing her prepare to write). You know your own concerns best. So goodby, my dear. I have not heard of anything to please me so well since Charlotte was brought to bed."

She departed, only to return instantly. "I have just been thinking of Betty's sister, my dear. I should be very glad to get her such a good mistress. But whether she would do for a lady's maid, I am sure I can't tell. She is an excellent housemaid and works very well at her needle. However, you will think of all that at your leisure."

"Certainly, ma'am," replied Elinor, barely registering the words, her anxiety focused solely on solitude. How she should begin—how she should phrase her note to Edward—was now her sole concern. The peculiar circumstances surrounding them complicated a task that would be trivial for any other pair. She feared saying too much or too little, sitting deliberatively over her paper, pen poised, until the room fell silent save for the scratching of ink.

Her concentration was shattered by Edward's sudden entrance. He had encountered Mrs. Jennings at the threshold on his way to leave his farewell card; she, after apologizing for her absence, had insisted he enter, claiming Miss Dashwood awaited him on urgent business. Elinor had just congratulated herself that writing was preferable to speaking when her visitor arrived, forcing her into this greatest of exertions.

Her astonishment and confusion were profound. She had not seen him since his engagement became public, and thus not since he knew she was aware of it. Combined with the weight of what she needed to convey, the encounter left her uncomfortably flustered for several minutes. Edward, too, was visibly distressed. They sat together in a state of promising embarrassment. Whether he had apologized upon entry, he could not recall; but deciding caution was safer, he formally excused himself as soon as he could speak, settling into a chair.

"Mrs. Jennings told me," he said, "that you wished to speak with me—at least, I understood her so—or I certainly should not have intruded in such a manner. Though at the same time, I should have been extremely sorry to leave London without seeing you and your sister, especially as it will most likely be some time before I have the pleasure of meeting you again. I go to Oxford tomorrow."

"You would not have gone, however," Elinor replied, regaining her composure and determined to dispel the dread quickly, "without receiving our good wishes, even if we had not been able to give them in person. Mrs. Jennings was quite right. I have something of consequence to inform you of, which I was on the point of communicating by paper. I am charged with a most agreeable office..." She breathed slightly faster than usual. "Colonel Brandon, who was here only ten minutes ago, has desired me to say that, understanding you mean to take orders, he has great pleasure in offering you the living of Delaford, now just vacant. He only wishes it were more valuable. Allow me to congratulate you on having so respectable and well-judging a friend, and to join in his wish that the living—it is about two hundred a year—were much more considerable, and such as might better enable you to... as might be more than a temporary accommodation to yourself—such, in short, as might establish all your views of happiness."

What Edward felt, unable to articulate it himself, remains unsaid by others. He *looked* all the astonishment such unexpected, unimagined news could inspire, uttering only two words: "Colonel Brandon!"

"Yes," Elinor continued, gathering resolution as the worst of the shock faded, "Colonel Brandon means it as a testimony of his concern for the cruel situation in which the unjustifiable conduct of your family has placed you—a concern which I am sure Marianne, myself, and all your friends must share; and likewise as a proof of his high esteem for your general character and his particular approbation of your behavior on this occasion."

"Colonel Brandon gives *me* a living! Can it be possible?"

"The unkindness of your own relations has made you astonished to find friendship anywhere."

"No," he replied, struck by sudden realization, "not to find it in *you*; for I cannot be ignorant that to you, to your goodness, I owe it all. I feel it—I would express it if I could—but, as you well know, I am no orator."

"You are very much mistaken. I do assure you that you owe it entirely, at least almost entirely, to your own merit and Colonel Brandon's discernment of it. I have had no hand in it. I did not even know, until I understood his design, that the living was vacant; nor had it ever occurred to me that he might have such a living in his gift. As a friend of mine, of my family, he may, perhaps—in fact, I know he *has*—still greater pleasure in bestowing it; but, upon my word, you owe nothing to my solicitation."

Truth obliged her to acknowledge a small share in the action, yet she was so unwilling to appear as Edward's benefactress that she admitted it with hesitation. This ambiguity likely fixed the suspicion in his mind that had recently taken root.

For a short time,

Edward carried his thanks to Colonel Brandon, then proceeded with a buoyancy that bordered on manic glee to Lucy. By the time he reached Bartlett's Buildings, his spirits were so elevated that she could assure Mrs. Jennings, who called upon her the following day with her congratulations, that she had never witnessed him in such high spirits in her entire life. Her own happiness was equally certain, and she joined Mrs. Jennings wholeheartedly in their anticipation of settling comfortably at Delaford Parsonage before Michaelmas. Yet, far from withholding the credit due to her, Lucy spoke of her friendship for both sisters with fervent gratitude, readily acknowledging their obligations to her and declaring that no exertion on Miss Dashwood's part, past or future, would ever surprise her, for she believed her capable of anything in the world for those she truly valued. Regarding Colonel Brandon, she was not only prepared to venerate him as a saint but was genuinely anxious that he be treated as such in all worldly matters; she desired his tithes raised to the maximum and secretly resolved to exploit his resources at Delaford—his servants, his carriage, his cattle, and his poultry—to the fullest extent possible.

It had been over a week since John Dashwood visited in Berkeley Street, and since that time, no attention had been paid to his wife's indisposition save for a single verbal inquiry. Elinor began to feel it imperative to pay her a visit. This obligation, however, clashed violently with her inclinations and lacked the support of her companions. Marianne, content merely to refuse attendance herself, became urgently insistent that her sister not go at all. Mrs. Jennings, whose carriage was perpetually at Elinor's disposal, harbored such a profound distaste for Mrs. John Dashwood that neither her curiosity regarding the recent revelations nor her strong desire to champion Edward's cause could overcome her reluctance to endure her company again. Consequently, Elinor departed alone to pay a visit she possessed little desire for, risking a tête-à-tête with a woman whom neither of the others held in such high regard.

Mrs. Dashwood was initially denied entry, but before the carriage could turn from the house, her husband emerged unexpectedly. He expressed great pleasure in meeting Elinor, claimed he had just intended to call in Berkeley Street, assured her that Fanny would be delighted to see her, and invited her inside. They ascended to the drawing-room, where nobody was present. "Fanny is in her own room, I suppose," he remarked. "I shall go to her presently; I am certain she will have no objection whatsoever to seeing you. Indeed, she will not. Especially now, there cannot be—but however, you and Marianne were always great favorites. Why would not Marianne come?" Elinor offered excuses for her sister. "I am not sorry to see you alone," he replied, "for I have much to say to you. This living of Colonel Brandon's—can it be true? Has he really given it to Edward? I heard it by chance yesterday and was coming specifically to inquire further."

"It is perfectly true," Elinor confirmed. "Colonel Brandon has given the living of Delaford to Edward."

"Really! Well, this is astonishing! No relationship! No connection between them! And now that livings command such prices! What was the value of this?"

"About two hundred a year."

"Very well. For the next presentation to a living of that value—supposing the late incumbent to be old and infirm, likely to vacate it soon—he might have secured, I dare say, four thousand pounds. And how did he fail to settle that matter before this person's death? Now, indeed, it would be too late to sell it, but a man of Colonel Brandon's sense! I wonder he should be so improvident in a point of such common, such natural concern! Well, I am convinced there is a vast deal of inconsistency in almost every human character. I suppose, however, on recollection, that the case may probably be this: Edward is only to hold the living until the person to whom the Colonel has actually sold the presentation reaches an age fit to take it. Yes, yes, that is the fact, depend upon it."

Elinor contradicted this assertion very positively, explaining that she had personally conveyed the offer from Colonel Brandon to Edward and therefore understood the exact terms under which it was granted, compelling him to submit to her authority.

"It is truly astonishing!" he exclaimed after hearing her explanation. "What could be the Colonel's motive?"

"A very simple one—to be of use to Mr. Ferrars."

"Well, well; whatever Colonel Brandon may be, Edward is a very lucky man. However, you will not mention the matter to Fanny. Although I have broken it to her and she bears it vastly well, she will not like to hear it discussed much. 'Mrs. Ferrars,' added he, lowering his voice to a tone befitting such an important subject, 'knows nothing about it at present, and I believe it will be best to keep it entirely concealed from her as long as possible. When the marriage takes place, I fear she must hear of it all.'"

"But why should such precaution be used? Though it is not to be supposed that Mrs. Ferrars can derive the slightest satisfaction from knowing her son has money enough to live upon—for that must be quite out of the question—yet why, upon her late behavior, is she supposed to feel at all? She has done with her son; she cast him off forever and caused all those over whom she had influence to cast him off likewise. Surely, after doing so, she cannot be imagined liable to any impression of sorrow or joy on his account; she cannot be interested in anything that befalls him. She would not be so weak as to throw away the comfort of a child and yet retain the anxiety of a parent!"

"Ah! Elinor," said John, "your reasoning is excellent, but it is founded on ignorance of human nature. When Edward's unhappy match occurs, depend upon it his mother will feel exactly as if she had never discarded him; and therefore, every circumstance that may accelerate that dreadful event must be concealed from her as much as possible. Mrs. Ferrars can never forget that Edward is her son."

"You surprise me; I should think it must nearly have escaped her memory by this time."

"You wrong her exceedingly. Mrs. Ferrars is one of the most affectionate mothers in the world."

Elinor remained silent. "We think now," said Mr. Dashwood after a short pause, "of Robert's marrying Miss Morton."

Elinor, smiling at the grave and decisive importance of her brother's tone, calmly replied, "The lady, I suppose, has no choice in the affair."

"Choice! How do you mean?"

"I only mean that I suppose, from your manner of speaking, it must be the same to Miss Morton whether she marries Edward or Robert."

"Certainly, there can be no difference; for Robert will now, to all intents and purposes, be considered the eldest son. As to anything else, they are both very agreeable young men; I do not know that one is superior to the other."

Elinor said no more, and John fell silent for a short time. His reflections concluded thus: "Of one thing, my dear sister," he said, kindly taking her hand and speaking in an awful whisper, "I may assure you; and I will do it, because I know it must gratify you. I have good reason to think—in fact, I have it from the best authority, or I should not repeat it, for otherwise it would be very wrong to say anything about it—but I have it from the very best authority—not that I ever precisely heard Mrs. Ferrars say it herself, but her daughter did, and I have it from her—that in short, whatever objections there might be against a certain—a certain connection, you understand me—it would have been far preferable to her; it would not have given her half the vexation that this does. I was exceedingly pleased to hear that Mrs. Ferrars considered it in that light; a very gratifying circumstance you know to us all. 'It would have been beyond comparison,' she said, 'the least evil of the two, and she would be glad to compound now for nothing worse.' But however, all that is quite out of the question—not to be thought of or mentioned—as to any attachment you know, it never could be; all that is gone by. But I thought I would just tell you of this, because I knew how much it must please you. Not that you have any reason to regret, my dear Elinor. There is no doubt of your doing exceedingly well—quite as well, or better, perhaps, all things considered. Has Colonel Brandon been with you lately?"

Elinor had heard enough, if not to gratify her vanity and raise her self-importance, to agitate her nerves and fill her mind; she was therefore glad to be spared the necessity of replying extensively and the danger of hearing anything more from her brother by the entrance of Mr. Robert Ferrars. After a few moments' chat, John Dashwood, remembering that Fanny was yet uninformed of her sister's presence, quitted the room in search of her, leaving Elinor to deepen her acquaintance with Robert. He, by the gay unconcern and happy self-complacency of his manner while enjoying such an unfair division of his mother's love and liberality to the prejudice of his banished brother—an advantage earned solely by his own dissipated course of life and that brother's integrity—was confirming her most unfavorable opinion of his head and heart.

They had scarcely been two minutes alone before he began to

The final exchanges between the siblings in town concluded with a single, ironic note: a faint invitation from Fanny to visit Norland—a destination so improbable it bordered on fiction—and a warm, private assurance from John that he would arrive promptly at Delaford. Yet, amidst these gestures, a singular pattern emerged that amused Elinor: every friend seemed determined to send her to Delaford, a place she now wished to avoid above all others. It was designated her future home by her brother and Mrs. Jennings, and even Lucy, upon their parting, pressed an invitation to reside there.

Very early in April, the two parties departed their respective residences in Hanover Square and Berkeley Street, converging by appointment on the road. The journey required more than two days due to the necessity of accommodating Charlotte and her infant, while Mr. Palmer, traveling with greater expedition alongside Colonel Brandon, intended to join them at Cleveland shortly after their arrival. Marianne, though she had spent few hours in comfort during her London tenure and yearned desperately to escape it, found the act of bidding farewell excruciating. She lingered over the house where she had last harbored the extinguished hopes and shattered confidence regarding Willoughby. Furthermore, leaving the estate where Willoughby remained, immersed in new engagements and schemes from which she was forever excluded, drew forth a flood of tears.

Elinor's departure, conversely, carried a distinct positivity. Her thoughts lacked objects for lingering regret; she parted from no creature whose absence would cause her sorrow. She welcomed the liberation from the relentless pursuit of Lucy's friendship, expressed gratitude for departing unseen by Willoughby following his marriage, and looked forward with genuine hope to the tranquility of Barton, confident it would restore Marianne's peace and solidify her own.

Their journey proceeded safely. On the second day, they crossed into the county of Somerset, a region oscillating in Marianne's imagination between cherished sanctuary and prohibited territory. By the forenoon of the third day, they reached Cleveland. The residence was a spacious, modern edifice situated on a sloping lawn, lacking a formal park but possessing extensive pleasure grounds. A smooth gravel road wound through an open shrubbery and closer wood walk, leading to the front, where the house itself was shielded by a dense screen of firs, mountain-ashes, acacias, and towering Lombardy poplars.

Marianne entered the house with a heart swelling with emotion, aware she was merely eighty miles from Barton and thirty from Combe Magna. Within five minutes, however, she had already departed, slipping away through the winding shrubberies—which were just beginning to display their spring beauty—to ascend a distant eminence. From a nearby Grecian temple, her gaze wandered across the vast tract of country to the southeast, resting fondly on the farthest ridges of the horizon, imagining Combe Magna visible from their summits. In these moments of exquisite, invaluable misery, she rejoiced in tears of agony to be at Cleveland. Returning via a different circuit, intoxicated by the happy privilege of rural liberty—the ability to wander in free, luxurious solitude—she resolved to dedicate nearly every hour of her stay to such solitary rambles.

She returned just in time to join the others as they exited the house for an excursion through its immediate premises. The remainder of the morning was easily occupied lounging around the kitchen garden, inspecting the blooms on its walls, and listening to the gardener's lamentations regarding blights, followed by a dawdle through the greenhouse. Here, the loss of her favorite plants, unwarily exposed to the lingering frost, elicited laughter from Charlotte. Finally, she visited the poultry yard, where the disappointed hopes of the dairy-maid—hens abandoning their nests, thefts by foxes, or the rapid decline of a promising brood—provided fresh sources of merriment.

The morning had been fine and dry, and Marianne had not anticipated a change in weather. Consequently, she was taken aback to find herself barred from venturing out again after dinner by a settled, persistent rain. She had planned a twilight walk to the Grecian temple and beyond; while a merely cold or damp evening might not have deterred her, a heavy, unyielding storm rendered the idea absurd. With the party small and the hours passing quietly, Mrs. Palmer attended to her child, Mrs. Jennings engaged in carpet-work, and they discussed their departed friends, arranged Lady Middleton's engagements, and speculated whether Mr. Palmer and Colonel Brandon would travel further than Reading that night. Elinor, though little concerned, joined the discourse. Meanwhile, Marianne, possessing the knack of locating the library regardless of the family's attempts to avoid it, secured a book.

Nothing was lacking on Mrs. Palmer's part that constant, friendly goodwill could provide to ensure they felt welcome. The openness and heartiness of her manner more than compensated for her occasional deficiencies in polite formality. Her kindness, underscored by a remarkably pretty face, was engaging; her folly, though evident, was not disgusting because it lacked conceit. Elinor could have forgiven anything but her laugh.

The two gentlemen arrived the following day for a late dinner, providing a pleasant expansion of the party and a welcome variety to the conversation, which a prolonged morning of continuous rain had otherwise reduced to monotony. Elinor, having observed so little of Mr. Palmer yet noted such variety in his address toward her sister and herself, was unsure what to expect in his domestic setting. She found him perfectly gentlemanly in his behavior to all visitors, though occasionally rude to his wife and mother-in-law. He proved capable of being a pleasant companion, hindered only by an excessive tendency to regard himself as superior to everyone else, particularly to Mrs. Jennings and Charlotte. Regarding his broader character and habits, Elinor perceived nothing unusual for his sex and era: he was fastidious in his eating, irregular in his hours, affectionate toward his child despite pretending to slight it, and wasted his mornings playing billiards instead of attending to business.

Overall, Elinor liked him much better than she had anticipated. In her heart, she was relieved she could like him no more—not sorry to be driven by observations of his Epicureanism, selfishness, and conceit to rest complacently on the memory of Edward's generous temper, simple taste, and diffident nature.

Intelligence concerning Edward—or at least some of his affairs—arrived via Colonel Brandon, who had recently visited Dorsetshire. Treating Elinor simultaneously as the disinterested friend of Mr. Ferrars and the kind confidante of himself, he spoke extensively of the parsonage at Delaford, detailing its deficiencies and outlining his plans to remedy them. His behavior in this matter, as well as in every other respect, justified Mrs. Jennings's conviction of his attachment. His open pleasure in meeting her after only ten days' absence, his readiness to converse, and his deference for her opinion would have sufficed, perhaps, had not Elinor, from the outset, believed Marianne to be his true favorite. This suspicion might have arisen naturally, but as it was, such a notion had rarely entered her head except through Mrs. Jennings's prompting. She prided herself as the superior observer of the two; while Mrs. Jennings focused solely on his outward conduct, Elinor scrutinized his eyes. When Mrs. Jennings failed to notice his looks of anxious solicitude regarding Marianne's unspoken symptoms of a heavy cold—manifesting in her head and throat—Elinor discerned therein the quick, needless alarm of a lover.

Two delightful twilight walks on the third and fourth evenings, traversing not merely the dry gravel of the shrubbery but the entire grounds, especially the most remote sections where the trees were oldest and the grass longest and wettest, ultimately proved detrimental. Assisted by the greater imprudence of sitting in wet shoes and stockings, these excursions induced a violent cold in Marianne. Though initially trifled with or denied, the ailment inevitably intensified, commanding the concern of everyone present and drawing attention from Marianne herself. Prescriptions poured in from all directions, as was customary, and were uniformly declined. Despite being heavy, feverish, plagued by limb pains, a cough, and a sore throat, she maintained that a good night's rest would cure her entirely. It took considerable effort for Elinor to prevail upon her, as she retired to bed, to attempt even one or two of the simplest remedies.

The morning sun rose on a scene of deceptive tranquility. Marianne rose at her accustomed hour, offering a brittle smile to every inquiry regarding her health, attempting to mask her frailty with the pretense of normalcy. Yet, the evidence contradicted her efforts: a day spent shivering beside the fire, clutching a book she could neither focus upon nor comprehend, or collapsing onto a sofa in a state of profound lethargy, spoke volumes against her claimed improvement. When she finally retreated to bed, increasingly indisposed, Colonel Brandon found himself astonished solely by Elinor's composure. Though Elinor had nursed her relentlessly throughout the day and administered harsh remedies against Marianne's will, she shared her sister's fatalistic reliance on sleep, dismissing genuine alarm with a stoicism that bordered on negligence.

However, the night that followed shattered this fragile equilibrium. A feverish, restless darkness dispelled all expectations of recovery. When Marianne, refusing to yield, eventually confessed her inability to sit upright and collapsed back into bed, Elinor seized upon Mrs. Jennings' counsel to summon the Palmers' physician. Dr. Harris arrived promptly, examining the patient with clinical detachment. While he encouraged Miss Dashwood with the assurance that mere days would restore her sister to health, his diagnosis carried a darker weight. By identifying a "putrid tendency" and casually invoking the term "infection," he triggered an instantaneous panic in Mrs. Palmer, who feared contagion for her infant son. Mrs. Jennings, who had harbored suspicions of the illness's gravity since the onset, grew grave upon hearing Dr. Harris's report. Validating Charlotte's fears, she insisted on the immediate evacuation of the household with the baby. Mr. Palmer, though initially dismissive of their hysteria, found his wife's anxiety and relentless pleading insurmountable. Consequently, her departure was finalized; within an hour of the doctor's arrival, she departed for a relative's estate several miles beyond Bath, taking her child and nurse with her. At her desperate request, Mr. Palmer pledged to follow shortly, and she implored her mother to accompany her. Yet, Mrs. Jennings, driven by a kindness that earned Elinor's deepest affection, declared her resolve to remain at Cleveland until Marianne's condition stabilized, vowing to fill the void of the absent mother through her own vigilant care. Elinor found herself inundated with her support, eager to share the burden and utilizing her superior nursing experience to mitigate the crisis.

Poor Marianne, drained by the virulence of her malady and acutely aware of her universal decline, could no longer cling to the illusion of imminent recovery. The knowledge that tomorrow—the day they were scheduled to depart for home, accompanied by Mrs. Jennings' servant to surprise their mother—would instead mark a catastrophic delay, intensified every ache. Her sparse utterances were laments of this inevitable postponement, though Elinor strove to lift her spirits, convincing her that the delay would be fleeting, a belief Elinor held firmly in that moment.

The subsequent day yielded no significant change. Marianne showed neither improvement nor deterioration, remaining static in her suffering. The household had diminished further; Mr. Palmer, reluctant to flee due to his inherent benevolence and a desire not to appear cowardly before his wife, was ultimately persuaded by Colonel Brandon to fulfill his promise. As he prepared to leave, Colonel Brandon himself voiced intentions to depart as well, exerting himself significantly to broach the subject. Here, Mrs. Jennings intervened with tactful wisdom. To remove the Colonel while his heart was torn apart by concern for his beloved sister would be to strip them of their sole solace. She explained that his presence was indispensable to her, proposing games of piquet in the evenings while Elinor tended to Marianne upstairs. Her plea, warmly echoed by Mr. Palmer—who found relief in leaving someone capable of guiding Elinor in emergencies—proved irresistible. Colonel Brandon, gratified by the opportunity to remain, could not sustain even a feigned objection.

Marianne remained ignorant of these shifting tides. Unaware that she had inadvertently precipitated the departure of the Cleveland household's masters within a week of their arrival, she felt no surprise at the absence of Mrs. Palmer, nor did she harbor concern, omitting the name entirely from her discourse.

Two days elapsed since Mr. Palmer's exit, and Marianne's condition remained stubbornly unchanged. Dr. Harris, attending daily, maintained his bold prognosis of swift recovery, and Elinor matched his sanguinity. However, the outlook for the others remained bleak. Mrs. Jennings had concluded early on that Marianne would never survive, and Colonel Brandon, whose primary utility lay in absorbing Mrs. Jennings' dire predictions, lacked the mental fortitude to resist their influence. He attempted to logically dismantle fears that the apothecary's contradictory judgments rendered absurd, yet the solitary hours stretching across each day fostered the intrusion of every melancholic thought. He could not banish the conviction that he would never see Marianne alive again.

Then, on the morning of the third day, the gloomy forecasts nearly evaporated. Upon Dr. Harris's arrival, he declared the patient materially improved. Her pulse strengthened considerably, and symptoms appeared markedly more favorable than the previous visit. Elinor, bolstered by renewed hope, radiated cheerfulness, rejoicing that her correspondence to her mother had reflected her own judgment rather than her friend's pessimism, minimizing the indisposition that had stalled their journey. She began to fixate on the date of Marianne's potential travel.

Yet, the day closed without the auspicious conclusion it had promised. Toward evening, Marianne relapsed, becoming heavier, more restless, and increasingly uncomfortable. Elinor, still optimistic, attributed the shift merely to the exhaustion of sitting up for her bed to be made. Administering the prescribed cordials, she watched with satisfaction as Marianne sank into a slumber, anticipating beneficial results. Although the sleep was not as tranquil as desired, it lasted a considerable duration. Determined to witness the outcome firsthand, Elinor resolved to remain by her side throughout. Mrs. Jennings, unaware of the sudden turn, retired unusually early; her maid, one of the primary caregivers, sought recreation in the housekeeper's quarters, leaving Elinor alone with Marianne.

The patient's repose deteriorated progressively. Elinor, watching with unceasing vigilance as her sister shifted positions constantly and noting the frequent, inarticulate murmurs of distress, found herself almost wishing to wake her from such agonizing dreams. Suddenly, jolted awake by an incidental noise within the house, Marianne sprang up with feverish intensity, crying out, "Is Mama coming?"

"Not yet," Elinor replied, masking her own terror while helping Marianne settle back down. "But I hope she arrives soon. It is a great distance from here to Barton."

"But she must not go via London!" Marianne shrieked, her voice hurried and frantic. "If she takes the road through London, I shall never see her!"

Elinor sensed with alarm that Marianne was losing her faculties. As she attempted to soothe her, she urgently checked the pulse. It was lower and quicker than ever before. Marianne's continued ramblings about her mother caused Elinor's alarm to escalate rapidly, compelling her to dispatch an immediate summons to Dr. Harris and send a messenger to Barton for their mother. The thought of consulting Colonel Brandon on how to facilitate the mother's arrival followed instantly. Having rung for the maid to replace her at the bedside, Elinor hastened to the drawing-room, where she knew he typically resided at a later hour.

There was no time for deliberation. Her fears and logistical challenges confronted him immediately. Regarding her fears, he possessed no courage or confidence to alleviate them; he listened in silent despair. Yet, her difficulties were instantly resolved. With a readiness that suggested a pre-meditated plan, he volunteered to serve as the messenger to fetch Mrs. Dashwood. Elinor offered no resistance that proved formidable. Thanking him with brief, fervent gratitude, she watched as he hurried to dispatch his servant with instructions to Dr. Harris and to arrange for post-horses directly. Meanwhile, she penned a few lines to her mother.

The comfort of such a steadfast ally in that critical moment—Colonel Brandon, or rather, the companionship he represented for her mother—was felt with profound gratitude. A companion whose judgment would steer, whose attentions would relieve, and whose friendship would soothe. In mitigating the shock of such a summons, his presence, his demeanor, and his assistance served as a buffer.

Meanwhile, regardless of his internal turmoil, Colonel Brandon acted with the firmness of a collected mind. He orchestrated every necessary arrangement with utmost dispatch, calculating with precision the timeframe for his return. No moment was wasted. The horses arrived ahead of schedule. Pressing Elinor's hand with a look of solemn gravity and speaking words too low for her to hear, he hurried into the carriage. It was approximately twelve o'clock. Elinor returned to Marianne's chamber to await the apothecary and tend to her sister through the remainder of the night. It was a period of nearly equal torment for both: hour after hour of sleepless agony and delirium for Marianne, countered by the most excruciating anxiety for Elinor, before Dr. Harris finally appeared. Once her apprehensions were ignited, they exacted a toll disproportionate to her prior sense of security. The servant who stayed with her, refusing to disturb Mrs. Jennings, only exacerbated her torment by hinting at the mistress's longstanding doubts.

Marianne's thoughts remained intermittently fixed, incoherently, on her mother. Every mention of her name inflicted a fresh wound upon E

"No room. Full up."

The door slammed with the dull, decisive thud of bureaucratic finality.

Elinor started back, her eyes widening in genuine horror at the sight of the man standing there. Before she could act on the first impulse of her heart—to turn instantly and flee—the movement was arrested by his rapid advance. He spoke in a voice that carried the weight of command rather than supplication.

"Miss Dashwood," he said, "for half an hour—for ten minutes—I entreat you to stay."

"No, sir," she replied with absolute firmness. "I shall *not* stay. Your business cannot be with *me*. The servants, I suppose, forgot to inform you that Mr. Palmer was not in the house."

"Had they told me," he cried with vehemence, "that Mr. Palmer and all his relations were consigned to hell, it would not have turned me from the door. My business is with you, and only you."

"With me!"—uttered in the utmost amazement—"Well, sir, be quick—and if you can, less violent."

"Sit down, and I will be both."

She hesitated, paralyzed by indecision. The terrifying possibility of Colonel Brandon arriving to find her in this company flashed through her mind. Yet, she had promised to hear him, and her curiosity, no less than her honor, was engaged. Concluding that prudence demanded dispatch and that her acquiescence would best facilitate his exit, she walked silently toward the table and sat. He took the opposite chair, and for thirty seconds, neither spoke.

"Pray be quick, sir," Elinor said impatiently. "I have no time to spare."

He sat in an attitude of deep meditation, seemingly deaf to her urgency.

"Your sister," he said abruptly a moment later, "is out of danger. I heard it from the servant. God be praised!—But is it true? Is it really true?"

Elinor remained silent. He repeated the inquiry with growing desperation.

"For God's sake tell me, is she out of danger, or is she not?"

"We hope she is."

He rose and paced across the room. "Had I known as much half an hour ago... but since I *am* here," he continued, adopting a forced vivacity as he returned to his seat, "what does it signify? For once, Miss Dashwood—it will be the last time, perhaps—let us be cheerful together. I am in a fine mood for gaiety. Tell me honestly," a deeper flush spreading across his cheeks, "do you think me most a knave or a fool?"

Elinor regarded him with greater astonishment than ever. She began to suspect he must be intoxicated; the strangeness of such a visit, coupled with such erratic manners, seemed intelligible only through that lens. With this impression, she immediately rose.

"Mr. Willoughby, I advise you at present to return to Combe. I am not at leisure to remain with you longer. Whatever your business may be with me, it will be better recollected and explained tomorrow."

"I understand you," he replied with an expressive smile and a voice perfectly calm. "Yes, I am very drunk. A pint of porter with my cold beef at Marlborough was enough to unsettle me."

"At Marlborough!" cried Elinor, increasingly bewildered by the illogical nature of his statements.

"Yes. I left London this morning at eight o'clock, and the only ten minutes I have spent outside my carriage since then procured me a luncheon at Marlborough."

The steadiness of his manner and the intelligence in his eye convinced Elinor that, whatever unpardonable folly had brought him to Cleveland, it was not intoxication. After a moment's reflection, she said:

"Mr. Willoughby, you *ought* to feel, and I certainly *do*, that after what has passed, your coming here in this manner, and forcing yourself upon my notice, requires a very particular excuse. What is it that you mean by it?"

"I mean," he said with serious energy, "if I can, to make you hate me one degree less than you do *now*. I mean to offer some kind of explanation, some kind of apology, for the past; to open my whole heart to you, and by convincing you that though I have been always a blockhead, I have not been always a rascal, to obtain something like forgiveness from Mary—from your sister."

"Is this the real reason of your coming?"

"Upon my soul it is," was his answer, with a warmth that evoked the former Willoughby in her memory, and in spite of herself, made her think him sincere.

"If that is all, you may be satisfied already; for Marianne *does*, she has *long* forgiven you."

"Has she?" he cried in the same eager tone. "Then she has forgiven me before she ought to have done it. But she shall forgive me again, and on more reasonable grounds. *Now* will you listen to me?"

Elinor bowed her assent.

"I do not know," he said after a pause marked by her expectation and his own thoughtfulness, "how *you* may have accounted for my behavior to your sister, or what diabolical motive you may have imputed to me. Perhaps you will hardly think the better of me—it is worth the trial however, and you shall hear everything. When I first became intimate in your family, I had no other intention, no other view in the acquaintance than to pass my time pleasantly while I was obliged to remain in Devonshire, more pleasantly than I had ever done before. Your sister's lovely person and interesting manners could not but please me; and her behavior to me almost from the first was of a kind—it is astonishing, when I reflect on what it was, and what *she* was, that my heart should have been so insensible! But at first I must confess, my vanity only was elevated by it. Careless of her happiness, thinking only of my own amusement, giving way to feelings which I had always been too much in the habit of indulging, I endeavored, by every means in my power, to make myself pleasing to her, without any design of returning her affection."

At this point, Miss Dashwood turned her eyes on him with the most angry contempt, stopping him by saying,

"It is hardly worth while, Mr. Willoughby, for you to relate, or for me to listen any longer. Such a beginning as this cannot be followed by anything. Do not let me be pained by hearing anything more on the subject."

"I insist on you hearing the whole of it," he replied. "My fortune was never large, and I had always been expensive, always in the habit of associating with people of better income than myself. Every year since my coming of age, or even before, I believe, had added to my debts; and though the death of my old cousin, Mrs. Smith, was to set me free; yet that event being uncertain, and possibly far distant, it had been for some time my intention to re-establish my circumstances by marrying a woman of fortune. To attach myself to your sister, therefore, was not a thing to be thought of; and with a meanness, selfishness, cruelty, which no indignant, no contemptuous look, even of yours, Miss Dashwood, can ever reprobate too much,—I was acting in this manner, trying to engage her regard, without a thought of returning it. But one thing may be said for me: even in that horrid state of selfish vanity, I did not know the extent of the injury I meditated, because I did not *then* know what it was to love. But have I ever known it? Well may it be doubted; for, had I really loved, could I have sacrificed my feelings to vanity, to avarice? or, what is more, could I have sacrificed hers? But I have done it. To avoid a comparative poverty, which her affection and her society would have deprived of all its horrors, I have, by raising myself to affluence, lost everything that could make it a blessing."

"You did then," said Elinor, a little softened, "believe yourself at one time attached to her?"

"To have resisted such attractions, to have withstood such tenderness! Is there a man on earth who could have done it? Yes, I found myself, by insensible degrees, sincerely fond of her; and the happiest hours of my life were what I spent with her when I felt my intentions were strictly honorable, and my feelings blameless. Even *then_, however, when fully determined on paying my addresses to her, I allowed myself most improperly to put off, from day to day, the moment of doing it, from an unwillingness to enter into an engagement while my circumstances were so greatly embarrassed. I will not reason here—nor will I stop for *you_ to expatiate on the absurdity, and the worse than absurdity, of scrupling to engage my faith where my honor was already bound. The event has proved, that I was a cunning fool, providing with great circumspection for a possible opportunity of making myself contemptible and wretched forever. At last, however, my resolution was taken, and I had determined, as soon as I could engage her alone, to justify the attentions I had so invariably paid her, and openly assure her of an affection which I had already taken such pains to display. But in the interim—in the interim of the very few hours that were to pass, before I could have an opportunity of speaking with her in private—a circumstance occurred—an unlucky

Elinor remained paralyzed by a cacophony of conflicting thoughts, a mental storm where sorrow was the only constant outcome, rendering her incapable of attending to her sister. Just thirty minutes prior, she had despised Willoughby as the epitome of male worthlessness; now, despite his glaring flaws, a profound pity for the suffering they caused had taken root. She viewed him as eternally severed from her family, feeling a tenderness and regret that weighed heavily on her conscience—not necessarily proportional to his virtues, but undeniably aligned with his desperate wishes. She recognized that his hold on her mind was fueled by factors reason demanded she ignore: his magnetic personal charm, his open and vivacious demeanor, and the burning, albeit morally questionable, passion he held for Marianne. Yet, she knew these influences were entrenched; long before she could logically dismiss them, the emotional imprint was permanent.

When she finally returned to the bedside of the unconscious Marianne, she found her awakening, revitalized by a sleep of such duration and sweetness that it matched the height of Elinor's own hopes. Elinor's heart swelled with emotion. The convergence of the past, the present, the future, Willoughby's departure, Marianne's safety, and her mother's imminent arrival created a surge of agitation that banished all signs of exhaustion, leaving her terrified only of exposing her inner turmoil to her sister. However, this fear was short-lived. Within half an hour of Willoughby's exit, the sound of another carriage summoned her downstairs. Driven by a desperate need to spare her mother further agony, Elinor sprinted into the hall, reaching the outer door just in time to catch and steady her mother as she stumbled inside. Mrs. Dashwood, whose terror upon approaching the house had nearly convinced her that Marianne was dead, possessed no voice for inquiry, not even for Elinor. Instead, Elinor, offering neither greeting nor question, immediately voiced the joyful relief. Her mother seized this news with her characteristic fervor, instantly overwhelmed by happiness as she had previously been by dread. Supported between her daughter and her friend, Mrs. Dashwood shed tears of joy, embracing Elinor repeatedly before turning to press Colonel Brandon's hand with a gaze that simultaneously expressed gratitude and the certainty that he shared in their bliss. He shared it, though in a silence deeper than her own.

Once Mrs. Dashwood regained her composure, her sole desire was to see Marianne. In two minutes, she was beside her beloved child, rendered dearer than ever by the ordeal of absence, unhappiness, and danger. Elinor's delight at witnessing the reunion was tempered only by the fear that it might rob Marianne of further rest. Yet, Mrs. Dashwood displayed remarkable prudence when a child's life hung in the balance. Satisfied by her mother's presence and aware of her own weakness, Marianne readily submitted to the enforced silence and quiet prescribed by the surrounding nurses. Mrs. Dashwood insisted on staying awake with her throughout the night; Elinor, complying with her mother's plea, retired to her own bed. However, the rest deemed necessary after a sleepless night and hours of exhausting anxiety was thwarted by irritability. "Poor Willoughby," she now permitted herself to whisper, became a constant fixture in her thoughts. She desperately wanted to hear his defense, oscillating between blaming herself for her earlier harsh judgment and acquitting her own conscience. Yet, the promise to relate this to her sister remained a torment. She dreaded the revelation, fearing its impact on Marianne, doubting whether her sister could ever find happiness again, and momentarily wishing Willoughby a widow. Then, recalling Colonel Brandon, she chastised herself, recognizing that the reward of her sister's heart belonged far more to Brandon's sufferings and constancy than to his rival's. She wished for nothing more than Mrs. Willoughby's survival.

The shock of Colonel Brandon's mission at Barton had been significantly mitigated for Mrs. Dashwood by her own preceding alarm. So consumed was her worry for Marianne that she had already resolved to depart for Cleveland that very day, settling her journey before Brandon's arrival, expecting the Careys to fetch Margaret at any moment, as her mother refused to take her where infection might lurk. Marianne improved daily, and the radiant cheerfulness of Mrs. Dashwood's appearance and spirits testified to her claim of being one of the happiest women in the world. Elinor could not hear these declarations or witness their proof without occasionally wondering if her mother remembered Edward. But Mrs. Dashwood, trusting Elinor's temperate report of her own disappointment, was swept away by the exuberance of her joy to focus solely on what would amplify it. Marianne was restored to her from a peril in which, as she now realized, her own misguided judgment in fostering the disastrous attachment to Willoughby had played a part. Furthermore, in her recovery, Mrs. Dashwood discovered another source of joy unknown to Elinor.

"As soon as we are alone," she confided the moment privacy arose, "you do not yet know all my happiness. Colonel Brandon loves Marianne. He has told me so himself." Her daughter, feeling alternately pleased and pained, surprised and unsurprised, offered only silent attention. "You are never like me, dear Elinor, or I should marvel at your composure now. Had I sat down to wish for any possible good for my family, I would have fixed on Colonel Brandon marrying one of you as the most desirable outcome. And I believe Marianne will be the happier of the two." Elinor was half-inclined to ask for the reasoning, satisfied that no argument based on impartial considerations of age, character, or feeling could satisfy her mother; but since her mother was inevitably carried away by her imagination on any intriguing subject, Elinor dismissed the query with a smile.

"He opened his whole heart to me yesterday during our travels," Mrs. Dashwood continued. "It emerged quite unexpectedly, quite unintentionally. I, you may believe, could speak of nothing but my child; he could not conceal his distress. I saw it equaled my own. Perhaps, thinking that mere friendship, as the world currently defines it, would not justify such warm sympathy—or perhaps not thinking at all—he yielded to irresistible feelings and made me acquainted with his earnest, tender, constant affection for Marianne. He has loved her, my Elinor, since the first moment he saw her." Here, Elinor perceived not the actual words or professions of Colonel Brandon, but the natural embellishments of her mother's active fancy, fashioning every detail to her liking. "His regard for her, infinitely surpassing anything Willoughby ever felt or feigned, as much warmer as it is more sincere or constant—however we choose to label it—has persisted through all knowledge of dear Marianne's unhappy infaturation with that worthless young man! Without selfishness, without encouraging false hope! Could he have seen her happy with another? Such a noble mind! Such openness, such sincerity! No one can be deceived by him."

"Colonel Brandon's character," Elinor stated, "as an excellent man, is well established." "I know it is," her mother replied seriously, "or after such a warning, I should be the last to encourage such affection, or even to be pleased by it. But his coming for me as he did, with such active, such ready friendship, is sufficient proof that he is one of the worthiest of men." "His character, however," Elinor countered, "does not rest on a single act of kindness, to which his affection for Marianne, were humanity absent, would have prompted him. To Mrs. Jennings, to the Middletons, he has been long and intimately known; they equally love and respect him. Even my own knowledge of him, though recently acquired, is considerable. I value and esteem him so highly that if Marianne can be happy with him, I shall be as ready as yourself to consider our connection the greatest blessing in the world. What answer did you give him? Did you allow him to hope?"

"Oh! my love, I could not then speak of hope to him or to myself. Marianne might have been dying at that moment. But he did not ask for hope or encouragement. His was an involuntary confidence, an irrepressible effusion to a soothing friend, not an application to a parent. Yet, after a time, I did say—for at first I was completely overcome—that if she lived, as I trusted she might, my greatest happiness would lie in promoting their marriage. Since our arrival, since our delightful security, I have repeated it to him more fully, giving him every encouragement in my power. 'Time,' I tell him, 'will do everything; Marianne's heart is not to be wasted forever on such a man as Willoughby. His own merits must soon secure it.'"

"To judge from the Colonel's spirits, however, you have not yet made him equally sanguine." "No. He believes Marianne's affection is too deeply rooted for any change under a great length of time, and even supposing her heart becomes free again, he is too diffident of himself to believe that, with such a difference in age and disposition, he could ever attach her. There, however, he is quite mistaken. His age is only slightly beyond hers, serving as an advantage by making his character and principles fixed; and his disposition, I am convinced, is exactly the one to make your sister happy. Moreover, his person and manners are all in his favor. My partiality does not blind me; he certainly is not as handsome as Willoughby; but at the same time, there is something much more pleasing in his countenance. There was always something in Willoughby's eyes at

The fever that had ravaged Marianne was not of a malignant strain, and bolstered by her youth, her innate vitality, and the devoted vigil of her mother, her convalescence proceeded with remarkable velocity. Within four days of Mrs. Dashwood's arrival, Marianne was strong enough to transfer to Mrs. Palmer's dressing room.
