It was there, driven by an impatience to offer personal thanks for the effort of bringing her mother, that she requested Colonel Brandon's presence.

Upon entering, the Colonel's reaction was visceral. Seeing the alteration in her countenance and grasping the pale hand she extended to him, his emotion transcended mere affection or the knowledge that it was observed by others. Elinor, observing the melancholy depth in his gaze and the shifting pallor of his complexion as he regarded her sister, deduced that the sight of Marianne—a living echo of Eliza—had triggered a cascade of past miseries in his mind. The hollow eye, the sickly skin, the posture of reclining weakness, and the warm acknowledgment of debt stirred memories of profound loss.

Mrs. Dashwood, though equally attentive, viewed the scene through a lens of pragmatic simplicity. She interpreted the Colonel's behavior as arising from basic, self-evident sentiments, whereas she convinced herself that Marianne's actions hinted at a burgeoning sentiment greater than simple gratitude.

Two days later, as Marianne's strength returned visibly every twelve hours, Mrs. Dashwood, yielding to shared desires, proposed moving to Barton. This decision anchored the plans of her friends; Mrs. Jennings could not abandon Cleveland during the Dashwoods' stay, and Colonel Brandon was swiftly persuaded to treat his residence there as equally fixed and necessary. In return, at the joint insistence of the Colonel and Mrs. Jennings, Mrs. Dashwood accepted the use of his carriage for the journey, ensuring better accommodation for her fragile child. The Colonel, delighted by the invitation from both women, pledged to repay the hospitality with a visit to the cottage within weeks.

The day of departure arrived. Marianne took a prolonged, earnest leave of Mrs. Jennings, filled with a gratitude born of secret remorse for her past neglects, and bid farewell to Colonel Brandon with the cordiality of a true friend. Assisted by him into the carriage—which he insisted she occupy at least half of—she watched as Mrs. Dashwood and Elinor followed. Left behind, the remaining guests discussed the travelers and their own dullness until Mrs. Jennings was summoned to her chaise for gossip with her maid regarding the loss of her young companions. Colonel Brandon then departed alone toward Delaford.

The Dashwoods spent two days on the road. Marianne endured the journey without significant fatigue. Every measure of zealous affection and solicitous care taken by her watchful companions yielded reward in her physical ease and spiritual calmness. For Elinor, witnessing this composure was particularly gratifying. Having seen her sister week after week suffocating under anguish she lacked the courage to speak of or the fortitude to hide, Elinor now rejoiced in an apparent serenity of mind. Trusting this state resulted from serious reflection, she believed it would inevitably lead to contentment.

As they neared Barton, entering landscapes where every field and tree held painful memory, Marianne fell silent and thoughtful. Turning her face away from their notice, she gazed earnestly through the window. Elinor felt neither wonder nor blame; when she saw Marianne cry upon alighting from the carriage, she perceived only a natural emotion, unobtrusive and worthy of pity. In her subsequent demeanor, Elinor detected a mind awakening to reasonable exertion. Hardly had they entered the sitting-room than Marianne scanned the room with a look of resolute firmness, seemingly determined to accustom herself to objects connected to the memory of Willoughby. She spoke little, yet every sentence aimed at cheerfulness. Though a sigh occasionally escaped, it was invariably followed by a smile. After dinner, she attempted to play the pianoforte. She approached the instrument, but her eye rested on an opera score procured by Willoughby, containing their favorite duets and bearing her name in his handwriting.

"That will not do," she declared. Shaking her head, she set the music aside. After running her fingers over the keys for a moment, she feigned feebleness and closed the instrument, firmly vowing to practice much in the future.

The following morning showed no abatement in these hopeful signs. Strengthened by rest, she looked and spoke with genuine spirit, anticipating Margaret's return and speaking of the restored family circle—their mutual pursuits and cheerful society—as the sole happiness worth wishing for.

"When the weather settles and I have recovered my strength," she said, "we will take long walks daily. We will walk to the farm at the edge of the downs to see how the children fare; to Sir John's new plantations at Barton Cross and the Abbeylands; and often to the old ruins of the Priory, tracing its foundations as far as recorded. I know we shall be happy. I know the summer will pass happily. I intend never to rise later than six, dividing every moment thereafter between music and reading. I have formed a plan and am determined to embark on a course of serious study. Our own library is too familiar for anything beyond amusement, but there are many works at the Park well worth reading, and others of modern production I can borrow from Colonel Brandon. By reading six hours a day, I shall gain in a year a vast amount of instruction I currently lack."

Elinor honored the nobility of this plan, though she smiled to see the same eager fancy that had once led her to languid indolence now working to introduce excess into a scheme of rational employment and virtuous self-control. Her smile faded into a sigh when she recalled the unfulfilled promise to Willoughby. She feared communicating this news might unsettle Marianne's mind and ruin this fair prospect of tranquil industry. Resolving to delay the inevitable, she decided to wait until her sister's health was more secure. Yet, the resolution was made only to be broken.

Three days after returning home, the weather finally permitted an invalid like Marianne to venture outdoors. A soft, genial morning appeared, tempting both daughter and mother. Leaning on Elinor's arm, Marianne was authorized to walk as long as she could without fatigue along the lane before the house.

Moving slowly, mindful of her recent frailty, the sisters advanced only far enough to command a full view of the hill behind them. Pausing, with her eyes fixed on it, Marianne calmly stated, "There, exactly there"—pointing with one hand to a projecting mound—"there I fell; and there I first saw Willoughby."

Her voice sank with the words, but quickly revived. "I am thankful to find I can look upon the spot with so little pain! Shall we ever talk of that subject, Elinor?" she asked hesitantly. "Or will it be wrong? I *can* talk of it now, I hope, as I ought to."

Elinor tenderly encouraged her openness.

"As for regret," Marianne continued, "I have done with that concerning *him*. I do not mean to discuss what my feelings *were*, but what they *are* now. At present, if I could be satisfied on one point—if I could be allowed to think he was not *always* acting a part, not *always* deceiving me; but above all, if I could be assured he was never so *very* wicked as my fears sometimes imagined, especially since the story of that unfortunate girl—"

She stopped. Elinor joyfully treasured her words, answering, "If you could be assured of that, you think you would be at ease."

"Yes. My peace of mind is doubly involved. It is horrible to suspect a person, who has been what *he* was to *me*, of such designs. But what must it make me appear to myself? In a situation like mine, what but a most shamefully unguarded affection could expose me to—"

"How then," her sister asked, "would you account for his behavior?"

"I would suppose him—Oh, how gladly would I suppose him—only fickle, very, very fickle."

Elinor said nothing. She debated internally whether to begin her narration immediately or postpone it until Marianne was stronger, and they crept on in silence for a few minutes.

"I am not wishing him too much good," Marianne sighed finally, "when I pray his secret reflections may be no more unpleasant than my own. He will suffer enough in them."

"Do you compare your conduct with his?"

"No. I compare it with what it ought to have been; I compare it with yours."

"Our situations have borne little resemblance."

"They have borne more than our conduct. Do not, my dearest Elinor, let your kindness defend what I know your judgment must censure. My illness has made me think. It has given me leisure and calmness for serious recollection. Long before I was recovered enough to speak, I was perfectly able to reflect. I considered the past: I saw in my own behavior, since the beginning of our acquaintance last autumn, nothing but a series of imprudence towards myself and a want of kindness to others. I saw that my own feelings prepared my sufferings, and that my want of fortitude under them almost led me to the grave. My illness, I well knew, was entirely brought on by my own negligence of health, which I felt even at the time to be wrong. Had I died, it would have been self-destruction. I did not know my danger until the danger was removed; but with such feelings as these reflections gave me, I wonder

Mrs. Dashwood received Willoughby's defense with a mixture of relief and sorrow. She rejoiced that he was cleared of the specific charge laid against him; she pitied his plight and genuinely wished him happiness. Yet, the past remained an immutable scar. No amount of vindication could restore Marianne's shattered faith, nor repair the irreparable damage to her character. Nothing could erase the memory of the agony Marianne had endured through Willoughby's machinations, nor absolve the guilt inherent in his treatment of Eliza. Consequently, he held no place in her daughter's former esteem, and Colonel Brandon's reputation remained untarnished. Had Mrs. Dashwood, like her daughter, heard Willoughby's tale firsthand—witnessed the raw desperation in his countenance and the intensity of his manner—her compassion might have been deeper. But it was neither within Elinor's power nor her desire to manufacture such emotions in another through her retelling. Reflection had tempered her judgment, sobering her view of Willoughby's worth; thus, she sought only to state the bare facts, exposing the truths intrinsic to his nature without the embellishments of tenderness that might cloud the mind.

In the evening, as the three sat together, Marianne voluntarily broached the subject again. It required significant effort; the restless, agitated thoughts that had plagued her for days surfaced in her rising flush and unsteady voice. "I wish to assure you both," she declared, "that I see everything—as you can desire me to do." Mrs. Dashwood would have interjected with soothing words, had not Elinor, wishing to hear her sister's unbiased assessment, signaled urgently for silence. Marianne continued slowly. "It is a great relief to me—what Elinor told me this morning. I have now heard exactly what I wished to hear." For a moment, her voice failed her, but regaining composure, she spoke with newfound calm. "I am now perfectly satisfied. I wish for no change. I never could have been happy with him, after knowing, as I must eventually have known, all this. I should have possessed no confidence, no esteem. Nothing could have altered those feelings."

"I know it—I know it!" cried her mother. "Happy with a man of libertine practices! With one who so injured the peace of the dearest of our friends and the finest of men! No—my Marianne has not a heart capable of finding happiness with such a creature! Her conscience, her exquisitely sensitive conscience, would have felt every ounce of the remorse a husband ought to feel." Marianne sighed and reiterated, "I wish for no change."

"You consider the matter," Elinor observed, "precisely as a sound mind and clear understanding must. You surely perceive, as I do, sufficient reason in this instance, and in many others, to conclude that your marriage would have entangled you in inevitable troubles and disappointments, poorly supported by an affection that was anything but certain. Had you wed, poverty would have been your constant companion. His extravagance is admitted even by himself; his entire conduct demonstrates that self-denial is a concept foreign to him. Your youthful inexperience combined with his relentless demands, operating on a meager income, would inevitably have precipitated distresses far more grievous precisely because they were unforeseen and unanticipated. Your innate sense of honor and integrity would undoubtedly drive you, upon realizing your situation, to attempt every form of economy imaginable. Perhaps, so long as your frugality reduced only your own comforts, you might have been permitted to persist; but beyond that point—and how little could your solitary management halt the ruin that had commenced prior to your union? Furthermore, had you attempted, however reasonably, to curtail *his* pleasures, is it not to be feared that, rather than persuading such selfish sentiments to consent, you would have diminished your own influence over his heart, causing him to regret the very connection that had ensnared him in such difficulties?"

Marianne's lips trembled as she repeated the word "selfish" in a tone questioning whether Elinor truly believed it. "Every aspect of his behavior," Elinor replied, "from the inception to the conclusion of this affair, was founded on selfishness. It was selfishness that first allowed him to toy with your affections; it was selfishness that, once his own were secured, delayed his confession; and it was selfishness that ultimately drove him from Barton. His own enjoyment, or his own ease, was his sole governing principle in every particular."

"It is very true. My happiness was never his object."

"At present," Elinor continued, "he regrets his actions. Why does he regret it? Because he discovers it has yielded no personal satisfaction. It has not made him happy. His circumstances are currently unburdened; he suffers no evils of that nature. He merely concludes that he has married a woman of a less amiable temperament than yourself. But does it follow that had he married you, he would have been content? The inconveniences would have differed. He would then have suffered under the financial hardships which, because they are absent, he now dismisses as trivial. He would have had a wife whose temper he could not complain of, yet he would have been perpetually necessitous—perpetually poor. He would likely soon learn to rank the countless comforts of a secure estate and substantial income as far superior to domestic bliss than the mere disposition of a spouse."

"I have no doubt of it," Marianne said. "And I have nothing to regret—nothing but my own folly."

"Rather attribute it to your mother's imprudence, my child," Mrs. Dashwood interjected. "*She* must bear the responsibility." Marianne would not allow her to proceed. Satisfied that both women recognized their respective errors, Elinor wished to avoid revisiting the past lest it further weaken her sister's spirit. Turning to the primary topic, she immediately resumed, "One observation may, I believe, be fairly drawn from the entirety of this story: that all of Willoughby's difficulties stem from the initial offense against virtue in his conduct toward Eliza Williams. That crime was the genesis of every subsequent transgression and the source of all his current discontent." Marianne assented most profoundly to this remark, prompting her mother to enumerate Colonel Brandon's sufferings and virtues with the warmth that friendship and intent could jointly dictate. Her daughter, however, appeared to listen with little interest.

Over the next few days, Elinor noted that Marianne did not regain strength as anticipated. While her resolve remained unyielding and she strove to appear cheerful and composed, Elinor trusted that time would heal her health. Margaret returned, and the family was once again reunited, quietly resettled at the cottage. Though they did not pursue their studies with the same vigor as during their initial stay at Barton, they planned a rigorous resumption of them in the future.

Elinor grew increasingly impatient for news of Edward. Since departing London, she had heard nothing of his plans, nothing certain regarding his current location. Letters had exchanged between her and her brother following Marianne's illness; in the first letter from John, this sentence appeared: "We know nothing of our unfortunate Edward, and can make no enquiries on so prohibited a subject, but conclude him to be still at Oxford." This was the sole intelligence concerning Edward provided by the correspondence, for his name was omitted from all subsequent letters. She was not destined, however, to remain ignorant of his movements for long. One morning, their manservant was dispatched to Exeter on business. Upon returning to the table and satisfying his mistress's inquiries regarding the outcome of his errand, he volunteered this communication: "I suppose you know, ma'am, that Mr. Ferrars is married."

Marianne gave a violent start, fixed her gaze upon Elinor, saw her turn pale, and collapsed into her chair in hysterics. Mrs. Dashwood, whose eyes, while answering the servant's query, had instinctively followed the same direction, was shocked to perceive through Elinor's countenance the depth of her suffering. A moment later, equally distressed by Marianne's condition, she knew not on which child to focus her primary attention. The servant, observing only that Miss Marianne had fallen ill, possessed enough sense to summon a maid, who, with Mrs. Dashwood's assistance, supported her into the adjoining room. By that time, Marianne was somewhat recovered. Her mother, leaving her to the care of Margaret and the maid, returned to Elinor. Although still greatly disordered, Elinor had regained enough use of her reason and voice to begin questioning Thomas regarding the source of his intelligence. Mrs. Dashwood immediately assumed the burden of inquiry, allowing Elinor to receive the information without the exertion of seeking it.

"Who told you that Mr. Ferrars was married, Thomas?"

"I saw Mr. Ferrars myself, ma'am, this morning in Exeter, along with his lady, Miss Steele as she was. They were stopped in a chaise at the door of the New London Inn as I went there with a message from Sally at the Park to her brother, who is one of the post-boys. I happened to glance up as I passed the chaise and saw directly it was the youngest Miss Steele. So I took off my hat, and she recognized me, calling out to inquire after you, ma'am, and the young ladies, especially Miss Marianne. She bid me convey her compliments and Mr. Ferrars's, their best regards and service, expressing how sorry they were they lacked the time to visit, noting they were in a great hurry to proceed further down for a short while, but promising that when they returned, they would ensure to call."

"But did she inform

Elinor now understood the crushing weight of certainty compared to the mere anticipation of disaster. While her mind had long accepted the inevitability of Edward's marriage to Lucy, a stubborn, irrational hope had persisted until the final moment—that some twist of fate, a sudden shift in his resolve, or a new opportunity for the lady would intervene to save them all. But the wedding was done. The news struck her with a force that condemned her own heart for the lingering flattery that had amplified the agony of the truth.

She was startled initially by the speed of the union; he was married before he could possibly be ordained, before he held the living that defined his future. Yet, logic quickly clarified the chaos. Lucy, driven by a frantic self-providence and a desperate haste to secure him, had undoubtedly overlooked every precaution except the risk of delay. They were wed in town and now racing toward her uncle's estate. Elinor tried to visualize the scene: Edward, within four miles of Barton, spotting her mother's servant, receiving Lucy's message. Soon they would be settled at Delaford—a place woven so tightly into her own interests that she simultaneously craved acquaintance with it and recoiled from its proximity.

In her mind's eye, she saw the parsonage house. She saw Lucy as the active, scheming manager, balancing a desire for smart appearances with extreme frugality, terrified of suspicion regarding her economy. She pursued her own agenda in every thought, courting the favor of Colonel Brandon, Mrs. Jennings, and every wealthy patron. As for Edward, she saw nothing distinct, nothing she wished to see. Whether happy or wretched, nothing pleased her; she turned her head from every mental sketch of him.

Elinor consoled herself with the belief that a connection in London would soon write to announce the event and provide details. Day after day passed, bringing no letter, no tidings. Though unsure who was at fault, she blamed every absent friend for being thoughtless or indolent. "When do you write to Colonel Brandon, ma'am?" the question sprang from her mind's impatience to find *something* happening. "I wrote to him, my love, last week, and rather expect to see, than to hear from him again. I earnestly pressed his coming to us, and should not be surprised to see him walk in today or tomorrow, or any day." This was progress, something to anchor her gaze upon. Colonel Brandon *must* possess information.

Scarcely had she solidified this determination when the silhouette of a rider on horseback arrested her attention at the window. He stopped at their gate. A gentleman. Colonel Brandon himself. Now she could hear the truth; now she trembled in expectation. But it was not Brandon. Neither his bearing nor his stature matched the memory. Could it be Edward? She looked again. He had just dismounted; she could not be mistaken—it *was* Edward.

She moved away and sat down. "He comes from Mr. Pratt's purposely to see us. I *will* be calm; I *will* be mistress of myself." Instantly, she realized the others shared her confusion. She saw her mother and Marianne flush, glance at her, and whisper urgently. She would have given anything to speak, to assure them that no coolness, no slight, would mar their behavior toward him—but she possessed no utterance and was forced to surrender the matter entirely to their discretion. No syllable passed aloud. They all waited in silence for the arrival of the visitor.

His footsteps echoed along the gravel path; moments later, he appeared in the passage, and shortly after, stood before them. His countenance, as he entered, was not even remotely happy for Elinor. His complexion was pale with agitation, his expression suggesting a fear of his reception and a consciousness that he deserved none. Mrs. Dashwood, trusting implicitly in the judgment of that daughter whom she currently intended to guide her in all things, met him with a look of forced complacency, offered her hand, and wished him joy. He colored, stammering an unintelligible reply. Elinor's lips had moved in unison with her mother's, and when the ritual concluded, she wished desperately that she had shaken his hand too. But it was too late. With a countenance striving to remain open, she sat down again and discussed the weather.

Marianne had retreated as far as possible from view to hide her distress; Margaret, grasping only fragments of the case, deemed it incumbent upon her to maintain dignity, so she seated herself as distant from him as possible and kept a strict silence. When Elinor finally ceased feigning rejoicing in the dryness of the season, an awful, suffocating pause descended upon the room. Mrs. Dashwood broke it, feeling obliged to hope he had left Mrs. Ferrars well. In a hurried manner, he affirmed it. Another silence stretched taut.

Resolving to exert herself, though dreading the sound of her own voice, Elinor asked, "Is Mrs. Ferrars at Longstaple?"

"At Longstaple!" he replied, with an air of genuine surprise.

"No," Elinor corrected, picking up some work from the table, "I meant to inquire for Mrs. *Edward* Ferrars." She dared not look up, but her mother and Marianne both turned their eyes sharply onto him. He flushed, seemed perplexed, looked doubtful, and after a hesitation, said, "Perhaps you mean—my brother—you mean Mrs.—Mrs. *Robert* Ferrars."

"Mrs. Robert Ferrars!" Marianne and her mother repeated in accents of utmost amazement. Though Elinor could not speak, her eyes were fixed on him with the same impatient wonder. He rose from his seat, walked to the window as if unsure of his next move, picked up a pair of scissors lying nearby, and began slicing the sheath to pieces as he spoke in a hurried voice.

"Perhaps you do not know: you may not have heard that my brother is lately married to—to the youngest—to Miss Lucy Steele."

His words were echoed with unspeakable astonishment by everyone present except Elinor. She sat with her head bowed over her work, in a state of such violent agitation that she barely recognized her surroundings.

"Yes," he continued, "they were married last week, and are now at Dawlish."

Elinor could endure it no longer. She almost ran from the room. As soon as the door closed behind her, she burst into tears of joy, weeping so violently at first that she feared she would never stop. Edward, who had been looking everywhere else rather than at her, saw her hurry away. Perhaps he saw—or even heard—her emotion; for immediately afterward, he fell into a reverie that no remark, no inquiry, no affectionate address from Mrs. Dashwood could penetrate. Finally, without saying a word, he quit the room and walked out toward the village, leaving the others in the greatest astonishment and perplexity regarding a change in his situation so wonderful and so sudden. They had no means of lessening this confusion but through their own wild conjectures.

Edward was free. The circumstances surrounding his release from the Ferrars household might seem inexplicable to the Dashwood family, yet the trajectory of his next move was inevitable. Having survived the trauma of one imprudent engagement contracted without maternal approval—a mistake he had endured for four years—he could not possibly resist the gravitational pull toward another. His mission to Barton was singular and direct: to propose to Elinor. Given his prior experience with such declarations, it was peculiar that he should feel so unnerved, requiring both fresh air and renewed courage to execute the task.

Yet, the speed with which he forged his resolve, the precision of his approach, and the reception he garnered require no detailed chronicle. By the time the family gathered at the table at four o'clock, nearly three hours after his arrival, Edward had secured Elinor's hand, obtained his mother's reluctant consent, and emerged not merely as a rhapsodic lover, but as a man restored to the pinnacle of human felicity. His situation was uniquely triumphant. He possessed the double victory of accepted love and liberation from a previous entanglement that had long constituted his misery. He had shed a woman he no longer loved, elevating himself instantly to a security he had once deemed impossible, transforming a state of despair into one of profound contentment. The shift was absolute: from the depths of suffering to the heights of joy, a metamorphosis evident in a cheerfulness so genuine and overflowing that his friends had never witnessed it before.

With his heart now fully open to Elinor, he confessed every weakness and error, treating his youthful infatuation with Lucy Steele with the philosophical detachment of a man twenty-four years old.

"It was a foolish, idle inclination," he admitted, "born of ignorance and idleness. Had my mother provided me with an active profession when I was removed from Mr. Pratt's care at eighteen, I am certain this would never have occurred. Although I departed Longstaple with what I then considered an unconquerable passion for his niece, any occupation, any object demanding my time and keeping me distant from her for mere months, would have allowed me to outgrow the fantasy. Mixing more freely with the world, as I surely would have done, would have dissolved the attachment quickly. Instead, I returned home to complete idleness. For the first twelve months following, I lacked even the nominal status of a university student, having not entered Oxford until I was nineteen. With nothing to occupy me but the delusion of love, and lacking comfort at home, companionship in my brother, or the ability to tolerate new acquaintances, it was natural to retreat to Longstaple, where I felt at home and guaranteed a welcome. Consequently, from eighteen to nineteen, I spent the majority of my time there. Lucy appeared the epitome of amiability and obligation. She was pretty—at least, I perceived her so *then*—and having seen so little of other women, I possessed no basis for comparison and thus failed to discern any defects. Considering the totality of factors, I trust that while our engagement was foolish and has since proven erroneous in every aspect, it was not, at the time, an unnatural or inexcusable act of folly."

The transformation wrought in the minds and hearts of the Dashwoods over those few hours was so profound it promised them all a sleepless night of celebration. Mrs. Dashwood, overwhelmed by joy, struggled to find words sufficient to express her love for Edward or her praise for Elinor. She wrestled with the paradox of thanking Edward for his release without wounding his sensibilities, and balancing the desire for private conversation between the couple with her own wish to observe their union.

Marianne could articulate her happiness only through tears. Regret and comparison inevitably surfaced; her joy, though sincere and rooted in her devotion to her sister, was of a nature that robbed her of spirit and language.

But Elinor—how are her feelings to be described? From the moment she learned of Lucy's marriage and Edward's freedom, until the instant he validated the hopes that had risen so swiftly, she oscillated through every emotion save tranquility. Yet, once the initial shock subsided, and she realized that every doubt and anxiety had vanished, comparing her current situation to the recent nightmare, seeing him honorably freed from his former bond and instantly turning his attentions to her with a tenderness and constancy she had always suspected, she was overcome by her own felicity. Human minds are naturally adaptable to improvement, but it took several hours to settle her spirits and grant her heart a degree of peace.

Edward intended to remain at the cottage for at least a week. Whatever other obligations awaited him, it was impossible to allocate less than seven days to the enjoyment of Elinor's company, insufficient to exhaust the topics of the past, present, and future. While a few hours of relentless discourse might cover more ground between rational strangers, lovers operate differently. Between them, no subject is truly concluded, no communication fully made, until it has been articulated at least twenty times over.

Lucy's marriage, a source of ceaseless and reasonable wonder for everyone, became one of the earliest topics of discussion between the lovers. Elinor's intimate knowledge of both parties rendered the event, in her view, one of the most extraordinary and inexplicable occurrences she had ever encountered. How could they have been united? By what magnetic force was Robert drawn to marry a girl whose beauty he had previously dismissed without admiration? A girl already betrothed to his brother, and whose existence had caused that brother to be rejected by his own family? It was beyond her comprehension. To her heart, it was delightful; to her imagination, absurd; but to her reason, it was an unsolvable puzzle.

Edward could only offer a tentative explanation: perhaps, upon their accidental first meeting, the vanity of one had been so inflamed by the flattery of the other that it gradually led to the rest. Elinor recalled what Robert had confided to her in Harley Street regarding his belief that his mediation, had it been applied earlier, might have altered the outcome. She relayed this to Edward.

"That was precisely like Robert," he observed immediately. "And that notion might well have occupied his mind when the acquaintance between them first began. Perhaps Lucy initially sought only to procure his good offices in my favor. Other designs undoubtedly arose later."

However, he was equally baffled as Elinor regarding the duration of their secret correspondence. At Oxford, where he had resided voluntarily since leaving London, he had no channel for news of her except her own letters, which remained as frequent and affectionate as ever. No suspicion had ever prepared him for the revelation. When the letter finally burst upon him, he believed he had been half-stupified for some time, caught between wonder, horror, and the sheer joy of such deliverance. He handed the letter to Elinor.

"DEAR SIR, 'Being very sure I have long lost your affections, I have thought myself at liberty to bestow my own on another, and have no doubt of being as happy with him as I once used to think I might be with you; but I scorn to accept a hand while the heart was another's. Sincerely wish you happy in your choice, and it shall not be my fault if we are not always good friends, as our near relationship now makes proper. I can safely say I owe you no ill-will, and am sure you will be too generous to do us any ill offices. Your brother has gained my affections entirely, and as we could not live without one another, we are just returned from the altar, and are now on our way to Dawlish for a few weeks, which place your dear brother has great curiosity to see, but thought I would first trouble you with these few lines, and shall always remain,

'Your sincere well-wisher, friend, and sister, 'LUCY FERRARS.'

'I have burnt all your letters, and will return your picture the first opportunity. Please to destroy my scrawls—but the ring with my hair you are very welcome to keep.'"

Elinor read the missive and returned it without comment.

"I will not ask your opinion of it as a composition," Edward remarked. "For worlds, I would not have had a letter of hers seen by *you* in former days. In a sister, it is bad enough, but in a wife! How I have blushed over the pages of her writing! And I believe I may say that since the first half-year of our foolish business, this is the only letter I ever received from her wherein the substance made me any amends for the defect of the style."

"However it may have come about," Elinor said after a pause, "they are certainly married. And your mother has brought upon herself a most appropriate punishment. The independence she settled on Robert, born of resentment against you, has placed it in his power to make his own choice; and she has actually bribed one son with a thousand-a-year to perform the very deed she disinherited the other for attempting. She will hardly be less hurt, I suppose, by Robert's marrying Lucy than she would have been by your marrying her."

"She will be more hurt," Edward countered. "Robert was always her favorite. She will be more devastated, and on the same principle, she will forgive him much sooner."

Edward knew the exact state of affairs between the families, as no communication with his relatives had yet been initiated. He had departed Oxford within forty-eight hours of receiving Lucy's letter, driven solely by the urge to reach Barton via the nearest road, leaving him no leisure to formulate a broader plan of action. He could do nothing until he was assured of his fate with Miss Dashwood. By the rapidity with which he sought that destiny, it was assumed that, despite his

Edward Ferrars was finally admitted to his mother's presence, emerging victorious from a battle of wills that had lasted far too long. Mrs. Ferrars, having deployed a defense of such ferocity and steadiness, successfully preserved herself from the stigma of being "too amiable," a label she seemed perpetually terrified of acquiring. Consequently, she pronounced him once more her son. Yet, the Ferrars household remained a landscape of volatile tides; for years she had nurtured two sons, only for the recent annihilation of Edward to rob her of one, and the prior death of Robert to leave her bereft of both. Now, with Edward resuscitated in spirit if not in health, she possessed a single heir again. However, Edward's sense of security remained precarious until he disclosed his engagement. He reasoned that the mere publication of his intent to marry Miss Dashwood might precipitate a fatal collapse in his constitution, carrying him off as swiftly as his previous misfortunes. Thus, with apprehensive caution, he revealed the truth, met with a calmness that exceeded his expectations.

Mrs. Ferrars initially attempted to dissuade him, marshaling every argument available to her. She posited that Miss Morton represented a superior option—a woman of higher rank and greater fortune. She reinforced this assertion with a brutal arithmetic of status: Miss Morton was the daughter of a nobleman possessing thirty thousand pounds, whereas Miss Dashwood was merely the offspring of a private gentleman holding a paltry three thousand. When she observed that Edward, while acknowledging the factual accuracy of her representation, showed no inclination to be guided by it, she deemed further resistance futile. Drawing upon the bitter lessons of the past, she submitted. After an ungracious delay necessitated by her own wounded dignity, designed to preempt any suspicion of genuine goodwill, she issued her decree of consent to the union of Edward and Elinor.

The subsequent consideration of financial augmentation yielded disappointing results. Although Edward was now her sole surviving son, he was not her eldest; Robert remained the primary beneficiary, inevitably endowed with a thousand pounds a year. No objections were raised against Edward accepting the clerical path for a modest stipend of two hundred and fifty pounds at most. Beyond the ten thousand pounds already advanced with Fanny, nothing was promised for the present or future. This sum, however, satisfied Edward and Elinor, exceeding their expectations. Even Mrs. Ferrars, through her evasive maneuvers, appeared the only party genuinely surprised by her stinginess.

With their income secured, the couple awaited only the completion of the parsonage at Barton, a project Colonel Brandon pursued with an eager desire to accommodate Elinor. As usual, the construction suffered from the unaccountable dilatoriness of the workmen, plagued by a thousand disappointments and delays. Eventually, Elinor breached her firm resolution to postpone marriage until the house was ready. The ceremony took place in Barton church early in the autumn. Their first month was spent at the Mansion-house, allowing them to supervise the finishing touches of the parsonage, select wallpapers, design shrubberies, and plan the layout of the grounds. By Michaelmas, Mrs. Jennings arrived to visit them, confirming her prophecies with remarkable accuracy. She found in Elinor and her husband what she truly believed to be one of the happiest couples in the world. Indeed, they lacked nothing save the marriage of Colonel Brandon and Marianne, and perhaps slightly better pasture for their cows.

Upon their settlement, they were besieged by visitors. Almost all relations and friends paid homage. Mrs. Ferrars descended to inspect the happiness she had reluctantly authorized, treating them with a facade of decent affection that never masked her true disdain. That favor was reserved exclusively for Robert and his wife, whose rise to prominence was swift and total. Lucy Steele's behavior in this affair stands as a testament to the efficacy of relentless self-interest. Her respectful humility, assiduous attentions, and endless flattery, once a small opening permitted, reconciled Mrs. Ferrars to Robert's choice and re-established him completely in her mother's grace. While Edward was never cordially forgiven for his intention to marry Lucy, and Elinor, despite her superior fortune and birth, was regarded as an intruder, Lucy was elevated to the status of a favorite child. She became indispensable to Mrs. Ferrars, receiving liberal assistance and maintaining harmonious relations with the Dashwoods, barring the inevitable jealousies between Fanny and Lucy and the occasional domestic friction between her own husband and herself.

Robert's ascent was built on a foundation of deception. When he first courted Lucy in Bartlett's Buildings, his stated aim was merely to persuade her to abandon her engagement to Edward. Expecting that a few interviews would suffice to overcome their mutual affection, he erred in his estimation. Lucy required repeated assurances; doubts lingered in her mind until another half-hour of discourse could dispel them. This demand for constant attendance secured his presence, and the rest followed logically. Gradually, the conversation shifted from Edward to Robert, a topic on which he possessed infinite material. Soon, it became evident that he had entirely supplanted his brother. He was proud of his conquest, proud of having tricked Edward, and very proud of his clandestine marriage without maternal consent.

Following this, they enjoyed several months of profound happiness at Dawlish, where Lucy cultivated her extensive network of relations and Robert drafted plans for magnificent cottages. Returning to town, Robert secured his mother's forgiveness through the simple expedient of asking for it—an act initiated by Lucy's strategic counsel. Initially, this pardon extended only to Robert. Lucy, owing no filial duty to her mother and thus committing no transgression, remained unpardoned for some weeks. However, her persistent display of humility, self-condemnation regarding Robert's offense, and feigned gratitude for her mother's perceived unkindness eventually procured the haughty notice that overwhelmed her with graciousness. Within a short time, she ascended to the highest state of affection and influence.

Lucy became as essential to Mrs. Ferrars as either Robert or Fanny. Meanwhile, Edward, though never fully absolved of his past error, displayed a ready discharge of his duties, an increasing attachment to his wife and home, and a regular cheerfulness of spirit, suggesting he was no less content with his lot than Robert. Elinor's marriage divided her from her family as little as circumstances permitted; her mother and sisters spent more than half their time at Barton. Mrs. Dashwood's frequent visits to Delaford were driven by both policy and pleasure; her desire to bring Marianne and Colonel Brandon together was a cherished objective, arguably more fervent than John's earlier suggestions.

Marianne Dashwood was destined for an extraordinary fate. Born to discover the falsity of her own opinions, she was compelled to counteract her most favored maxims through her conduct. She was born to overcome an affection formed late in life—at seventeen, based solely on strong esteem and lively friendship—and voluntarily offer her hand to another. That other was a man who had suffered no less than herself under the events of a former attachment, whom she had previously deemed too old to marry, and who still clung to the constitutional safeguard of a flannel waistcoat. Yet, so it was. Instead of succumbing to an irresistible passion or retiring into solitude, she found herself at nineteen submitting to new attachments, entering new duties, and becoming the mistress of a family and the patroness of a village.

Colonel Brandon, in Marianne, found consolation for every past affliction. Her regard and society restored his mind to animation and his spirits to cheerfulness. Equally, Marianne discovered her own happiness in forming his. Willoughby could not hear of her marriage without a pang; his punishment was completed shortly thereafter by the voluntary forgiveness of Mrs. Smith. By attributing her clemency to Willoughby's marriage to a woman of character, she provided him with the reason to believe that had he behaved honorably toward Marianne, he might have achieved both happiness and wealth. His repentance was undoubtedly sincere, and he long viewed Colonel Brandon with envy and Marianne with regret. Yet, he was not forever inconsolable, nor did he flee society or die of a broken heart. He lived to exert himself and frequently to enjoy life, finding domestic felicity in breeding horses and dogs and various sports. Nevertheless, for Marianne, he retained a decided regard, considering her his secret standard of perfection in womanhood; many a rising beauty was subsequently slighted by him as bearing no comparison to Mrs. Brandon.

Mrs. Dashwood wisely remained at the cottage, avoiding a move to Delaford. Fortunately for Sir John and Mrs. Jennings, when Marianne departed, Margaret had reached an age highly suitable for dancing and not ineligible for the presumption of having a lover. Between Barton and Delaford, a constant communication flowed, dictated by strong family affection. Among the merits and happiness of Elinor and Marianne, it cannot be ranked as the least considerable that, though sisters living almost within sight of one another, they managed to coexist without disagreement, thereby preventing any cooling of relations between their respective husbands. THE END
